================================================================================
  ABOUT THIS DOCUMENT
================================================================================
  This is the augmented CAPA learning guide — a single source of truth for
  content, structure, visual language and quality bar across the whole Argo
  project family (Argo CD, Argo Workflows, Argo Rollouts, Argo Events). It
  takes a complete novice from zero -> CAPA-certified -> confident GitOps
  practitioner, without ever needing to leave this document.

  MISSION — every Argo concept is taught once, in depth, and demonstrated on
  ONE real application (easypj/jobpost) so knowledge compounds instead of
  resetting each chapter. Each topic carries a "CAPA lens" (what's tested)
  AND a "Daily-Work lens" (real GitOps operations).

  INTENDED AUDIENCE
  -----------------
    • Primary ... complete novices to Argo preparing for the CAPA exam (no
                  prior Argo assumed; basic Kubernetes is refreshed in Ch 2).
    • Secondary . practitioners wanting one deep, exam-AND-production
                  reference for day-to-day GitOps / CI-CD / progressive-
                  delivery work.

  SCOPE
  -----
    IN  ... 100% of the official CAPA exam domains (Argo Workflows 36%, Argo
            CD 34%, Argo Rollouts 18%, Argo Events 12%), PLUS production
            "Beyond CAPA" depth (HA, hardening, observability, DR).
    OUT ... plain Kubernetes administration (CKA) and application-developer
            patterns (CKAD) are referenced only where they intersect Argo —
            they are not the focus.

  CONVENTIONS
  -----------
    Difficulty, exam-relevance and content types use a fixed symbol system —
    see SECTION 1 (Symbol & Icon Legend) and SECTION 2 (Visual Component
    Catalog). Reading guidance is in SECTION 0. The running project is
    SECTION 5; the exam audit is SECTION 6; the roadmap is SECTION 7; the
    master table of contents closes the front matter.


================================================================================
  SECTION 0 — HOW TO READ THIS GUIDE  (the design philosophy)
================================================================================

    THE 5 PROMISES OF THIS GUIDE
    ────────────────────────────
    ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
    │1. ZERO-ASSUMPTION       Every term is defined the first time it        │
    │                         appears. No "you should already know Argo."    │
    │2. SHOW, DON'T TELL      Never 3 paragraphs in a row without a          │
    │                         diagram, table, card, or code block.           │
    │3. ONE REAL PROJECT      Every concept is demonstrated on easypj/       │
    │                         jobpost — the SAME app, start to finish.       │
    │4. CAPA + DAILY WORK     Each topic has a "CAPA lens" (what's           │
    │                         tested) AND a "Daily-Work lens" (real use).    │
    │5. SELF-CONTAINED        CLI + YAML + internals included so you         │
    │                         never need to leave this document.             │
    └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    THE LEARNER'S LADDER  (how difficulty is signposted)
    ────────────────────────────────────────────────────
       🟢 BEGINNER      Concepts, vocabulary, "what & why"
       🟡 INTERMEDIATE  Hands-on, YAML, CLI, "how"
       🔴 ADVANCED      Internals, production, integration, "what if it breaks"
       🎯 CAPA TOPIC    Directly graded on the CAPA exam
       🏢 DAILY WORK    Real-world GitOps administration the exam doesn't test
       🧪 LAB           A hands-on, timed, exam-style task

    HOW TO USE THIS GUIDE
    ─────────────────────
      • Each chapter builds on the previous one — read in order the first pass.
      • Every concept includes easypj/jobpost real examples.
      • 🟢 = Beginner | 🟡 = Intermediate | 🔴 = Advanced
      • 🎯 = CAPA Exam Topic (directly tested) — drill these hardest.
      • Do the 🧪 Practice Drill + Lab Questions at the end of every chapter;
        hands-on > reading.
      • Returning practitioner? Jump to a Part by its TOC anchor; each
        chapter is self-contained behind its SECTION MAP diagram.

    PREREQUISITES & YOUR TOOLKIT  (what you need before Chapter 1)
    ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
    │☸️ Basic Kubernetes knowledge (pods, deployments, services, YAML) —     │
    │   refreshed in Chapter 2, not assumed in depth.                        │
    │🐙 Familiarity with terminal / command line                              │
    │🌱 Git basics (commit, push, pull, branches)                             │
    │💻 A Kubernetes cluster (minikube, kind, or k3s) with 4 GB+ RAM          │
    │🐍 Any programming literacy (examples use Python/Django — easypj)        │
    │⏱️ Budget ~55 hours; Chapter 23.5 has a 4-week study plan.              │
    │🐍 The example app lives in Dem/easypj (Django, Python).                 │
    └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

================================================================================
  SECTION 2 — THE VISUAL COMPONENT CATALOG  (the art direction)
================================================================================
    These are the building blocks used to render content. The rule:
    "NEVER a wall of text." Every subsection must contain >=1 of these.
    Each entry = [WHAT it is] + [WHEN to use it].

    2.1  CONCEPT CARD GRID          — 2-4 cards, icon + title + 1-line idea.
         WHEN: introduce a set of sibling concepts (e.g. the 4 Argo tools).
         [ 📦 Argo CD ][ 🔃 Workflows ][ 🚀 Rollouts ][ 🔔 Events ]

    2.2  ANATOMY CALLOUT            — label every field of a YAML/command.
         WHEN: first time a manifest or command is shown.
         argocd  app   sync   easypj-dev
         └cmd──┘ └obj┘ └verb┘ └──name───┘

    2.3  COMPARISON TABLE          — side-by-side feature matrix.
         WHEN: "X vs Y" decisions (Blue-Green vs Canary, Kustomize vs Helm).

    2.4  DECISION TREE 🧭          — branching "which one do I use?" guide.
         WHEN: choosing among options (DAG vs Steps, Sync vs Async EventSource).

    2.5  SEQUENCE / FLOW DIAGRAM   — numbered steps with arrows (left->right
         or top->down). WHEN: a process over time (sync lifecycle, canary steps).

    2.6  ARCHITECTURE DIAGRAM      — boxed components + connections + legend.
         WHEN: showing how parts of the system relate spatially (Argo CD's
         server/repo-server/controller triangle).

    2.7  MENTAL-MODEL / ANALOGY 🧠 — relate Argo to everyday life.
         WHEN: a concept is abstract (GitOps reconcile loop = a thermostat).

    2.8  COMMAND CHEAT-CARD 🔑     — grouped, copy-paste commands with notes.
         WHEN: end of every chapter ("most-used commands to remember").

    2.9  YAML SKELETON (annotated) — minimal manifest, every line commented.
         WHEN: teaching the shape of a CRD; also used inside lab answers.

    2.10 UNDER THE HOOD 🔧         — deep-dive (internals, reconcile loops,
         controller watch/queue, NATS EventBus internals). WHEN: once per chapter.

    2.11 GOTCHA BOX ⚠️            — the trap + why it happens + the fix.
         WHEN: a place learners reliably fail (wrong namespace, missing sync
         wave, forgotten activeService).

    2.12 SPEED TIP ⚡             — exam time-saver (CLI shortcuts, aliases).
         WHEN: an imperative shortcut beats writing YAML.

    2.13 DAILY-WORK OPS 🏢        — "in a real job you would..." scenario.
         WHEN: connecting a tested skill to actual GitOps administration.

    2.14 TROUBLESHOOTING FLOW 🩺  — symptom -> checks -> cause -> fix tree.
         WHEN: any failure mode (OutOfSync, Degraded, AnalysisRun stuck).

    2.15 BEFORE / AFTER SPLIT     — "without Argo" vs "with Argo" two-panel.
         WHEN: motivating why a feature exists (manual deploy vs GitOps).

    2.16 LIFECYCLE PILLS          — state badges joined by arrows
         (OutOfSync -> Syncing -> Synced). WHEN: object states.

    2.17 STAT / PROGRESS BARS     — weights, adoption %, exam-domain %.
         WHEN: quantitative emphasis (CAPA domain weights).

    2.18 RELATIONSHIP / MIND MAP  — central node with labeled spokes.
         WHEN: "how everything connects" (Events -> Workflows -> CD -> Rollouts).

    2.19 KEY TAKEAWAYS BANNER     — ✅ bullet recap + 🎯 exam relevance +
         🔑 commands. WHEN: end of every chapter AND every part.

    2.20 VISUAL SUMMARY           — one big picture that re-tells the whole
         chapter/part in a single diagram. WHEN: closing every chapter/part.
         (RULE: every diagram-only block also carries a short prose summary —
         we never give a diagram without its text recap.)

    2.21 PRACTICE DRILL 🧪        — short timed checklist task (do-it-now).
         WHEN: mid/after a chapter, before the formal lab questions.

    2.22 LAB Q&A CARD 🧪          — exam-style question + Answer + Explanation
         (with YAML/diagram). WHEN: 10 per chapter, end.

    2.23 EXAM-TOPIC BADGE 🎯      — inline tag marking graded competencies.

    2.24 KEY TERM / GLOSSARY POP 📖 — bold term + tight definition box.


================================================================================
  SECTION 3 — THE PER-CHAPTER SCAFFOLD  (fixed anatomy of EVERY chapter)
================================================================================
    Every chapter is assembled from these blocks IN THIS ORDER. Blocks [A]-[E]
    and [F]'s numbered sections are the LOCKED existing content (titles never
    renamed — they anchor the sidemenu of argo_1.html); blocks [G]-[N] are the
    augmentation added in this pass.

      [A] CHAPTER HEADER      Title + difficulty meta (🟢/🟡/🔴, ⏱️ est time,
                              🎯 CAPA domain %, tags) — the META line.
      [B] WHY THIS MATTERS    1-paragraph hook framed on easypj + a Before/
                              After or analogy visual.
      [C] LEARNING OBJECTIVES "🎯 What you'll be able to DO" — action bullets.
      [D] CAPA RELEVANCE      Which exact official CAPA competencies this
                              chapter maps to.
      [E] SECTION-MAP DIAGRAM A single diagram showing how all sections of
                              THIS chapter relate (the "you are here" map).
      [F] CONCEPT SECTIONS    The numbered sections (x.1, x.2, ...) — EXISTING,
                              titles locked. Each one gains, where useful:
                                · 📖 key terms · 🧠 analogy
                                · ⚠️ gotcha · ⚡ speed tip · 🏢 Daily-Work note
      [G] UNDER THE HOOD 🔧 / DEEPER   One internals deep-dive for the chapter.
      [H] KEY TAKEAWAYS 🔑   ✅ recap + 🎯 exam relevance.
      [I] EXAM GOTCHAS ⚠️    The top traps for this chapter, listed.
      [J] SPEED TIPS ⚡      Imperative shortcuts / CLI patterns.
      [K] COMMANDS TO REMEMBER 🔑  Cheat-card of must-know commands.
      [L] PRACTICE DRILL 🧪  A short, timed do-it-now checklist.
      [M] LAB QUESTIONS 🧪   10 exam-style Q&A. EACH has: Question, Answer
                              (commands/steps), Explanation (the WHY) + at
                              least one YAML/diagram/table where relevant.
      [N] VISUAL SUMMARY      One closing big-picture diagram + prose recap.


================================================================================
  SECTION 4 — THE PER-PART SCAFFOLD  (fixed anatomy of EVERY part)
================================================================================
      [A] PART COVER          Title, difficulty band, CAPA-domain weight,
                              chapter range, "what you'll build" line — EXISTING.
      [B] PART INTRO          What this part is, why it exists, what it
                              unlocks — framed on the easypj journey.
      [C] "HOW THE CHAPTERS FIT" DIAGRAM   A map showing each chapter in the
                              part and the dependency/flow between them.
      [D] SECTION SUMMARY + IMPLEMENTATION  (placed BEFORE the chapters) — a
                              concise summary of the whole part and HOW it is
                              implemented end-to-end on easypj.
      [E] CHAPTERS            (each following the Section 3 scaffold) — EXISTING.
      [F] PART KEY TAKEAWAYS  ✅ + 🎯 + 🔑 across the whole part.
      [G] PART VISUAL SUMMARY One diagram tying the whole part together.

  SECTION 5 — THE RUNNING PROJECT: easypj / jobpost
================================================================================
    Everything in this guide is taught on ONE real application so concepts
    compound instead of resetting each chapter.

    5.1  WHAT IT IS & THE DATA MODEL
         📖 easypj  = the Django PROJECT (the deployable unit / repo root).
         📖 jobpost = the Django APP inside it (a job-board: post & browse
                      jobs, skills, employers, locations).

    ╔══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╗
    ║         🚀 YOUR HANDS-ON PROJECT: easypj / jobpost           ║
    ╚══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╝

    ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
    │  easypj is a Django-based Job Board Application that you     │
    │  will containerize, GitOps-deploy, workflow-automate,        │
    │  canary-release, and event-trigger throughout this guide.    │
    │                                                              │
    │  PROJECT STRUCTURE:                                          │
    │  easypj/                         ← Django Project Root      │
    │  ├── manage.py                   ← Django CLI                │
    │  ├── .env                        ← DATABASE_URL (psql)       │
    │  ├── requirements.txt            ← Python dependencies       │
    │  ├── Dockerfile                  ← Container definition      │
    │  ├── .dockerignore               ← Build exclusions          │
    │  ├── easypj/                     ← Project settings package  │
    │  │   ├── __init__.py                                         │
    │  │   ├── settings.py             ← Django configuration      │
    │  │   ├── urls.py                 ← URL routing               │
    │  │   └── wsgi.py                 ← WSGI entry point          │
    │  └── jobpost/                    ← Job Post Application      │
    │      ├── __init__.py                                         │
    │      ├── models.py               ← DATA MODELS (5)           │
    │      ├── views.py                ← Request handlers          │
    │      ├── urls.py                 ← App URL routing           │
    │      └── admin.py                ← Admin interface           │
    │                                                              │
    │  DATA MODELS (what we're deploying):                         │
    │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
    │  │ MODEL       │ FIELDS                    │ DESCRIPTION   │ │
    │  ├─────────────┼───────────────────────────┼───────────────┤ │
    │  │ Skills      │ name, description         │ Job skills tag │ │
    │  │ User        │ username, email, password │ Job seeker    │ │
    │  │ Author      │ name, bio, avatar         │ Job poster    │ │
    │  │ Location    │ city, state, country, zip │ Job location  │ │
    │  │ JobPost     │ title, description,       │ THE MAIN      │ │
    │  │             │ skills (M2M→Skills),      │ JOB LISTING   │ │
    │  │             │ author (FK→Author),       │ with filters  │ │
    │  │             │ location (FK→Location),   │ by skill,     │ │
    │  │             │ created_at, updated_at,   │ author, &     │ │
    │  │             │ is_active                 │ location      │ │
    │  └─────────────┴───────────────────────────┴───────────────┘ │
    │                                                              │
    │  HOW EACH ARGO TOOL IS USED WITH easypj:                     │
    │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
    │  │ ARGO TOOL      │ easypj USE CASE                       │ │
    │  ├────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────┤ │
    │  │ Argo CD        │ Deploy easypj to dev/staging/prod    │ │
    │  │                │ via GitOps. Git = source of truth.    │ │
    │  ├────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────┤ │
    │  │ Argo Workflows │ CI pipeline: test→build→push image   │ │
    │  │                │ DB migration: backup→migrate→verify  │ │
    │  │                │ Nightly backup: CronWorkflow          │ │
    │  ├────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────┤ │
    │  │ Argo Rollouts  │ Canary deploy new jobpost search      │ │
    │  │                │ feature: 10%→25%→50%→100%            │ │
    │  │                │ Auto-rollback if error rate > 5%      │ │
    │  ├────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────┤ │
    │  │ Argo Events    │ GitHub push → auto-deploy to dev      │ │
    │  │                │ S3 CSV upload → import jobs to DB     │ │
    │  │                │ New JobPost → validate + notify       │ │
    │  └────────────────┴───────────────────────────────────────┘ │
    │                                                              │
    │  DOCKER IMAGE: registry.example.com/easypj:v1.0             │
    │  GITOPS REPO:  github.com/team-easypj/easypj-k8s            │
    │  NAMESPACES:   easypj-dev, easypj-staging, easypj-prod      │
    └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘


    5.2  THE CONTAINERIZATION + GITOPS STORY  (the through-line)
         🐳 Dev:    manage.py runserver + SQLite (db.sqlite3) locally — what
                    a fresh Django checkout ships with, zero setup.
         🐳 Build:  multi-stage Dockerfile -> small gunicorn image easypj:vN
                    (Chapter 2.6).
         ☸️ Deploy: Pods (via Argo CD Application) + Service + Ingress, config
                    via ConfigMap/Secret, data via PostgreSQL (Kubernetes-
                    native, not a local file).
         📖 Why swap SQLite -> PostgreSQL? SQLite is a single file on local
            disk — it cannot be shared by multiple replicas across nodes, and
            it cannot survive a Pod being rescheduled. Argo scales and
            reschedules easypj constantly (canary Pods, rollbacks, multi-
            replica Deployments), so the database must be a networked,
            concurrent server: PostgreSQL (🐘), reached via a DATABASE_URL
            environment variable injected from a Kubernetes Secret. This swap
            is the single most important "why" behind the ConfigMap/Secret
            patterns used throughout Chapters 4-8. See Appendix F.2 for the
            full PostgreSQL-on-Kubernetes manifest set.
         🔀 Why GitOps on top of that? Even with a proper Pod+Service+DB
            setup, someone still has to run `kubectl apply` correctly, in
            order, on the right cluster, every time. GitOps (Argo CD) removes
            the human from that loop: Git holds the desired state, Argo CD
            continuously reconciles the cluster to match it — see Chapter 1
            for the full philosophy.

    5.3  TARGET ARCHITECTURE  (what we build by the end of Part 6)
    ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
    │🌍 Ingress  (easypj.example.com)                                       │
    │              │                                                       │
    │       🔌 Service: easypj (ClusterIP)                                  │
    │        ┌─────┼───────────────┐                                       │
    │     📦 web-0  📦 web-1  📦 web-2   (Rollout, canary, 📈 metrics)         │
    │        │  envFrom ⚙️ ConfigMap + 🔒 Secret (DATABASE_URL)             │
    │        ▼                                                             │
    │       🔌 Service: easypj-db                                           │
    │        │                                                             │
    │     🐘 PostgreSQL  (StatefulSet or managed) ── 💾 PVC                  │
    │                                                                      │
    │  Driven end-to-end by:                                               │
    │  📦 Argo CD Application  ◀── 📁 Git (team-easypj/easypj-k8s)           │
    │  🔃 Argo Workflows       ── CI: test→build→push→update Git            │
    │  🚀 Argo Rollouts        ── canary 10%→25%→50%→100%, auto-rollback    │
    │  🔔 Argo Events          ── GitHub push, S3 CSV upload triggers       │
    └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
         PROSE RECAP: Git is the single source of truth. A push to GitHub
         (🔔 Events) kicks off a CI pipeline (🔃 Workflows) that builds and
         pushes an image, then updates the GitOps repo. Argo CD (📦) notices
         the Git change and syncs it — but instead of an instant, all-at-once
         Deployment, it syncs a Rollout (🚀), which shifts traffic gradually
         and watches Prometheus metrics, auto-rolling-back on errors.

    5.4  WHERE THE PROJECT APPEARS  (mapping across all 7 Parts)
         P1 understand GitOps + prep the cluster · P2 deploy easypj with
         Argo CD · P3 automate its CI/DB-migration/ETL with Workflows ·
         P4 progressively deliver it with Rollouts · P5 trigger it
         automatically with Events · P6 wire ALL FOUR tools together in
         production · P7 prove mastery of all of the above (CAPA exam prep).


================================================================================
  SECTION 6 — CAPA EXAM AT A GLANCE  &  OFFICIAL COMPETENCY MAP
================================================================================

    6.1  THE FACTS
    ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
    │Duration: 90 minutes    Format: Multiple Choice ONLY — NO terminal│
    │~60 questions   Pass: ~75% (LF doesn't publish an official cut)   │
    │CLOSED BOOK — no notes, no docs, no external references allowed  │
    │1 free retake   Valid: 2 years   Cost: ~$250 USD (check current)  │
    │No prerequisites   12-month eligibility window from purchase      │
    │Proctoring: PSI, remote, webcam+mic, ID + 360° room scan, join    │
    │  ~30 min early                                                    │
    └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
    ⚠️ GOTCHA — this is a genuine correction: CAPA is NOT hands-on-CLI
       and NOT open-book (unlike some other Linux Foundation certs).
       Questions are scenario-based (read YAML/CLI OUTPUT shown as
       text, pick the correct diagnosis/fix) but you never type a
       command or open a browser tab during the exam itself. The
       terminal-aliases (6.6) and doc-bookmarks (6.7) below are STUDY
       tools that build recognition speed — not exam-day resources.

    6.2  DOMAIN WEIGHTS  (official)
    ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
    │  CAPA EXAM BREAKDOWN (Certified Argo Project Associate)     │
    │                                                              │
    │  ████████████████████████████████████░░░░  36%  Argo        │
    │                                                Workflows    │
    │  • Understand Argo Workflow Fundamentals                    │
    │  • Generating and Consuming Artifacts                       │
    │  • Understand Argo Workflow Templates                       │
    │  • Understand the Argo Workflow Spec                        │
    │  • Work with DAG (Directed-Acyclic Graphs)                  │
    │  • Run Data Processing Jobs with Argo Workflows             │
    │                                                              │

    10 lab questions per chapter). The "Beyond CAPA" depth in Chapters 22.5-

    exceeds the syllabus so you never need external docs.

    6.5  CAPA vs CKA/CKAD — WHAT'S THE DIFFERENCE?
    ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
    │              CKA              CKAD              CAPA         │
    │  Focus:      Administrator    Developer         Argo/GitOps  │
    │  You manage: The CLUSTER      APPS on cluster   DELIVERY     │
    │  Topics:     Install, upgrade Deploy, config,   ArgoCD,      │
    │              backup etcd,     observability,    Workflows,   │
    │              troubleshoot     Helm, API ver     Rollouts,    │
    │              nodes/cluster                      Events       │
    │                                                              │
    │  Think of:   🏗️ Building     🎨 Decorating    🚚 Delivery   │
    │              the house        the rooms         pipeline     │
    │                                                              │
    │  THIS GUIDE = CAPA (we automate & deliver continuously)      │
    └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    6.6  CAPA EXAM DAY SETUP — YOUR TERMINAL ALIASES
    ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
    │  Run these FIRST when your exam terminal opens:              │
    │                                                              │
    │  # Argo CD shortcuts                                         │
    │  alias acd='argocd'                                          │
    │  alias acda='argocd app'                                     │
    │  alias acdap='argocd app list'                               │
    │  alias acdag='argocd app get'                                │
    │  alias acdas='argocd app sync'                               │
    │  alias acdad='argocd app diff'                               │
    │  alias acdh='argocd app history'                             │
    │                                                              │
    │  # Argo Workflows shortcuts                                  │
    │  alias aw='argo'                                             │
    │  alias awl='argo list'                                       │
    │  alias aws='argo submit'                                     │
    │  alias awg='argo get'                                        │
    │  alias awlog='argo logs'                                     │
    │  alias awt='argo template'                                   │
    │                                                              │
    │  # Argo Rollouts shortcuts                                   │
    │  alias kar='kubectl argo rollouts'                           │
    │  alias karg='kubectl argo rollouts get rollout'              │
    │  alias karp='kubectl argo rollouts promote'                  │
    │                                                              │
    │  # General kubectl (from CKA reference)                      │
    │  alias k='kubectl'                                           │
    │  alias kgp='kubectl get pods'                                │
    │  alias kd='kubectl describe'                                 │
    │  alias kaf='kubectl apply -f'                                │
    │  export do="--dry-run=client -o yaml"                        │
    │                                                              │
    │  # Quick Argo manifest generation                            │
    │  # Create Application from CLI:                              │
    │  argocd app create easypj \                                 │
    │    --repo https://github.com/me/easypj-k8s \                │
    │    --path k8s/overlays/dev \                                 │
    │    --dest-server https://kubernetes.default.svc \            │
    │    --dest-namespace easypj-dev \                             │
    │    --sync-policy automated \                                 │
    │    --auto-prune                                              │
    │                                                              │
    │  ⏱️ These aliases save 10-15 minutes during the exam!       │
    └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    6.8  CAPA EXAM STRATEGY — TOP 10 MISTAKES TO AVOID
    ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
    │  ❌ COMMON MISTAKES                 ✅ WHAT TO DO INSTEAD    │
    │                                                              │
    │  1. Forgetting to check Argo CD    ALWAYS verify: argocd     │
    │     context/session first          context (which cluster)   │
    │                                                              │
    │  2. Writing YAML from scratch      Use argocd app create     │
    │     for Application manifests      --dry-run or kubectl      │
    │                                     create --dry-run -o yaml │
    │                                                              │
    │  3. Not verifying sync status      ALWAYS: argocd app get    │
    │     after applying changes         <app> to confirm SYNCED   │
    │                                                              │
    │  4. Confusing Workflow and         WorkflowTemplate = reuse, │
    │     WorkflowTemplate purposes      Workflow = one-time run   │
    │                                                              │
    │  5. Ignoring sync waves causing    Use wave annotations:     │
    │     dependency deployment failures argocd.argoproj.io/sync-  │
    │                                     wave: "0" (or -1, 1...)  │
    │                                                              │
    │  6. Not setting resource limits    Always set limits on      │
    │     on workflow pods (OOM kills)   Workflow/WorkflowTemplate │
    │                                                              │
    │  7. Using Rollout without          Rollout needs activeService│
    │     activeService & previewService & previewService defined  │
    │                                                              │
    │  8. EventSource webhook not        Check: kubectl get events │
    │     receiving — no EventBus        -n argo-events, verify    │
    │                                     EventBus pod is running   │
    │                                                              │
    │  9. Not bookmarking Argo docs      Before exam: bookmark     │
    │     pages before the exam          argoproj.github.io docs   │
    │                                                              │
    │  10. Skipping hands-on labs        killer.sh + own cluster   │
    │      for memorization only         practice > memorization   │
    └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘


================================================================================
  SECTION 7 — THE LEARNING ROADMAP
================================================================================

    7.1  WHAT IS ARGO? — THE BIG PICTURE
    ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
    │  Argo is a collection of open-source Kubernetes-native       │
    │  tools for running jobs, managing deployments, and           │
    │  implementing GitOps. All four projects live under the       │
    │  Argo umbrella and are CNCF graduated/incubating projects.   │
    │                                                              │
    │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │
    │  │                   ☸️ KUBERNETES                       │    │
    │  │  ┌──────────┐  ┌──────────┐  ┌──────────┐  ┌──────┐│    │
    │  │  │🔄 Argo   │  │📦 Argo   │  │🚀 Argo   │  │🔔 Argo││    │
    │  │  │Workflows │  │   CD     │  │Rollouts  │  │Events ││    │
    │  │  │          │  │          │  │          │  │      ││    │
    │  │  │Run Jobs  │  │GitOps    │  │Progressive│  │Event ││    │
    │  │  │& Pipelines│ │Deploy   │  │Delivery  │  │Driven││    │
    │  │  └──────────┘  └──────────┘  └──────────┘  └──────┘│    │
    │  │                                                      │    │
    │  │  ALL ARE KUBERNETES CRDs — installed via kubectl     │    │
    │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘    │
    └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    7.2  THE ARGO ECOSYSTEM  (how the four tools relate)
    ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
    │                                                              │
    │   🔔 Argo Events ──────▶ 🔄 Argo Workflows                  │
    │   (triggers)              (executes pipelines/jobs)          │
    │        │                       │                             │
    │        │                       ▼                             │
    │        │               🏗️ CI builds image                    │
    │        │                       │                             │
    │        │                       ▼                             │
    │        └──────────────▶ 📦 Argo CD                          │
    │                        (deploys via GitOps)                  │
    │                                │                             │
    │                                ▼                             │
    │                        🚀 Argo Rollouts                      │
    │                        (progressive delivery)                │
    │                                                              │
    └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    7.3  VISUAL ROADMAP  (7-Part journey from zero to CAPA certified)
    ┌──────────┐    ┌──────────┐    ┌──────────┐    ┌──────────┐
    │  Part 1  │───▶│  Part 2  │───▶│  Part 3  │───▶│  Part 4  │
    │Foundation│    │Argo CD   │    │Workflows │    │Rollouts  │
    │  🟢 Ch1-3│    │🟢🟡 Ch4-8│   │🟡 Ch9-13 │    │🟡 Ch14-16│
    └──────────┘    └──────────┘    └──────────┘    └──────────┘
         │                                                │
         ▼                                                ▼
    ┌──────────┐    ┌──────────┐    ┌──────────┐
    │  Part 5  │───▶│  Part 6  │───▶│  Part 7  │
    │Events    │    │easypj    │    │CAPA Prep │
    │🟡 Ch17-19│    │🔴 Ch20-22│    │🔴 Ch23-25│
    └──────────┘    └──────────┘    └──────────┘

         PROSE RECAP — The journey is three arcs. Arc 1 (Part 1, 🟢) builds
         GitOps vocabulary and readies your cluster. Arc 2 (Parts 2-5, 🟡) is
         the graded CAPA core: deploy easypj with Argo CD, automate it with
         Workflows, progressively release it with Rollouts, and trigger it
         with Events — each Part ending in a hands-on Practice Drill. Arc 3
         (Parts 6-7, 🔴) is production + mastery: wire all four tools
         together end-to-end, harden it, then a dedicated exam-strategy
         chapter with practice questions and a full CAPA simulation lab.

         BUILD ORDER NOTE: chapters are numbered 1..25, grouped into 7
         Parts; appendices A..J are reference material. Parts 1-5 plus
         Chapter 21 (Integration Patterns) cover the graded CAPA domains;
         Part 6 is "and beyond" production depth (Ch 22.5-22.12 extend
         past the syllabus); Part 7 is exam mastery.

    7.4  REAL-WORLD ARGO ADOPTERS — WHO USES ARGO?
    ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
    │  COMPANY ADOPTION — Argo in Production                       │
    │                                                              │
    │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
    │  │ 🏦 Intuit      │ Creator of Argo │ 2000+ apps via CD │   │
    │  │ 🏦 Capital One │ Argo CD + WF    │ 1000+ workflows   │   │
    │  │ 🎮 NVIDIA      │ Argo Workflows  │ ML training pipes  │   │
    │  │ 🛒 Adobe       │ Argo CD         │ 500+ microservices │   │
    │  │ 🎬 Netflix     │ Argo Rollouts   │ Canary deployments │   │
    │  │ 🔍 Google      │ Argo CD + WF    │ Internal platforms │   │
    │  │ 💼 Goldman S.  │ Argo Events     │ Event-driven trades│   │
    │  │ 🏥 CERN        │ Argo Workflows  │ Physics simulations│   │
    │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
    └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    7.5  ARGO HISTORY TIMELINE (How We Got Here)
    ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
    │ARGO PROJECT EVOLUTION  (fact-checked against cncf.io + argoproj blog)  │
    │                                                                        │
    │2017 ──── Aug/Oct: Jesse Suen's team at Applatix (independent           │
    │  │       startup, NOT yet Intuit) launches Argo Workflows on GitHub    │
    │  │                                                                     │
    │2018 ──── Jan: Intuit ACQUIRES Applatix — Argo becomes Intuit-backed    │
    │  │       Aug 30: Argo CD publicly announced/open-sourced               │
    │  │       BlackRock contributes Argo Events (was internal "axis")       │
    │  │       Argo Rollouts development begins at Intuit (payments team)    │
    │  │                                                                     │
    │2019 ──── All four sub-projects (CD/Workflows/Rollouts/Events)          │
    │  │       actively developed under the shared Argo umbrella             │
    │  │                                                                     │
    │2020 ──── 04-07: Argo (ALL FOUR projects, as ONE unified project)       │
    │  │       accepted directly into CNCF Incubating — skipped Sandbox      │
    │  │                                                                     │
    │2021 ──── Continued incubation growth across all four sub-projects      │
    │  │       (no separate per-project maturity levels — see 📖 below)       │
    │  │                                                                     │
    │2022 ──── 12-06: Argo GRADUATES — ALL FOUR sub-projects graduate        │
    │  │       TOGETHER as one unified CNCF project 🎓                        │
    │  │       CAPA Certification announced                                  │
    │  │                                                                     │
    │2023 ──── Argo CD v2.7+ (multi-source, plugin 2.0)                      │
    │  │       Argo Workflows v3.5+ (template refs, memoization)             │
    │  │       Flux CD (Argo's closest GitOps peer) graduates ~1yr later     │
    │  │                                                                     │
    │2024 ──── All projects mature, CAPA goes live                           │
    │  │       Over 50,000+ GitHub stars across all projects                 │
    │                                                                        │
    │📖 KEY TERM — "Argo" is registered with the CNCF as ONE project          │
    │   spanning all four tools. There is no such thing as "Argo CD is       │
    │   Graduated but Argo Events is Incubating" — they share a SINGLE       │
    │   maturity level (currently: Graduated, since 2022-12-06).             │
    └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

PART 1: FOUNDATION — GITOPS, ARGO & THE BIG PICTURE                     🟢
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
    🎯 EASYPJ FOCUS: Understand the GitOps philosophy and get your
       local cluster ready to deploy the easypj/jobpost Django app.

Chapter 1: What is GitOps? (The Philosophy Behind Argo)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
    🟢 BEGINNER · ⏱️ ~1.5 hours · 🎯 CAPA domain: Foundation (prerequisite for all 4 domains) · 🏢 Daily-Work: high

    [B] WHY THIS MATTERS
    ────────────────────
    Every single chapter after this one assumes you already believe
    GitOps is worth the trouble — that Git-as-source-of-truth and
    pull-based reconciliation are better defaults than SSHing into a
    box or running a deploy script by hand. If that belief isn't solid,
    the next 24 chapters read as "here's more YAML to memorize" instead
    of "here's how to eliminate the exact 2 AM easypj incident this
    chapter opens with." This chapter builds that belief from a real
    failure mode, not an abstract definition — then names the 4 tools
    (Argo CD, Workflows, Rollouts, Events) that make it real.

    ┌─────────────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────────┐
    │  BEFORE (manual/push CI/CD) │  AFTER (GitOps / Argo)            │
    ├─────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────┤
    │  "I ran the deploy script,  │  Git commit IS the deploy — the   │
    │  I think it worked"         │  cluster continuously pulls and   │
    │                              │  reconciles to match it            │
    │  Cluster credentials live   │  Credentials never leave the      │
    │  in the CI system           │  cluster — the controller pulls,  │
    │                              │  nothing pushes to it              │
    │  Rollback = redeploy an old │  Rollback = git revert — the      │
    │  build and hope             │  commit itself IS the state        │
    │  Drift = invisible until    │  Drift = flagged OutOfSync         │
    │  something breaks            │  continuously, auto-corrected      │
    │                              │  if selfHeal: true                 │
    └─────────────────────────────┴──────────────────────────────────┘

    [C] LEARNING OBJECTIVES — 🎯 What you'll be able to DO
    ────────────────────────────────────────────────────
      - Explain configuration drift concretely and why it's invisible
        by default without a reconciling controller watching for it.
      - Recite OpenGitOps' 4 principles (Declarative, Versioned &
        Immutable, Pulled Automatically, Continuously Reconciled) and
        explain what breaks if any single one is dropped.
      - Draw push vs. pull deployment models and state the concrete
        security/rollback/drift advantages pull has over push.
      - Correctly place Argo CD, Workflows, Rollouts, and Events as ONE
        CNCF project with a shared maturity level, not four independent
        tools — and map each to a real easypj/jobpost scenario.
      - State Argo's real origin timeline (Applatix 2017 → Intuit
        acquisition 2018 → CNCF Incubating 2020 → Graduated 2022,
        unified across all four sub-projects) without the common
        per-tool-maturity misconception.

    [D] CAPA RELEVANCE
    ───────────────────
    This chapter has no single weighted competency of its own — per
    Appendix H's chapter-mapping table, Chapters 1-2 are Foundation:
    "prerequisite for all domains." That's not a lesser status: the 4
    OpenGitOps principles (1.2) and the CNCF single-maturity fact (1.4)
    are exactly the kind of crisp, memorizable, definitional content
    CAPA likes to test directly and early in the exam, and every later
    domain-specific competency (Argo CD 34%, Workflows 36%, Rollouts
    18%, Events 12%) silently assumes you already hold this vocabulary.

    PROSE RECAP: 1.1-1.3 build the CASE for GitOps from a real failure
    mode. 1.4-1.5 introduce Argo and immediately ground it in easypj.
    1.6 makes the comparison explicit and memorizable. 1.7 gives the
    vocabulary a real timeline so "Argo" stops being an abstract noun.
    Chapter 2 then refreshes the Kubernetes primitives every later
    chapter assumes you already have.

    1.1  The Problem Before GitOps
         - Traditional deployment: manual SSH + scripts + human error
         - Configuration drift: "It worked in staging, why not in prod?"
         - Audit nightmare: "Who changed what and when?"
         📖 KEY TERM — Configuration Drift: the gap that opens between a
            system's documented/intended configuration and what's actually
            running. It's caused by undocumented manual changes (a hotfix
            `kubectl edit`, a manual `kubectl scale`, a console click) that
            never get written back to source control. Drift is invisible
            by default — nothing is watching for it until something breaks.
         - The easypj pain point: deploying jobpost manually at 2 AM
         📖 KEY TERM — The term "GitOps" was coined in August 2017 by Alexis
            Richardson, CEO of Weaveworks, in a blog post titled "Operations
            by Pull Request." It wasn't an abstract idea first — it was a
            name for the exact practice of fixing pain like the 2 AM easypj
            scenario above.
         [PAIN POINT CARDS — "Without GitOps" scenario]
         ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │ 😰 Deploying easypj WITHOUT GitOps                 │
         │                                                    │
         │  • Developer: "I ran the deploy script"            │
         │  • Ops: "The script failed halfway, DB migrated    │
         │    but new code didn't deploy"                     │
         │  • Manager: "Which version is in production?"      │
         │  • Everyone: "Let's check the server... oh wait"   │
         │  • RESULT: 45 min downtime, unknown state          │
         └────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
         🧠 ANALOGY — "Pets vs. Cattle": A manually-managed server is a
            "pet" — it has a name, someone SSHes in to nurse it back to
            health, and over months it silently accumulates undocumented
            tweaks nobody remembers making. A GitOps-managed environment
            behaves like "cattle" — if a resource misbehaves, nobody
            debugs it in place; Git's desired state is just reconciled
            again. This mindset shift — not the tooling — is the real
            change GitOps forces on a team.

    1.2  GitOps Defined — The 4 Principles
         - Principle 1: Declarative — Everything described as code (YAML)
         - Principle 2: Versioned & Immutable — Git is the single source of truth
         - Principle 3: Pulled Automatically — Software agents automatically
           pull the desired state from the source; nothing pushes to them
         - Principle 4: Continuously Reconciled — Software agents ensure actual = desired
         [ICON GRID: 4 principle cards with emoji icons]
         ┌──────────────┐  ┌──────────────┐  ┌──────────────┐  ┌──────────────┐
         │ 📋 Declarative│  │ 📚 Versioned │  │ ⬇️ Pull Based │  │ 🔄 Reconciled│
         │ Everything    │  │ Git = single │  │ Agents pull   │  │ Actual state  │
         │ described     │  │ source of    │  │ desired state │  │ constantly    │
         │ as code/YAML  │  │ truth        │  │ (not push)    │  │ = desired     │
         └──────────────┘  └──────────────┘  └──────────────┘  └──────────────┘
         📖 KEY TERM — OpenGitOps: these 4 principles aren't just this
            guide's teaching summary — they are the formal definition from
            OpenGitOps, a CNCF Sandbox project (accepted 2021-01-26) whose
            job is to give "GitOps" a vendor-neutral, testable meaning. A
            tool can only be called GitOps-compliant if it satisfies all 4.
            That's why the CAPA exam treats this as an exact, memorizable
            list rather than a loose idea.
         🧠 WHY ALL 4, NOT FEWER — drop any single principle and the system
            stops qualifying as GitOps:
              • No Declarative      → nothing to diff; imperative scripts
                                       have no fixed "shape" to compare against
              • No Versioned/Immutable → no rollback, no audit trail — "who
                                       changed this and when" is unanswerable again
              • No Pulled Automatically → you're back to push-based CI/CD,
                                       with cluster credentials living outside the cluster
              • No Continuously Reconciled → drift silently creeps back in
                                       between deploys — you're only "correct" at deploy time
         ⚠️ GOTCHA — "Versioned & Immutable" does NOT mean the live
            Kubernetes objects can't be mutated (the reconciler mutates
            them constantly). It means the DESIRED-STATE DECLARATION in
            Git is immutable and versioned — every commit is a permanent,
            addressable snapshot you can diff or roll back to. Students
            who mix these two up misread this principle on the exam.

    1.3  Push vs. Pull Deployment Models
         [COMPARISON DIAGRAM: Push-based CI/CD vs Pull-based GitOps]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────┐  ┌──────────────────────────────────┐
         │  ❌ PUSH MODEL (Traditional)      │  │  ✅ PULL MODEL (GitOps / Argo)    │
         │                                    │  │                                    │
         │  CI Server ──push──▶ K8s Cluster  │  │  Developer ──push──▶ Git Repo    │
         │  (needs cluster credentials!)     │  │       ▲                          │
         │  Risk: exposed credentials        │  │       │ pull + reconcile          │
         │  No self-healing                  │  │  ┌────┴────────┐                 │
         │  Manual rollback                  │  │  │ Argo CD     │                 │
         │                                    │  │  │ (in cluster) │                 │
         └──────────────────────────────────┘  │  └─────────────┘                 │
                                                │  ✅ No external creds             │
                                                │  ✅ Auto-healing                  │
                                                │  ✅ Git revert = rollback         │
                                                └──────────────────────────────────┘
         🧠 ANALOGY — Push is like someone mailing a package to your house
            unannounced (you must be home, door unlocked, for it to be
            delivered). Pull is like checking your own PO box on your own
            schedule — no one needs a key to your house at all.
         📖 KEY TERM — Polling vs. Webhook: Argo CD's controller doesn't
            wait for anyone to tell it Git changed — by default it polls
            the repo on a timer (`timeout.reconciliation` in the
            argocd-cm ConfigMap, default 120s + up to 60s jitter, ~3 min
            max). A configured Git webhook skips the wait entirely and
            triggers an immediate refresh. Either way, the PULL is always
            initiated from inside the cluster — never by the developer or
            CI system.
         📖 KEY TERM — What "Pull + Reconcile" Actually Runs: the pull
            arrow in the diagram above isn't abstract. Per Argo CD's own
            Getting Started guide, running (or auto-triggering) a sync
            "retrieves the manifests from the repository and performs a
            `kubectl apply` of the manifests." That's the entire pull
            model in one sentence — the only difference from a human
            running `kubectl apply` from a laptop is WHO/WHAT triggers
            it: an in-cluster controller on a timer or webhook, not a
            person with cluster credentials.
         🏢 DAILY-WORK — The docs also note that "by default, Argo CD
            isn't exposed outside the cluster" for its OWN API/UI — the
            same pull-first, closed-by-default posture this section
            argues for easypj's deployment path. Chapter 3 covers
            exposing it deliberately (port-forward, LoadBalancer,
            Ingress) — it's an opt-in decision, not a default.

    1.4  What is Argo? — The CNCF Project Family
         - Argo CD: Declarative GitOps continuous delivery
         - Argo Workflows: Container-native workflow engine
         - Argo Rollouts: Progressive delivery (blue-green, canary)
         - Argo Events: Event-driven workflow automation
         📖 CNCF status (fact-checked against cncf.io, 2026): "Argo" is ONE
            CNCF project encompassing all four tools — they share a SINGLE
            maturity level, not four independent ones. Argo was accepted
            directly into the CNCF Incubator on 2020-04-07 (it never passed
            through Sandbox) and ALL FOUR sub-projects graduated TOGETHER on
            2022-12-06. There is no such thing as "Argo CD graduated while
            Workflows/Events/Rollouts stayed Incubating" — a common
            misconception this guide used to repeat; the CAPA exam expects
            you to know Argo graduated as a single unified project.
         [FAMILY TREE DIAGRAM: Argo umbrella with 4 sub-projects]
         ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │                    🐙 ARGO  (one CNCF project,              │
         │                     Graduated 2022-12-06)                  │
         │        ┌───────────────┬───────────────┬───────────────┐   │
         │        ▼               ▼               ▼               ▼   │
         │  📦 Argo CD      🔃 Argo Workflows  🚀 Argo Rollouts  🔔 Argo Events │
         │  GitOps CD       Workflow engine    Progressive       Event-driven  │
         │  (sync/reconcile) (Jobs, DAGs)       delivery          automation   │
         │                                     (canary/blue-green)            │
         │  Separate GitHub repos (argoproj/argo-cd, argo-workflows,          │
         │  argo-rollouts, argo-events) — independently installable —         │
         │  but ONE shared CNCF maturity level.                               │
         └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
         📖 KEY TERM — What the Official Docs Assume You Already Know:
            Argo CD's own "Understand The Basics" page opens with
            "Before effectively using Argo CD, it is necessary to
            understand the underlying technology that the platform is
            built on" — then names exactly 3 prerequisite buckets before
            any Argo-CD-specific content: (1) Docker + Kubernetes
            fundamentals, (2) an application-templating choice —
            Kustomize or Helm — for how easypj's manifests get built,
            and (3) a CI tool (its two named examples are GitHub Actions
            and Jenkins) for whatever builds/tests easypj's image BEFORE
            Argo CD ever touches it. Chapter 2 refreshes bucket (1);
            Chapter 4.2 covers bucket (2) for easypj; bucket (3) is
            exactly the "upstream CI" box in 1.5's GOTCHA below — Argo
            CD assumes it exists, but never does it itself.

    1.5  Why Argo for easypj/jobpost?
         - Auto-deploy Django app when code is pushed to GitHub
         - Run database migrations as Argo Workflows
         - Canary deploy new jobpost features to 10% of users
         - Trigger workflows on new job post events (Argo Events)
         [USE CASE MAP: easypj workflow mapped to Argo tools]
         ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  easypj/jobpost — ARGO TOOL MAPPING                        │
         │                                                            │
         │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
         │  │ SCENARIO                        │ ARGO TOOL          │ │
         │  ├─────────────────────────────────┼────────────────────┤ │
         │  │ "I pushed code to GitHub —     │ Argo Events →      │ │
         │  │  deploy it automatically!"     │ Argo Workflows →   │ │
         │  │                                 │ Argo CD            │ │
         │  ├─────────────────────────────────┼────────────────────┤ │
         │  │ "I need to run Django           │ Argo Workflows     │ │
         │  │  migrations before deploying"   │ (PreSync hook or   │ │
         │  │                                 │  WorkflowTemplate) │ │
         │  ├─────────────────────────────────┼────────────────────┤ │
         │  │ "Show the new jobpost search    │ Argo Rollouts      │ │
         │  │  to 10% of users first"         │ (Canary strategy)  │ │
         │  ├─────────────────────────────────┼────────────────────┤ │
         │  │ "Someone uploaded a CSV of      │ Argo Events →      │ │
         │  │  jobs to S3 — import it!"       │ Argo Workflows     │ │
         │  ├─────────────────────────────────┼────────────────────┤ │
         │  │ "Run DB backup every night      │ Argo Workflows     │ │
         │  │  at 2 AM"                       │ (CronWorkflow)     │ │
         │  ├─────────────────────────────────┼────────────────────┤ │
         │  │ "Which version is in prod       │ Argo CD            │ │
         │  │  right now?"                    │ (Git = source of   │ │
         │  │                                 │  truth)            │ │
         │  └─────────────────────────────────┴────────────────────┘ │
         │                                                            │
         │  COMPLETE FLOW for easypj:                                  │
         │  👨‍💻 git push → 🔔 Events trigger → 🔄 Workflow CI →       │
         │  🏗️ docker build easypj:v1.2 → 📦 push to registry →      │
         │  ✏️ update GitOps repo → 📦 Argo CD syncs →               │
         │  🚀 Rollout canary 10% → ✅ metrics OK → 100%             │
         └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
         ⚠️ GOTCHA — Argo CD alone cannot build easypj's Docker image or
            run its test suite — that's CI, not CD. Argo CD only handles
            the delivery side: syncing already-built, already-tagged
            manifests from Git into the cluster. Building/testing the
            image happens BEFORE the GitOps repo is even touched (via
            Argo Workflows or an external CI tool like GitHub Actions).
         🏢 DAILY-WORK — In production, teams usually split easypj into TWO
            repos: an "app repo" (Django source code, Dockerfile) and a
            separate "GitOps/config repo" (the K8s YAML Argo CD watches).
            CI builds an image from the app repo, then a separate step
            bumps the image tag in the config repo — that commit is what
            Argo CD actually reacts to.
         📖 KEY TERM — PreSync Hook: a Job-like resource tagged with the
            annotation `argocd.argoproj.io/hook: PreSync`. Argo CD runs it
            BEFORE syncing the rest of the manifests — exactly the pattern
            for running Django's `migrate` before new pods with the new
            code start up.

    1.6  GitOps vs. Traditional CI/CD — Comparison Table
         [COMPARISON TABLE: 6 aspects — credentials, rollback, drift detection, audit, etc.]
         ┌───────────────────────┬────────────────────────┬─────────────────────────┐
         │ Aspect                │ Traditional CI/CD       │ GitOps (Argo CD)         │
         ├───────────────────────┼────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────┤
         │ Cluster credentials   │ Live outside cluster,   │ Never leave the          │
         │                       │ stored in CI system     │ cluster (in-cluster      │
         │                       │                         │ controller only)         │
         │ Rollback              │ Re-run an old pipeline  │ `git revert` — the       │
         │                       │ job / redeploy manually │ commit IS the state      │
         │ Drift detection       │ None by default —       │ Continuous — flags       │
         │                       │ silent until it breaks  │ OutOfSync                │
         │ Audit trail           │ CI job logs (often      │ Full Git history —       │
         │                       │ rotate/expire)          │ permanent, signed        │
         │ Deploy trigger        │ Pipeline run / manual   │ Git commit merge         │
         │                       │ click                   │ (auto-detected)          │
         │ Self-healing          │ No — manual drift stays │ Yes, IF selfHeal:        │
         │                       │ until someone notices   │ true is configured       │
         └───────────────────────┴────────────────────────┴─────────────────────────┘

    1.7  The History and Origin of Argo
         - Aug/Oct 2017: Jesse Suen's team at Applatix (an independent K8s
           startup, NOT yet part of Intuit) launches Argo on GitHub, then
           re-implements the workflow engine as a Kubernetes CRD
         🧠 Argo's Aug/Oct 2017 launch happened the SAME YEAR Alexis
            Richardson coined the term "GitOps" (also Aug 2017, see 1.1) —
            Argo wasn't built to chase an existing trend; it emerged
            alongside the term itself.
         - Jan 2018: Intuit ACQUIRES Applatix — this is when Argo becomes an
           Intuit-backed project, one year AFTER it was first created
         - 2018: BlackRock's Data Science Platform team contributes Argo
           Events (originally an internal BlackRock project called "axis")
         - 2020-04-07: Argo (all four sub-projects together) accepted
           directly into the CNCF Incubator — it skipped Sandbox entirely
         - 2022-12-06: Argo GRADUATES — all four sub-projects graduate
           TOGETHER as one unified CNCF project, roughly a year before its
           closest GitOps competitor Flux CD graduates (Nov 2023)
         - Key versions and milestones
         [TIMELINE: 2017 (Applatix) → 2018 (Intuit acquisition + Events) →
          2020 (CNCF Incubating) → 2022 (Graduated) → 2024 (mature, CAPA live)]
         ⚠️ GOTCHA — There is no standalone tool called just "Argo" that
            you install. "Argo" is the umbrella CNCF project name; what
            you actually install are Argo CD, Argo Workflows, Argo
            Rollouts, or Argo Events individually, picking only the ones
            you need.

    1.8  Core Concepts — Argo CD's Own Vocabulary (Official Glossary Preview)
         Argo CD's own "Core Concepts" doc frames these terms this way:
         "Let's assume you're familiar with core Git, Docker, Kubernetes,
         Continuous Delivery, and GitOps concepts. Below are some of the
         concepts that are specific to Argo CD." In other words: 1.1-1.7
         taught you GitOps in general; this glossary is the Argo-CD-
         flavored vocabulary layered on top of it — memorize these 9
         terms now, before Chapter 4 uses all of them without re-defining
         them.
         ┌──────────────────────────┬─────────────────────────────────────┬──────────────────┐
         │ TERM                     │ OFFICIAL DEFINITION                  │ FULL DEPTH IN...  │
         ├──────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────┤
         │ Application               │ "A group of Kubernetes resources as │ 4.1 (the object)  │
         │                           │ defined by a manifest. This is a    │                    │
         │                           │ Custom Resource Definition (CRD)."  │                    │
         │ Application source type   │ "Which Tool is used to build the    │ 4.2 (Directory/    │
         │                           │ application" — Directory, Helm,     │ Helm/Kustomize/    │
         │                           │ Kustomize, or a Plugin.             │ Plugin)            │
         │ Target state              │ "The desired state of an            │ 8's KEY TERM,      │
         │                           │ application, as represented by      │ Target vs. Live    │
         │                           │ files in a Git repository."         │                    │
         │ Live state                │ "The live state of that             │ 8's KEY TERM       │
         │                           │ application. What pods etc are      │                    │
         │                           │ deployed."                          │                    │
         │ Sync status               │ "Whether or not the live state      │ 4.6 (Synced /      │
         │                           │ matches the target state. Is the    │ OutOfSync /        │
         │                           │ deployed application the same as    │ Unknown)           │
         │                           │ Git says it should be?"             │                    │
         │ Sync                      │ "The process of making an           │ [G] (this          │
         │                           │ application move to its target      │ chapter) + 4.4-4.5 │
         │                           │ state. E.g. by applying changes to  │ (waves/options)    │
         │                           │ a Kubernetes cluster."              │                    │
         │ Sync operation status     │ "Whether or not a sync succeeded."  │ 4.6                │
         │ Refresh                   │ "Compare the latest code in Git     │ [G] (this          │
         │                           │ with the live state. Figure out     │ chapter)'s          │
         │                           │ what is different."                 │ DIFFS step         │
         │ Health                    │ "The health of the application, is  │ 4.6 (6 Health      │
         │                           │ it running correctly? Can it serve  │ Status values)     │
         │                           │ requests?"                          │                    │
         │ Tool / Config mgmt tool   │ "A tool to create manifests from a  │ 4.2                │
         │                           │ directory of files. E.g. Kustomize."│                    │
         │ Config mgmt plugin (CMP)  │ "A custom tool" (the official docs  │ 4.2's KEY TERM     │
         │                           │ intentionally keep this one         │ (sidecar CMP)      │
         │                           │ minimal — this guide expands it     │                    │
         │                           │ in 4.2, don't over-read into the    │                    │
         │                           │ 3-word official gloss itself)       │                    │
         └──────────────────────────┴─────────────────────────────────────┴──────────────────┘
         🧠 CONNECT THE DOTS — this glossary is just 1.2's principles and
            [G]'s WATCH→DIFF→RECONCILE loop with formal Argo CD nouns
            attached: Target State = the "Declarative, Versioned" Git
            side (Principles 1-2); Live State = what's actually running;
            Refresh = the DIFFS step naming Target vs. Live; Sync = the
            RECONCILES step made concrete ("applying changes to a
            Kubernetes cluster" — literally `kubectl apply` under the
            hood, per 1.3's KEY TERM above); Sync Status / Health
            Status are the two axes 4.6 teaches you to read together.
         ⚠️ GOTCHA — "Application source type" and "Tool" are the SAME
            concept from two angles: the doc defines Application source
            type as simply "which Tool is used," then separately defines
            Tool/"Configuration management tool" as synonyms for the
            same thing (e.g., Kustomize). Don't treat them as two
            different fields to memorize — Chapter 4.2 is where the 4
            real values (Directory/Helm/Kustomize/Plugin) live.

    [G] UNDER THE HOOD 🔧 — Why "Pull, Not Push" Is the Load-Bearing Principle
    ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
    Of the 4 OpenGitOps principles (1.2), Principle 3 (Pulled
    Automatically) is the one every later chapter's architecture
    actually depends on mechanically, not just philosophically. Every
    controller you'll install in this guide — Argo CD's application-
    controller, the Workflow Controller, the Rollout controller, Argo
    Events' Sensor — follows the IDENTICAL shape:

        controller starts inside the cluster
              │
              v
        controller WATCHES its own input (Git repo state, a Workflow
        object, a Rollout object, an external event) — on a poll timer
        or a webhook, never by someone pushing INTO it
              │
              v
        controller DIFFS what it sees against what it last knew
              │
              v
        controller RECONCILES the live cluster toward the diff
              │
              v
        (repeat forever)

    This is WHY Argo CD never needs a Kubernetes credential outside the
    cluster (1.3's push-model risk simply doesn't exist for it), why
    `git revert` is a real rollback mechanism (the controller just
    reconciles toward the reverted state on its next pass), and why
    drift gets caught automatically instead of silently accumulating —
    the watch step never stops running. Every single Argo tool in
    Chapters 3-22 is a variation on this one loop; Chapter 21.2 names
    it explicitly as "the shared reconciliation model" once you've seen
    all four tools use it.

    PROSE RECAP: GitOps isn't 4 independent rules — Principles 1-2
    (Declarative, Versioned) describe WHAT the controller watches;
    Principle 3 (Pull) describes WHO initiates contact; Principle 4
    (Reconciled) describes the loop that never stops. Understand the
    loop and all 4 principles stop being a list to memorize.

    [H] KEY TAKEAWAYS 🔑
    ─────────────────────
    - Configuration drift is invisible by default — it takes an active,
      continuously-running reconciler to detect and correct it.
    - The 4 OpenGitOps principles are a formal, testable definition
      (CNCF Sandbox project, accepted 2021-01-26), not this guide's own
      paraphrase — drop any one and a system stops qualifying as GitOps.
    - "Versioned & Immutable" describes the Git DECLARATION, not the
      live Kubernetes objects (which the reconciler mutates constantly)
      — a common exam-trap mix-up.
    - Pull-based deployment means cluster credentials never leave the
      cluster — the controller initiates every contact, nothing pushes
      into it.
    - "Argo" is ONE CNCF project (Incubating 2020-04-07, Graduated
      2022-12-06) with FOUR sub-projects sharing a single maturity
      level — there is no per-tool graduation split.
    - Argo CD alone cannot build or test easypj's Docker image — that's
      CI's job; Argo CD only handles delivery from an already-built,
      already-tagged Git state.
    🎯 EXAM RELEVANCE: this chapter's definitional content (4
       principles, CNCF status, push-vs-pull tradeoffs) is exactly the
       kind of crisp, early-exam, high-confidence-if-you-know-it
       material CAPA tests directly.

    [I] EXAM GOTCHAS ⚠️
    ─────────────────────
    1. Confusing "Versioned & Immutable" (the Git declaration) with the
       live cluster objects being immutable — they aren't; the
       reconciler changes them constantly.
    2. Assuming "Argo CD graduated while Workflows/Rollouts/Events
       stayed Incubating" — false; all four graduated together as one
       unified project.
    3. Believing Argo CD builds/tests container images — it only
       delivers already-built, already-tagged manifests from Git.
    4. Thinking a Git webhook is REQUIRED for Argo CD to detect
       changes — it isn't; polling (`timeout.reconciliation`, default
       120s + up to 60s jitter) happens regardless, a webhook just
       skips the wait.
    5. Treating "Argo" as something you install directly — you install
       Argo CD, Workflows, Rollouts, or Events individually; there is
       no standalone "Argo" binary/manifest.

    [J] SPEED TIPS ⚡
    ───────────────────
    - When an exam question describes a scenario ("push to GitHub
      should auto-deploy," "test a feature on 10% of users first"),
      match it to 1.5's use-case map FIRST — the right tool is usually
      obvious once you name the scenario type, before reading any
      answer choices.
    - Memorize the 4 OpenGitOps principles in order — Declarative,
      Versioned & Immutable, Pulled Automatically, Continuously
      Reconciled — exam phrasing tends to quote them close to verbatim.

    [K] COMMANDS TO REMEMBER 🔑
    ──────────────────────────────
      # Chapter 1 is conceptual — no CLI yet. The first real commands
      # arrive in Chapter 2 (kubectl refresher) and Chapter 3 (the
      # first argocd install + login). Nothing to memorize here beyond
      # vocabulary.

    [L] PRACTICE DRILL 🧪 (~10 min, do-it-now)
    ─────────────────────────────────────────
      1. Write, from memory, all 4 OpenGitOps principles in order —
         then check against 1.2.
      2. Sketch push vs. pull as two boxes-and-arrows diagrams (no
         peeking at 1.3) — label which one needs cluster credentials
         living OUTSIDE the cluster.
      3. For each of these 4 easypj scenarios, name the right Argo
         tool without looking at 1.5's table: (a) auto-deploy on push,
         (b) canary a risky feature, (c) run a nightly DB backup,
         (d) react to a CSV landing in S3.
      4. State Argo's graduation status in one sentence, correctly.

    [M] LAB QUESTIONS 🧪 (10, exam-style)
    ───────────────────────────────────────
    Q1. A team removes "Continuously Reconciled" from their process —
        they still deploy declaratively from versioned Git via a pull-
        based agent, but only run that agent once per deploy. What
        breaks?
    A1. Drift silently creeps back in between deploys — the system is
        only "correct" at the moment of that one reconciliation, not
        continuously.
    Explanation: each of the 4 principles guards against a specific
    failure mode; dropping Reconciliation reintroduces drift even
    though the other 3 principles are intact.

    Q2. True or False: in a pull-based GitOps model, the CI system
        needs valid Kubernetes cluster credentials to deploy.
    A2. False.
    Explanation: the in-cluster controller initiates the pull itself;
    no external system (CI, developer laptop) ever needs cluster
    credentials — that risk is specific to the push model.

    Q3. A developer says "I reverted my bad commit, but the cluster
        still has the broken version running." Assuming a correctly
        configured Argo CD Application, what's the most likely
        explanation?
    A3. The reconciliation poll interval hasn't elapsed yet (default
        ~120s + up to 60s jitter) and no webhook is configured to
        trigger an immediate refresh — it will self-correct shortly, or
        can be forced immediately.
    Explanation: git revert IS the rollback mechanism, but reconcile
    timing still applies unless a webhook shortcuts the wait.

    Q4. Which OpenGitOps principle is violated by a team that keeps
        their Kubernetes manifests in Git, but has an operator manually
        run `kubectl apply` from their laptop after every merge?
    A4. Pulled Automatically — a human running kubectl apply is a PUSH
        action, even though the source is Git-versioned and declarative.
    Explanation: Declarative + Versioned describe WHAT/WHERE the state
    lives; Pulled Automatically specifically requires an automated
    in-cluster agent to be the one applying it, not a human trigger.
    Q5. True or False: Argo CD, Argo Workflows, Argo Rollouts, and Argo
        Events are four separate CNCF projects, each with its own
        independent maturity level.
    A5. False.
    Explanation: they are four sub-projects of ONE CNCF project
    ("Argo"), which was accepted directly into Incubating (skipping
    Sandbox) on 2020-04-07 and graduated as a single unified project on
    2022-12-06.

    Q6. easypj needs to import a CSV of job postings the moment
        marketing uploads one to a bucket. Which Argo tool(s) handle
        this, and in what order?
    A6. Argo Events detects the upload and triggers Argo Workflows to
        do the actual import — Argo CD isn't involved (nothing about
        cluster DEPLOYMENT state changes).
    Explanation: matches 1.5's use-case map; not every automation
    scenario touches all 4 tools.

    Q7. Why can't Argo CD alone be used to build and test easypj's
        Docker image as part of a "full CI/CD pipeline"?
    A7. Argo CD is CD only — it delivers already-built, already-tagged
        manifests from Git into the cluster; building/testing happens
        upstream (Argo Workflows or an external CI tool) before the
        GitOps repo is even touched.
    Explanation: a common scope misunderstanding — CD ≠ CI, even though
    they're often discussed together.

    Q8. What real historical event happened in January 2018 regarding
        Argo's origin, and why does the exact date matter?
    A8. Intuit ACQUIRED Applatix (Argo's creator) in Jan 2018 — a full
        year AFTER Argo was first created (Aug/Oct 2017 at Applatix,
        an independent startup). It matters because "Argo was created
        at Intuit" is a common but incorrect conflation of these two
        separate events.
    Explanation: reinforces the corrected 1.7 timeline.

    Q9. A junior engineer says "we deploy config as code, so we're
        doing GitOps." What's missing from a complete evaluation?
    A9. "Config as code" alone only satisfies Declarative — GitOps also
        requires Versioned & Immutable (Git as source of truth),
        Pulled Automatically (an in-cluster agent pulls it, nobody
        pushes), and Continuously Reconciled (drift is actively
        corrected, not just applied once).
    Explanation: tests whether a learner can distinguish "using YAML"
    from actually satisfying all 4 OpenGitOps principles.

    Q10. Which company's internal project became Argo Events, and what
         was it originally called?
    A10. BlackRock's Data Science Platform team contributed Argo
         Events, originally an internal BlackRock project called "axis."
    Explanation: a specific, exam-relevant history fact distinct from
    Applatix/Intuit's role in Argo CD/Workflows' origin.

    [N] VISUAL SUMMARY

    introduced the 4 Argo tools as ONE project with a shared history.
    Everything from Chapter 2 onward assumes you can name these 4
    principles and place all 4 tools without hesitation.


Chapter 2: Prerequisites — Kubernetes Refresher for Argo

    🟢 BEGINNER · ⏱️ ~3 hours · 🎯 CAPA domain: none directly (foundational) · 🏢 Daily-Work: high

    [B] WHY THIS MATTERS
    ────────────────────
    Every Argo object you're about to learn is built ENTIRELY out of
    Kubernetes primitives — an Argo CD Application is a CRD sitting right
    next to your Pods; an Argo Workflow step is, under the hood, just a
    Pod. Chapter 1 sold you on WHY GitOps matters; this chapter makes
    sure you have the RAW MATERIALS to build it: enough Kubernetes to
    read a manifest without flinching, enough Git to trust a repo as a
    single source of truth, enough YAML to not lose an hour to one
    missing space, and — by the end — a real local cluster PLUS a real
    easypj/jobpost Docker image sitting on your machine, ready for
    Chapter 3 to install Argo CD onto. Nothing here is directly graded
    on the CAPA exam, but every item in this bag gets used starting
    next chapter.

    ┌───────────────────────────────┬───────────────────────────────┐

    [C] LEARNING OBJECTIVES — 🎯 By the end of this chapter you can:
    ────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
      1. Identify the core K8s objects (Pod, Deployment, Service,
         ConfigMap, Secret, Namespace) every Argo tool builds on.
      2. Explain what a CRD is and name the CRDs each of the four
         Argo tools installs (CD, Workflows, Rollouts, Events).
      3. Read/write a Git GitOps repo layout (base+overlays+argo-apps)
         and explain how targetRevision maps to branch/tag/commit.
      4. Write valid, correctly-indented multi-document YAML and spot
         the top mistakes that break Argo manifests.
      5. Stand up a local cluster (minikube/kind/k3s) and install/
         verify kubectl, the argocd CLI, and the argo CLI.
      6. Build a production-shaped, multi-stage Docker image for
         easypj/jobpost and push it to a registry.
