    PROSE RECAP: 2.1-2.2 build vocabulary (what objects exist, native
    and Argo-added). 2.3-2.4 build the GitOps toolchain (Git as truth,
    YAML as the language it's written in). 2.5-2.6 build the physical
    lab — a running cluster and a real container image for easypj — so
    Chapter 3 has something real to install Argo CD onto.

    2.1  Quick Kubernetes Concepts Review (for Argo)
         - Pods, Deployments, Services, ConfigMaps, Secrets
         📖 KEY TERM — official definitions, condensed: a Pod is "the
            smallest deployable unit of computing you can create and
            manage in Kubernetes" — one or more containers sharing
            network + storage. A Deployment never touches Pods
            directly — it manages a ReplicaSet, which manages the
            Pods; this 2-layer indirection is what makes rolling
            updates possible (a Deployment can own two ReplicaSets
            mid-rollout, old and new, shifting Pod count between them).
         ⚠️ GOTCHA — a Secret is base64-ENCODED, not encrypted.
            `kubectl get secret x -o yaml` hands back data anyone can
            decode with one `base64 -d`; at rest in etcd it's still
            plaintext unless the cluster operator has explicitly
            configured EncryptionConfiguration. Treat Secrets as
            "obscured," not "protected," until you've confirmed
            encryption-at-rest on the specific cluster you're targeting.
         - Namespaces and how Argo uses them
         📖 KEY TERM — a fresh cluster ships with 4 Namespaces already:
            default, kube-system (control-plane components), kube-
            public (readable by all, even unauthenticated, callers),
            and kube-node-lease (per-node Lease objects the kubelet
            heartbeats into — how the control plane detects a dead
            node fast). Argo CD is NOT one of these — installing it
            creates a 5th, `argocd`, and every Application object must
            live there (or an explicitly whitelisted namespace) or
            Argo CD's controller never sees it.
         - Labels and Selectors (critical for Argo CD targeting)
         📖 KEY TERM — Argo CD's DEFAULT resource-tracking method (as
            of current releases) is the `annotation` method: it stamps
            every resource it manages with an
            `argocd.argoproj.io/tracking-id` annotation, not just the
            older `app.kubernetes.io/instance` label (that label-only
            method still exists but is legacy). In easypj's lab,
            `kubectl get deploy easypj -o yaml | grep tracking-id` is
            the authoritative way to see which Application owns a
            resource — don't assume the label alone is the source of
            truth.
         ⚠️ GOTCHA — a Deployment's `spec.selector` is IMMUTABLE once
            created (apps/v1 API rule) — change the label a Deployment
            selects on and plain `kubectl apply` fails with "field is
            immutable." Argo CD hits the identical wall on sync; the
            documented workaround is the
            `argocd.argoproj.io/sync-options: Force=true,Replace=true`
            annotation, which makes Argo CD delete+recreate the object
            instead of patching it.
         [QUICK REFERENCE TABLE: K8s objects needed for Argo]
         ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  K8S OBJECTS YOU MUST KNOW BEFORE ARGO                     │
         │                                                            │
         │  ┌─────────────┬───────────────────┬──────────────────────┐│
         │  │ OBJECT      │ PURPOSE            │ WHY ARGO CD CARES    ││
         │  ├─────────────┼───────────────────┼──────────────────────┤│
         │  │ Pod         │ Smallest unit —    │ What actually runs   ││
         │  │             │ 1+ containers      │ easypj/jobpost        ││
         │  │ Deployment  │ Manages ReplicaSets│ The Kind Argo CD      ││
         │  │             │ → rolling updates  │ health-checks most    ││
         │  │ Service     │ Stable network     │ Fronts easypj's Pods; ││
         │  │             │ identity for Pods  │ Rollouts swaps its    ││
         │  │             │                    │ selector for canary   ││
         │  │ ConfigMap   │ Non-secret config  │ DJANGO_SETTINGS_      ││
         │  │             │ as key-value data  │ MODULE, DEBUG, etc.   ││
         │  │ Secret      │ base64-encoded     │ DB password, Django   ││
         │  │             │ sensitive data     │ SECRET_KEY (Ch 7)     ││
         │  │ Namespace   │ Logical cluster    │ easypj-dev/staging/   ││
         │  │             │ partition          │ prod live in separate ││
         │  │             │                    │ ones                  ││
         │  └─────────────┴───────────────────┴──────────────────────┘│
         │                                                            │
         │  🎯 Every one of these is a NATIVE Kind Argo CD already     │
         │  understands out of the box — Chapter 2.2 next explains     │
         │  how Argo's OWN objects (Application, Workflow, Rollout...) │
         │  get taught to Kubernetes the exact same way.                │
         └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    2.2  CRDs (Custom Resource Definitions) — How Argo Extends Kubernetes
         - What is a CRD? — "Teaching Kubernetes new tricks"
         📖 KEY TERM — precisely, a CustomResourceDefinition is itself
            a CLUSTER-SCOPED object (never namespaced, even though the
            custom resources it defines usually ARE) that teaches
            kube-apiserver a new Kind. Its metadata.name isn't
            arbitrary — it MUST equal <plural>.<group> exactly, e.g.
            applications.argoproj.io. That's why `kubectl get crd`
            output always looks like a list of dotted FQDNs.
         🧠 ANALOGY — a CRD is like adding a new TABLE (with a schema)
            to a database: `kubectl apply -f crd.yaml` is the
            equivalent of CREATE TABLE. Each Application/Workflow/
            Rollout object you then apply is just a ROW in that table
            — same kubectl, same RBAC verbs, same -o yaml as native
            objects, which is the entire point of the mechanism.
         - How Argo installs CRDs: Application, Workflow, Rollout, EventSource, Sensor
         ⚠️ GOTCHA — because CRDs are cluster-scoped, installing or
            upgrading ANY of them (Argo CD, Workflows, Rollouts, or
            Events) requires cluster-admin-level privilege — a
            namespace-scoped Role can never create a CRD, only a
            ClusterRole can. Common CI/CD trap: a deploy pipeline with
            only namespace-scoped RBAC can create Applications all day
            but will hard-fail the one time it needs to bump Argo CD's
            own CRD version.
         - Viewing installed CRDs: kubectl get crd | grep argo
         🎯 EXAM RELEVANCE — this command only proves a CRD's SCHEMA is
            installed, not that its CONTROLLER is running and healthy.
            Those are two independent halves — expect a CAPA question
            where CRDs show installed but nothing reconciles, and the
            correct diagnosis is "controller Pod down / CrashLoopBackOff,"
            not "reinstall the CRD."
         [DIAGRAM: K8s API server with native resources + Argo CRDs]
         ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  KUBERNETES API SERVER                                     │
         │                                                            │
         │  ┌──────────────────┐  ┌─────────────────────────────────┐│
         │  │ Native Resources  │  │ Argo CRDs (installed by Argo)   ││
         │  │                  │  │                                  ││
         │  │  Pod             │  │  Application (argoproj.io)       ││
         │  │  Deployment      │  │  AppProject (argoproj.io)        ││
         │  │  Service         │  │  Workflow (argoproj.io)          ││
         │  │  ConfigMap       │  │  WorkflowTemplate (argoproj.io)  ││
         │  │  Secret          │  │  Rollout (argoproj.io)           ││
         │  │  Namespace       │  │  EventSource (argoproj.io)       ││
         │  │  ...             │  │  Sensor (argoproj.io)            ││
         │  └──────────────────┘  └─────────────────────────────────┘│
         └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    2.3  Git Refresher for GitOps
         - Branches, commits, pull requests
         📖 KEY TERM — a commit SHA is CONTENT-ADDRESSED (a hash of the
            commit's tree+parent+message) and can never be reused for
            different content. A branch is just a movable POINTER (a
            file under .git/refs/heads/ holding one SHA) that advances
            on every commit. This is exactly why Argo CD's
            targetRevision behaves differently for each: point it at a
            branch and Argo CD tracks a moving target; point it at a
            SHA (or an immutable tag) and it's pinned.
         - Git as source of truth — monorepo vs. app-of-apps
         ⚠️ GOTCHA — "monorepo" and "app-of-apps" are NOT two competing
            choices. Monorepo/polyrepo is a REPO-COUNT decision (does
            easypj-k8s live in one repo or many?). App-of-apps is an
            ARGO CD PATTERN — a parent Application whose only job is to
            declare other Applications — and it works equally well
            against a monorepo or spread across polyrepos. Don't
            conflate the two on the exam.
         🏢 DAILY-WORK — in production, easypj-k8s is almost always a
            SEPARATE repo from easypj/jobpost's application source
            code, not a subfolder of it: (1) a CI job bumping the image
            tag in easypj-k8s won't re-trigger the app repo's own
            build — no infinite loop — and (2) commit access to "what's
            running in prod" can be restricted independently of "who
            writes application code."
         - Git tags and how Argo CD uses them for versioning
         ⚠️ GOTCHA — Git tags are conventionally treated as immutable,
            but Git itself does NOT enforce that: `git tag -f v1.2.3
            <newsha>` silently moves an existing tag, and `git push
            --force --tags` propagates it. "Immutable" for GitOps
            purposes is a TEAM POLICY (tag-protection rules on the Git
            host), not a Git guarantee — a force-moved tag is exactly
            the worst-case rollback scenario this chapter's advice is
            trying to prevent.
         [DIAGRAM: Git repo structure for easypj GitOps]
         ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  GITOPS REPO STRUCTURE — easypj-k8s                        │
         │                                                            │
         │  main branch                                               │
         │  ├── base/              ← Common K8s manifests (DRY)       │
         │  │   ├── deployment.yaml                                   │
         │  │   ├── service.yaml                                      │
         │  │   ├── configmap.yaml                                    │
         │  │   ├── ingress.yaml                                      │
         │  │   └── kustomization.yaml                               │
         │  │                                                        │
         │  ├── overlays/         ← Environment variations            │
         │  │   ├── dev/                                              │
         │  │   │   ├── kustomization.yaml  ← patches base for dev   │
         │  │   │   └── replica-count.yaml  ← dev: 1 replica          │
         │  │   ├── staging/                                          │
         │  │   │   ├── kustomization.yaml  ← patches base for staging│
         │  │   │   └── replica-count.yaml  ← staging: 2 replicas     │
         │  │   └── prod/                                             │
         │  │       ├── kustomization.yaml  ← patches base for prod  │
         │  │       └── replica-count.yaml  ← prod: 5 replicas        │
         │  │                                                        │
         │  └── argo-apps/        ← Argo CD Application definitions   │
         │      ├── easypj-dev.yaml                                   │
         │      ├── easypj-staging.yaml                               │
         │      ├── easypj-prod.yaml                                  │
         │      └── app-of-apps.yaml ← Bootstrap: deploys all above  │
         │                                                            │
         │  HOW ARGO CD USES IT:                                      │
         │  Application → source.path = "overlays/dev"                │
         │  Application → source.targetRevision = "main"              │
         │  Argo CD clones → runs Kustomize build overlays/dev        │
         │  → applies resulting YAML to cluster                       │
         │                                                            │
         │  FOR VERSIONING: Git tags like "v1.2.3" let you point     │
         │  targetRevision to a specific release for rollback.        │
         └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    2.4  YAML Mastery for Argo
         - Indentation rules (Argo is VERY strict!)
         🔧 UNDER THE HOOD — YAML never survives past the first hop.
            kubectl parses it client-side and sends kube-apiserver JSON
            (or protobuf) over HTTPS — the apiserver's Go structs know
            nothing about indentation. Since Kubernetes 1.6, objects
            are then stored in etcd protobuf-ENCODED, not as JSON or
            YAML. "Fixing an indentation error" really means "getting
            past the client-side YAML->JSON parse step" — it's always
            caught before your data becomes a Kubernetes object at all.
         - Multi-document YAML (---)
         - Common YAML pitfalls in Argo manifests
         ⚠️ GOTCHA — the "Norway Problem": YAML 1.1 (what virtually all
            Kubernetes tooling parses) treats bare NO, YES, ON, OFF, Y,
            N, TRUE, FALSE (any case) as BOOLEANS, not strings — so a
            ConfigMap value like `country: NO` silently becomes boolean
            false, not the string "NO". Always quote a value that could
            be misread this way: `country: "NO"`. This is a real, still-
            open YAML spec quirk, not an Argo-specific bug — and a
            classic ConfigMap data-corruption trap.
         [GOTCHA CARDS: Top 5 YAML mistakes in Argo]
         ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  ⚠️ TOP 5 YAML MISTAKES IN ARGO                            │
         │                                                            │
         │  1. MISSING "argocd" NAMESPACE                             │
         │     ❌ metadata.namespace: default                         │
         │     ✅ metadata.namespace: argocd                          │
         │     Argo CD Applications MUST be in argocd namespace!      │
         │                                                            │
         │  2. INDENTATION ERRORS in nested specs                     │
         │     ❌ spec:                                               │
         │       source:                                             │
         │       repoURL: ...    (not indented under source!)        │
         │     ✅ spec:                                               │
         │         source:                                           │
         │           repoURL: ...  (2-space indent under source)     │
         │                                                            │
         │  3. WRONG API VERSION                                      │
         │     ❌ apiVersion: argoproj.io/v1                          │
         │     ✅ apiVersion: argoproj.io/v1alpha1                    │
         │     All Argo CRDs use v1alpha1 (check docs for specific)  │
         │                                                            │
         │  4. FORGETTING --- SEPARATOR in multi-doc YAML             │
         │     ❌ apiVersion: v1                                      │
         │        kind: Namespace                                    │
         │        ---                                                 │
         │        apiVersion: argoproj.io/v1alpha1                    │
         │        kind: Application                                  │
         │     (Looks OK but first doc needs --- at start too!)      │
         │                                                            │
         │  5. COPY-PASTE CLUSTER URL WITH TRAILING SLASH             │
         │     ❌ server: https://kubernetes.default.svc/             │
         │     ✅ server: https://kubernetes.default.svc              │
         │     Trailing slash causes mysterious connection errors    │
         └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    2.5  Your Lab Environment Setup
         - Installing minikube/kind/k3s
         📖 KEY TERM — minikube/kind's --driver flag picks the
            ISOLATION LAYER the "node" runs in: docker (a single
            container standing in for a whole node — what kind always
            uses, and minikube's usual default), vs. a real VM driver
            (hyperkit/virtualbox/qemu). The docker driver is why a
            container can never request more CPU/RAM than the Docker
            Engine it runs inside is itself allowed to use.
         - Installing kubectl and configuring kubeconfig
         📖 KEY TERM — a kubeconfig context bundles exactly 3 things: a
            CLUSTER (API server URL + CA cert), a USER (credentials),
            and a default NAMESPACE. `kubectl config use-context`
            switches all 3 at once — the #1 reason "kubectl works but
            hits the wrong cluster" bugs happen: someone's
            current-context silently points somewhere else.
         ⚠️ GOTCHA — kubectl is only officially supported within ONE
            minor version of kube-apiserver, either direction (server
            1.34 <-> kubectl 1.33/1.34/1.35). It often still works
            further out of range, but field behavior can silently
            differ — sanity-check with `kubectl version` (client +
            server) rather than assuming any kubectl binary works
            against any cluster.
         - Verifying cluster: kubectl cluster-info
         ⚠️ GOTCHA / FACT-CHECK — the expected `kubectl get nodes`
            output in the Lab Setup diagram below lists ROLES as
            `master`, but that label was replaced by `control-plane`
            starting kubeadm v1.20 (Dec 2020) — years before the
            v1.29.x this same diagram claims. On a real kind/minikube
            cluster at that version you'll see `control-plane`; on k3s
            you'll see the combined `control-plane,etcd,master`. Don't
            be alarmed if your own output doesn't match this guide's
            example line-for-line.
         [STEP-BY-STEP: Lab setup commands with expected output]
         ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  LAB SETUP — Complete Commands                             │
         │                                                            │
         │  # Option A: KIND (lightweight, Docker-based)              │
         │  brew install kind      # macOS                            │
         │  kind create cluster --name argo-lab                       │
         │                                                            │
         │  # Option B: Minikube                                      │
         │  minikube start --cpus=4 --memory=8192 --driver=docker     │
         │                                                            │
         │  # Option C: k3s (Rancher, very lightweight)               │
         │  curl -sfL https://get.k3s.io | sh -                       │
         │                                                            │
         │  # Verify cluster is running:                              │
         │  kubectl cluster-info                                      │
         │  kubectl get nodes                                         │

         │  #          node  Ready   master 30s   v1.29.x             │
         │                                                            │

         │  curl -LO "https://dl.k8s.io/release/$(curl -L -s         │
         │    https://dl.k8s.io/release/stable.txt)/bin/linux/amd64/  │

         │  # Install Argo Workflows CLI:                             │
         │  curl -sLO ...argo-workflows/.../argo-linux-amd64.gz      │
         │  gunzip argo-linux-amd64.gz && chmod +x argo-linux-amd64   │
         │  mv argo-linux-amd64 /usr/local/bin/argo                  │
         │                                                            │

         │  && argo version                                           │
         └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    2.6  Docker Image for easypj — Preparing Your App
         - Writing a Dockerfile for easypj/jobpost Django app
         ⚠️ GOTCHA — the Dockerfile's HEALTHCHECK instruction (below)
            is 100% IGNORED by Kubernetes. Kubelet never reads or
            executes it — Kubernetes only acts on livenessProbe /
            readinessProbe / startupProbe defined explicitly in the Pod
            spec. HEALTHCHECK only matters if you run this image with
            plain `docker run` outside Kubernetes. This matters a lot
            starting in the Rollouts chapters: a canary's automated
            analysis gates on the Pod's readinessProbe, never on this
            Dockerfile line.
         - Multi-stage build for smaller images
         - .dockerignore best practices
         🏢 DAILY-WORK — .dockerignore keeps .env out of the build
            CONTEXT, but it can't undo a secret already baked into an
            earlier layer (e.g. an ARG SECRET_KEY=... or a stray COPY
            .env higher up) — `docker history` can still recover it.
            The only real fix for secrets in images is: never ARG/COPY
            them in the first place; inject at runtime via a Kubernetes
            Secret instead.
         - Building and pushing to a registry
         [CODE BLOCK: Complete Dockerfile for easypj/jobpost]
         ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  DOCKERFILE — multi-stage build for easypj                 │
         │                                                            │
         │  # STAGE 1: Build (compile dependencies)                   │
         │  FROM python:3.12-slim AS builder                          │
         │  WORKDIR /app                                              │
         │  COPY requirements.txt .                                   │
         │  RUN pip install --user --no-cache-dir -r requirements.txt │
         │                                                            │
         │  # STAGE 2: Runtime (minimal image)                        │
         │  FROM python:3.12-slim                                     │
         │  WORKDIR /app                                              │
         │                                                            │
         │  # Copy only installed packages from builder               │
         │  COPY --from=builder /root/.local /root/.local             │
         │                                                            │
         │  # Copy application code                                   │
         │  COPY . .                                                  │
         │                                                            │
         │  # Environment variables                                   │
         │  ENV PYTHONUNBUFFERED=1 \                                  │
         │      PYTHONDONTWRITEBYTECODE=1 \                           │
         │      PATH=/root/.local/bin:$PATH                           │
         │                                                            │
         │  # Run as non-root user                                    │
         │  RUN useradd -m -u 1000 django && chown -R django:django /app│
         │  USER django                                               │
         │                                                            │
         │  # Collect static files and expose port                    │
         │  RUN python manage.py collectstatic --noinput              │
         │  EXPOSE 8000                                               │
         │                                                            │
         │  # Health check                                            │
         │  HEALTHCHECK --interval=30s --timeout=3s \                 │
         │    CMD python -c "import urllib.request; \                 │
         │    urllib.request.urlopen('http://localhost:8000/health/')" │
         │                                                            │
         │  CMD ["gunicorn", "easypj.wsgi:application", \             │
         │       "--bind", "0.0.0.0:8000", "--workers", "3"]         │
         │                                                            │
         │  # Build and push:                                         │
         │  # docker build -t registry.example.com/easypj:v1.0 .     │
         │  # docker push registry.example.com/easypj:v1.0           │
         │                                                            │
         │  .dockerignore (keep image small):                         │
         │    __pycache__/                                            │
         │    *.pyc                                                   │
         │    .git/                                                   │
         │    .env                                                    │
         │    .venv/                                                  │
         │    static/                                                 │
         │    media/                                                  │
         └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
         ⚠️ GOTCHA / FACT-CHECK — the Dockerfile above has a real,
            reproducible bug. USER django (the line before "Collect
            static files") runs BEFORE `RUN python manage.py
            collectstatic --noinput`. Django itself was pip-installed
            with --user in the builder stage (landing in
            /root/.local), then COPY'd verbatim into /root/.local in
            the final stage — never given --chown, and the earlier
            `chown -R django:django /app` only touches /app, never
            /root. On python:3.12-slim (Debian 12/bookworm), /root is
            mode 0700 — traversable ONLY by root — so django cannot
            even import django. This build fails on the collectstatic
            line every single time, at `docker build`, not just at
            runtime. Verify yourself:
            `docker run --rm python:3.12-slim stat -c '%a %U' /root`
            -> `700 root`.
            FIX — create the user first and copy into ITS home, not
            root's:
                RUN useradd -m -u 1000 django
                COPY --from=builder --chown=django:django \
                  /root/.local /home/django/.local
                COPY --chown=django:django . .
                ENV PATH=/home/django/.local/bin:$PATH
                USER django
                RUN python manage.py collectstatic --noinput
            (Matches the canonical multi-stage pip install --user
            pattern, which never combines it with a non-root USER
            switch until after the copy.)


    [G] UNDER THE HOOD 🔧 — How a CRD Actually Extends the K8s API Server
    ────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
    A CustomResourceDefinition is just another object stored in etcd —
    applying it does NOT recompile or restart kube-apiserver. Instead,
    kube-apiserver's built-in generic apiserver code reads installed
    CRDs and dynamically starts serving a new REST path, e.g.:
        /apis/argoproj.io/v1alpha1/namespaces/{ns}/applications
    This is a DIFFERENT mechanism from the API Aggregation Layer (used
    by things like metrics-server), which proxies to a separate
    subordinate API server process — CRDs never do that; they're
    served natively.

    Every Application/Workflow/Rollout/Sensor object you `kubectl apply`
    is validated against the OpenAPI v3 schema embedded in its CRD,
    then stored in etcd exactly like a Pod. On its own, that's ALL a
    CRD does — storage + validation + a REST API. Nothing happens until
    a CONTROLLER (a separate running process, e.g.
    argocd-application-controller) watches that Kind via an Informer,
    queues changed objects, and runs a reconcile loop that does the
    real work (clone Git + diff + apply, in Argo CD's case).

        kubectl apply -f app.yaml
              |
              v
        kube-apiserver validates against CRD's OpenAPI schema
              |
              v
        stored in etcd (just another object)
              |
              v
        controller's Informer fires (watch event) --> reconcile loop
              |                                            |
              v                                            v
        workqueue                                   real work happens
                                                (git clone, kubectl apply,
                                                 spawn a Pod, evaluate a
                                                 canary step, etc.)

    PROSE RECAP: CRD = noun (schema Kubernetes now understands and
    stores). Controller = verb (the code that watches that noun and
    acts). Every Argo tool ships both halves; memorize this pairing —
    it's the model every later chapter builds on.

    [H] KEY TAKEAWAYS 🔑
    ─────────────────────
    - Every Argo object is "just" a CRD stored in etcd like a native
      resource — inert without its controller.
    - CRD counts: Argo CD 3 (Application/AppProject/ApplicationSet),
      Workflows 8 (headline: Workflow/WorkflowTemplate/
      ClusterWorkflowTemplate/CronWorkflow), Rollouts 5 (Rollout/
      Experiment/AnalysisRun/AnalysisTemplate/ClusterAnalysisTemplate),
      Events 3 (EventSource/Sensor/EventBus) — all argoproj.io/v1alpha1.
    - Git is the single source of truth; targetRevision = branch, tag,
      or commit SHA; Argo CD polls every ~3 min by default (instant
      with a webhook).
    - Indentation, the argocd namespace rule, and the v1alpha1
      apiVersion are the 3 mistakes that break the most Argo YAML.
    - Your lab toolchain (cluster + kubectl + argocd CLI + argo CLI)
      is assumed running from Chapter 3 onward.
    - Never tag a GitOps image `:latest` — use an immutable tag (git
      SHA/semver) so every release is a visible Git diff.
    🎯 Exam relevance: none directly graded, but Ch3 and Ch9 assume
       everything above as baseline literacy.

    [I] EXAM GOTCHAS ⚠️
    ─────────────────────
    1. Applications MUST live in the argocd namespace (or a namespace
       explicitly whitelisted via application.namespaces) — one applied
       to `default` is simply invisible to Argo CD, no error thrown.
    2. All Argo CRDs use apiVersion: argoproj.io/v1alpha1 — not v1,
       not v1beta1. Typing argoproj.io/v1 is the #1 rejected-YAML cause.
    3. TAB characters are illegal anywhere in YAML indentation — a hard
       parse error, not a linter warning.
    4. A leading --- before the FIRST document in multi-doc YAML is
       OPTIONAL — only the separator BETWEEN documents is required.

    [J] SPEED TIPS ⚡
    ───────────────────
    1. `kubectl create deployment web --image=x --dry-run=client -o yaml
        > file.yaml` — generate a starter manifest instead of hand-
       typing indentation from scratch.
    2. `kubectl config current-context` before every lab exercise —
       confirms which cluster you're about to hit.
    3. `kubectl explain application.spec --api-version=argoproj.io/v1alpha1`
       — once a CRD is installed, kubectl explain gives inline field
       docs for Argo objects exactly like it does for Pods.

    [K] COMMANDS TO REMEMBER 🔑
    ─────────────────────────────
      Cluster:   kubectl cluster-info · kubectl get nodes ·
                 kubectl config current-context / get-contexts
      CRDs:      kubectl get crd | grep argo ·
                 kubectl api-resources --api-group=argoproj.io ·
                 kubectl explain <kind>.spec
      Git:       git clone <url> · git tag v1.2.3 · git log --oneline
      Docker:    docker build -t registry.example.com/easypj:<git-sha> . ·
                 docker push registry.example.com/easypj:<git-sha>
      Verify:    kubectl version --client · argocd version --client ·
                 argo version

    [L] PRACTICE DRILL 🧪 — ⏱️ 15-Minute Lab Stand-Up
    ────────────────────────────────────────────────────
      1. (5 min) Start a local cluster (kind/minikube/k3s).
      2. (2 min) `kubectl cluster-info` + `kubectl get nodes` — confirm
         STATUS=Ready.
      3. (5 min) Hand-write a 2-document YAML file (Namespace +
         ConfigMap) with correct --- separator and 2-space indentation;
         `kubectl apply -f`.
      4. (3 min) `docker build -t easypj:drill .` using the 2.6
         Dockerfile; confirm with `docker images`.
      SUCCESS CRITERIA: cluster Ready, both objects created with zero
      indentation errors, image builds clean.

    [M] LAB QUESTIONS 🧪 (10)
    ───────────────────────────
    Q1. Right after installing ONLY Argo CD, `kubectl get crd | grep argo`
        shows 3 lines. Which 3?
    A1. applications.argoproj.io, appprojects.argoproj.io,
        applicationsets.argoproj.io
    Explanation: Argo CD's install manifest defines exactly these 3 CRDs;
    Workflow/Rollout/Event CRDs only appear once those tools are
    separately installed.

    Q2. A teammate writes `apiVersion: argoproj.io/v1` on an Application
        and `kubectl apply -f` fails. Why, and the fix?
    A2. Wrong version — must be argoproj.io/v1alpha1.
    Explanation: no Argo CRD has reached v1/v1beta1 yet; this is the
    single most common YAML-rejection cause for beginners.

    Q3. You `kubectl apply -f app.yaml` an Application into `default`.
        It succeeds with no error, but `argocd app list` never shows it.
    A3. Wrong namespace — Applications must be in the argocd namespace
        (or an explicitly whitelisted one).
    Explanation: kubectl succeeds because `default` is a valid namespace
    at the API level; Argo CD's controller simply isn't watching it, so
    the object exists but is invisible to Argo CD.

    Q4. Fix this broken 2-document YAML: two documents with duplicate
        top-level apiVersion/kind keys and no separator between them.
    A4. Insert `---` on its own line between the two documents.
    Explanation: without it, a parser reads ONE document with a
    duplicate top-level apiVersion/kind key — invalid, or the first
    doc is silently dropped. (A leading --- before doc #1 is optional.)

    Q5. easypj's dev Application has targetRevision=main,
        path=overlays/dev. You want PROD to deploy exactly tag v1.2.3
        instead of tracking a branch. What ONE field changes on the
        PROD Application, to what?
    A5. source.targetRevision: v1.2.3 (path stays overlays/prod — a
        separate Application object).
    Explanation: targetRevision accepts branch/tag/SHA; pinning to a
    tag is how GitOps gets reproducible, controlled promotion/rollback.

    Q6. You add a key to easypj's ConfigMap and push. Argo CD shows
        "Synced" within 3 min, but running Pods still serve OLD config.
        Is Argo CD broken?
    A6. No.
    Explanation: Argo CD's job ends at "the object in etcd matches
    Git." Env-var consumers only read config at container start; a
    Pod restart (e.g. rollout restart) is needed to pick up new values
    — this is Kubernetes behavior, not an Argo bug.

    Q7. When would you pick k3s over kind/minikube for this lab?
    A7. kind: fastest spin-up, Docker-in-Docker, best for CI/ephemeral
        labs. minikube: richest addons, best for a persistent learner
        machine. k3s: real systemd service, survives reboots, closest
        to "real" lightweight prod.
    Explanation: CAPA's own exam environment supplies its own cluster —
    the choice here is purely local workflow preference.

    Q8. `minikube start --cpus=4 --memory=8192 --driver=docker` fails,
        and Docker Desktop is capped at 4GB in its own Settings >
        Resources. What's happening?
    A8. Docker Desktop's own resource cap is a hard ceiling underneath
        minikube's request — raise the Docker Desktop limit first.
    Explanation: --memory tells minikube what to ASK the driver for; it
    cannot exceed what the driver's VM has actually been given.

    Q9. `RUN python manage.py collectstatic --noinput` fails during
        `docker build` with "SECRET_KEY setting must not be empty."
    A9. collectstatic imports Django's full settings module at BUILD
        time, before any real K8s Secret exists — use a build-only
        DUMMY SECRET_KEY (safe, since collectstatic doesn't need the
        real one), or move collectstatic to a deploy-time Job instead.
    Explanation: a real, common Django-in-Docker pitfall independent
    of Argo, but one you'll hit the first time you containerize easypj.

    Q10. Two Applications target the same cluster, but one uses
         `server: https://kubernetes.default.svc` and the other
         `.../svc/` (trailing slash). `argocd cluster list` shows them
         as DIFFERENT clusters. Why, and the fix?
    A10. Argo CD treats the server URL as an exact string — no
         normalization — so the trailing slash creates a second,
         unregistered "cluster" key. Fix: always omit the trailing
         slash for the in-cluster destination.
    Explanation: documented Argo CD behavior — string consistency
    matters more than it "should" here.

    [N] VISUAL SUMMARY
    ────────────────────
    ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
    │  CHAPTER 2 IN ONE PICTURE                                          │
    │                                                                    │
    │  Git repo (targetRevision: branch/tag/SHA)                         │
    │       │  git push                                                  │
    │       v                                                            │
    │  YAML manifests (2-space indent, ---separated, v1alpha1)           │
    │       │  kubectl apply -f                                          │
    │       v                                                            │
    │  kube-apiserver (native objects + Argo CRDs, same REST path)       │
    │       │  validated + stored                                        │
    │       v                                                            │
    │  etcd  --> controller watch/reconcile --> real action              │
    │                                                                    │
    │  easypj/jobpost source --> docker build (multi-stage) -->          │
    │      easypj:<git-sha> --> pushed to registry                       │
    │      (never :latest — GitOps needs a visible tag diff)             │
    └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
    PROSE RECAP: Chapter 2 assembled every piece Chapter 3 will wire
    together: Kubernetes vocabulary (2.1), the CRD mechanism Argo uses
    to add itself to that vocabulary (2.2), Git as the source of truth
    (2.3) written in valid YAML (2.4), a real cluster with the three
    CLIs installed (2.5), and a real, immutably-tagged easypj Docker
    image (2.6) — ready for Argo CD to deploy starting next chapter.


Chapter 3: Installing and Configuring Argo CD
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
    🟡 INTERMEDIATE · ⏱️ ~3 hours · 🎯 CAPA domain: Argo CD 34% · 🏢 Daily-Work: high

    [B] WHY THIS MATTERS
    ────────────────────
    By the end of Chapter 2 you had easypj/jobpost built and containerized —
    an image sitting in a registry, a pile of Kubernetes YAML sitting in a
    repo. Getting it running the old way means SSH-ing to wherever you run
    kubectl, applying manifests by hand, and repeating that dance for dev,
    then staging, then production — hoping nobody `kubectl edit`s the live
    Service at 2am and silently diverges it from what's in Git. Argo CD
    exists to make that divergence *impossible to hide*: install it once
    into your cluster, point it at your easypj-k8s repo, and from that
    moment the cluster continuously pulls itself toward whatever Git says —
    not the other way around. This chapter installs the real control plane
    (not a diagram of one), meets the pods that do the work, and finishes
    with easypj running in-cluster as a genuine Argo CD Application, synced
    and healthy. Every later chapter assumes this control plane is already up.

    ┌───────────────────────────────┬───────────────────────────────┐
    │  BEFORE — MANUAL DEPLOY        │  AFTER — ARGO CD (GITOPS)      │
    ├───────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────┤
    │  You: kubectl apply -f *.yaml │  You: git push                 │
    │  You: repeat for staging       │  Argo CD: notices (watch/poll) │
    │  You: repeat for prod          │  Argo CD: diffs live vs Git    │
    │  Someone: kubectl edit (drift) │  Argo CD: flags/fixes drift    │
    │  Nobody notices until it breaks│  You: see OutOfSync in the UI  │
    └───────────────────────────────┴───────────────────────────────┘

    [C] LEARNING OBJECTIVES — "🎯 What you'll be able to DO"
    ────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
    - Explain what Argo CD is and reproduce its 3-component core
      architecture (API server / repo-server / application-controller).
    - Install Argo CD with the correct server-side-apply command and
      verify all 7 pods reach Running.
    - Retrieve initial admin credentials, authenticate via both UI and
      CLI, and rotate the password.
    - Name each of Argo CD's 7 components and state its one job.
    - Author and apply an Application manifest that deploys easypj from
      Git and drives it to Synced/Healthy.
    - Choose and justify a sync policy (manual vs automated, prune,
      self-heal, sync waves) for a given environment.
