    engine pointed at real work. Every pattern here — backup-then-
    migrate-then-verify, parallel CI fan-out, artifact-based ETL,
    schedule-appropriate concurrencyPolicy — reappears verbatim in
    Chapter 20's full pipeline and Chapter 25's hands-on labs.


Chapter 13: Argo Workflows CLI, UI, and Debugging
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
    🟡 INTERMEDIATE · ⏱️ ~2 hours · 🎯 CAPA domain: Argo Workflows 36% (CLI fluency across the whole domain) · 🏢 Daily-Work: very high

    [B] WHY THIS MATTERS
    ────────────────────
    Every chapter so far assumed you could operate a Workflow once it
    existed — submit it, watch it, read its logs. This chapter makes
    that assumption true, and closes the gaps that trip up almost
    everyone the first time: `argo terminate` and `argo stop` sound
    identical and behave very differently; `argo logs -c` looks like a
    step selector and is actually a container selector; `argo retry`
    and `argo resubmit` both "run it again" but resolve templates
    differently. Getting these exactly right is the difference between
    a clean incident response on easypj/jobpost and an on-call engineer
    losing 20 minutes to a command that did something subtly other than
    what they meant.

    ┌────────────────────────────────┬────────────────────────────────┐
    │  BEFORE CHAPTER 13              │  AFTER CHAPTER 13               │
    ├────────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────┤
    │ "argo terminate and argo stop   │ "stop runs onExit first;        │
    │  are basically the same"        │  terminate skips it — I pick    │
    │                                  │  on purpose, not by habit"      │
    │ "-c must mean 'which step'"     │ "-c is the CONTAINER (main vs   │
    │                                  │  wait); --node-field-selector   │
    │                                  │  picks the step"                │
    │ Reading logs in the UI only     │ Comfortable falling back to     │
    │                                  │ kubectl logs when argo logs is  │
    │                                  │ unexpectedly silent             │
    └────────────────────────────────┴────────────────────────────────┘

    [C] LEARNING OBJECTIVES — 🎯 What you'll be able to DO
    ────────────────────────────────────────────────────
      - Choose correctly between argo stop and argo terminate based on
        whether onExit cleanup matters.
      - Use `-c` for containers and `--node-field-selector` for steps
        without confusing the two.
      - Explain the resolve-time difference between argo retry and argo
        resubmit and pick the right one for a given incident.
      - Navigate the Workflows UI's list/DAG/timeline/logs views and
        know which one answers which question fastest.
      - Diagnose the 3 most common real failure classes (image pull,
        scheduling/resources, missing artifact repo) from symptoms alone.
      - Insert a temporary suspend step to get a live shell into a
        failing pipeline instead of guessing from logs.

    [D] CAPA RELEVANCE
    ───────────────────
    CLI command recall is directly and heavily tested across the whole
    Workflows domain (36%) — this chapter is the consolidated reference
    the exam draws its command-recognition questions from. Expect exact-
    command and exact-flag questions ("which command re-resolves a
    templateRef" / "which flag selects a container, not a step").

    [E] SECTION-MAP DIAGRAM — you are here

    13.1  Argo CLI — Essential Commands
          - argo submit, argo list, argo get, argo logs
          - argo watch (real-time workflow monitoring)
          - argo retry, argo terminate, argo delete
          - argo template create/delete/list
          [COMMAND REFERENCE TABLE: All essential CLI commands]
          ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
          │  ARGO WORKFLOWS CLI — ESSENTIAL COMMANDS                   │
          │                                                            │
          │  ┌───────────────────────────┬────────────────────────────┐│
          │  │ COMMAND                  │ DESCRIPTION                ││
          │  ├───────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────┤│
          │  │ SUBMIT & RUN              │                            ││
          │  │ argo submit <file.yaml>   │ Submit a workflow          ││

          │  │   wftmpl/<name>           │                            ││
          │  │ argo resubmit <wf>        │ NEW workflow, re-resolves    ││
          │  │                           │ templateRef (Ch 10 [G])      ││
          │  │ argo retry <wf>           │ SAME workflow object, only   ││
          │  │                           │ re-runs FAILED nodes         ││
          │  │ argo lint <file.yaml>     │ Validate syntax before submit││
          │  ├───────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────┤│
          │  │ MONITOR                   │                            ││
          │  │ argo list                 │ List all workflows          ││
          │  │ argo list -n <namespace>  │ List in specific namespace ││
          │  │ argo get <wf>             │ Show workflow details       ││
          │  │ argo watch <wf>           │ Real-time status updates    ││
          │  │ argo logs <wf>            │ Stream logs of all steps    ││
          │  │ argo logs <wf> -c <cntnr> │ Logs of specific CONTAINER  ││
          │  │                           │ (e.g. main vs wait/init) —   ││
          │  │                           │ NOT a step-name selector     ││
          │  │ argo wait <wf>            │ Wait for completion         ││
          │  ├───────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────┤│
          │  │ MANAGE                    │                            ││
          │  │ argo terminate <wf>       │ Stop NOW, SKIP exit handlers││
          │  │ argo stop <wf>            │ Stop, but RUN exit handlers ││
          │  │                           │ first (onExit, Ch 11.3)      ││
          │  │ argo delete <wf>          │ Delete a workflow           ││
          │  │ argo suspend <wf>         │ Pause a workflow            ││
          │  │ argo resume <wf>          │ Resume a paused workflow    ││
          │  │ argo archive list         │ List archived workflows     ││
          │  ├───────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────┤│
          │  │ TEMPLATES                 │                            ││
          │  │ argo template create <f>  │ Create WorkflowTemplate     ││
          │  │ argo template list        │ List WorkflowTemplates      ││
          │  │ argo template get <name>  │ Get template details        ││
          │  │ argo template delete <n>  │ Delete template             ││
          │  │ argo cron create <file>   │ Create CronWorkflow         ││
          │  │ argo cron list            │ List CronWorkflows          ││
          │  │ argo cron delete <name>   │ Delete CronWorkflow         ││
          │  ├───────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────┤│
          │  │ DIAGNOSTICS               │                            ││
          │  │ argo version              │ Show client+server version ││
          │  │ argo auth token           │ Get auth token for UI/API  ││
          │  └───────────────────────────┴────────────────────────────┘│
          │                                                            │
          │  EXAM TIP: Memorize submit, list, get, logs, delete.      │
          │  These are the most common Workflow CLI exam tasks.       │
          └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
          ⚠️ GOTCHA — `argo stop` vs `argo terminate`, the real distinction
          These sound like synonyms and are NOT: `argo terminate <wf>`
          kills every running pod immediately and SKIPS any `onExit`
          handler (Ch 11.3). `argo stop <wf>` also halts progression, but
          lets the onExit handler run first — if easypj's pipeline has an
          onExit that pages the on-call or cleans up a scratch namespace
          (Ch 11.6), `terminate` silently skips that cleanup while `stop`
          honors it. Default to `stop` unless you need the pods gone
          instantly and don't care about cleanup.

          ⚠️ GOTCHA — `argo logs <wf> -c <container>` is NOT a step
          selector. Argo Workflows pods can contain MULTIPLE containers
          (your `main` container plus the emissary init/wait sidecar,
          Ch 9.3) — `-c` picks WHICH CONTAINER's logs to stream, most
          often `main` vs `wait`. To see one STEP's logs specifically,
          combine it with a node selector instead:
              argo logs easypj-migrate-abc123 -c main          # main container only
              argo logs easypj-migrate-abc123 --node-field-selector \
                displayName=run-migration                       # one STEP's logs

          ⚠️ GOTCHA — `argo resubmit` vs `argo retry`, don't confuse them
          `argo retry <wf>` reuses the SAME Workflow object and re-runs
          ONLY the nodes that failed — succeeded nodes are left alone,
          and any templateRef stays resolved to whatever was frozen at
          the ORIGINAL submit time (Ch 10 [G]'s storedWorkflowTemplateSpec).
          `argo resubmit <wf>` creates a BRAND NEW Workflow object from
          scratch, re-resolving every templateRef fresh — if a shared
          WorkflowTemplate changed since the original run, resubmit picks
          up the new version; retry does not.

          ⚡ SPEED TIP — `argo lint file.yaml` before every `argo submit`
          catches a `depends`/`dependencies` mix-up (Ch 10.3), a missing
          `entrypoint`, or a bad indent in seconds — cheaper than waiting
          for a real submit to fail.

    13.2  Argo Workflows UI
          - Workflow visualization (DAG view)
          - Logs and output inspection
          - Timeline view
          [SCREENSHOT GUIDE: Navigating the Workflows UI]
          ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
          │  ARGO WORKFLOWS UI — WIREFRAME                              │
          │                                                            │
          │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
          │  │ WORKFLOW LIST  (filter: namespace/label/phase)          │ │
          │  │ easypj-migrate-abc123   Succeeded   2m ago               │ │
          │  │ easypj-push-def456      🔵 Running   14s ago              │ │
          │  │ easypj-nightly-backup   Succeeded   14h ago               │ │
          │  └───────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────┘ │
          │                          │ click a row                      │
          │                          ▼                                  │
          │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
          │  │ [DAG/GRAPH]  [TIMELINE]  [YAML]                         │ │
          │  │  DAG view:                                               │ │
          │  │    clone ──▶ [unit-tests ‖ lint ‖ SAST] ──▶ build        │ │
          │  │              (spinner = still Running,                  │ │
          │  │               🔴 = Failed, click any node for its logs)  │ │
          │  │  TIMELINE view (same DAG, drawn by DURATION):            │ │
          │  │    clone     ▬▬                                          │ │
          │  │    unit-tests   ▬▬▬▬▬  (started same instant as →)       │ │
          │  │    lint         ▬▬                                       │ │
          │  │    SAST         ▬▬▬▬▬▬▬  ← longest of the 3, sets pace   │ │
          │  │    build              ▬▬▬  (waits for slowest sibling)   │ │
          │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
          └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
          🖥️ UI TOUR — what each view is actually FOR
          - Workflow list: filter by namespace/label/phase — the fastest
            way to find "did the 2am easypj-nightly-backup CronWorkflow
            run last night" without memorizing a generated name.
          - DAG/Graph view: visualizes exactly the fan-out/fan-in shape
            from Ch 10.3 — a stuck canary-shaped node with a spinner is
            usually the first thing you click when a pipeline hangs.
          - Timeline view: shows step DURATION side by side — this is
            where you'd actually SEE that 20.3's "6 min vs 12 min"
            parallelization claim is true, not just trust the math.
          - Logs panel (per-node): same content as `argo logs --node-
            field-selector`, but with live tailing and no CLI syntax to
            remember mid-incident.

          🏢 DAILY-WORK: `kubectl -n argo port-forward svc/argo-server
          2746:2746` + `argo auth token` (Ch 9.4) gets you into the UI
          from any laptop without a public Ingress — good enough for a
          dev/staging cluster; production would put Argo Server behind
          SSO (Ch 22, RBAC hardening).

    13.3  Debugging Failed Workflows
          - Common errors: image pull, resource limits, artifact access
          - Using argo logs and kubectl describe
          - Retry strategies
          - Pausing workflows for debugging
          [TROUBLESHOOTING GUIDE: Common failures and fixes]
          🩺 TOP 3 REAL FAILURE CLASSES — symptom → cause → fix
          ┌──────────────────┬───────────────────────┬──────────────────┐
          │ SYMPTOM           │ LIKELY CAUSE           │ FIX               │
          ├──────────────────┼───────────────────────┼──────────────────┤
          │ Pod stuck          │ Wrong image tag,       │ kubectl describe  │
          │ ContainerCreating/ │ registry auth missing, │ pod <name> — read │
          │ ImagePullBackOff   │ or private registry    │ the Events section │
          │                    │ w/o imagePullSecrets   │                    │
          │ Step Failed at 0s, │ Resource requests/     │ kubectl describe   │
          │ no logs at all     │ limits impossible to   │ pod — look for     │
          │                    │ schedule (OOMKilled or │ "Insufficient cpu/ │
          │                    │ node has no capacity)  │ memory" events     │
          │ outputs.artifacts  │ No artifact repository │ Configure          │
          │ step fails at the  │ configured (Ch 9.4 —   │ artifactRepository │
          │ VERY end, after    │ quick-start-minimal    │ (Ch 10.5) or       │
          │ the command        │ has none by default)   │ default ConfigMap  │
          │ succeeded          │                        │                    │
          └──────────────────┴───────────────────────┴──────────────────┘

          🔧 UNDER THE HOOD — why `argo logs` sometimes shows nothing
          A Workflow pod normally has 2 containers: `main` (your command)
          and the emissary `wait` sidecar (Ch 9.3) that handles artifact
          save/log capture. If `main` crashes before emissary attaches,
          `argo logs` (which reads through the Argo Server/API, itself
          reading emissary's captured output) can come back empty even
          though `kubectl logs <pod> -c main` shows the real error
          directly from the kubelet. When `argo logs` is unexpectedly
          silent, always fall back to `kubectl logs <pod> -c main
          --previous` as the ground truth.

          🧪 PAUSING FOR LIVE DEBUGGING
          Insert a temporary `suspend: {}` step (Ch 10.2) right before
          the failing step, submit, then once paused:
              kubectl exec -it <pod-name> -c main -- /bin/sh
          to poke around the EXACT environment the real step would run
          in, before `argo resume <wf>` continues past it. Faster than
          guessing from logs alone for a genuinely mysterious failure.


    [G] UNDER THE HOOD 🔧 — What `argo stop` Actually Does Differently
    ────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
    `argo terminate` sets every running node's phase straight to
    Failed/Stopped and issues a hard kill to their pods — the controller
    never evaluates `spec.onExit` at all. `argo stop` instead marks the
    Workflow's MAIN entrypoint as stopped, but leaves the controller free
    to still reconcile `onExit` as its own template invocation (exactly
    like a normal completion would trigger it, Ch 11.3) — so a
    Slack-notify or cleanup-namespace onExit handler still fires. This
    is the same "stored spec" mechanism from Ch 10 [G]: the controller
    doesn't need the original submission source again, because it's
    still reconciling against the SAME Workflow object's own stored
    status/spec.

    PROSE RECAP: terminate = pull the plug; stop = tell it to shut down
    gracefully. Same instinct as `kill -9` vs `kill -TERM` on a Unix
    process — one skips cleanup, one honors it.

    [H] KEY TAKEAWAYS 🔑
    ─────────────────────
    - `argo stop` runs onExit; `argo terminate` skips it — pick on
      purpose based on whether cleanup/notification matters.
    - `-c` on `argo logs` selects a CONTAINER (main/wait), not a step;
      use `--node-field-selector displayName=<step>` for a step.
    - `argo retry` reuses the same Workflow object and only re-runs
      failed nodes (templates stay frozen at original resolve time);
      `argo resubmit` creates a new object and re-resolves everything.
    - `argo lint` catches syntax errors before submit — cheaper than a
      failed real run.
    - When `argo logs` is unexpectedly empty, `kubectl logs <pod> -c
      main --previous` is the reliable fallback.
    🎯 EXAM RELEVANCE: exact command/flag recall questions are common in
       this domain — the stop/terminate and retry/resubmit pairs are
       classic exam distractors.

    [I] EXAM GOTCHAS ⚠️
    ─────────────────────
    1. Assuming `-c` on `argo logs` picks a step — it picks a container
       (main vs the emissary wait sidecar).
    2. Using `argo terminate` when you actually needed onExit cleanup to
       run — use `argo stop` instead.
    3. Assuming `argo retry` picks up a just-edited shared
       WorkflowTemplate — it doesn't; only `argo resubmit` re-resolves.
    4. Trusting an empty `argo logs` output as "no error occurred" —
       check `kubectl logs <pod> -c main --previous` before concluding
       that.
    5. Not running `argo lint` before a submit that touches a
       hand-edited DAG's depends/dependencies fields.

    [J] SPEED TIPS ⚡
    ───────────────────
    - `argo lint file.yaml` before every submit of hand-edited YAML.
    - `argo logs <wf> --node-field-selector displayName=<step> -c main`
      combined selector for exactly one step's real command output.
    - `kubectl -n argo port-forward svc/argo-server 2746:2746 &` once
      per terminal session, then leave it running while you work the UI.

    [K] COMMANDS TO REMEMBER 🔑
    ──────────────────────────────
      argo lint <file.yaml>
      argo stop <wf>          # runs onExit
      argo terminate <wf>     # skips onExit
      argo retry <wf>         # same object, failed nodes only
      argo resubmit <wf>      # new object, re-resolves templates
      argo logs <wf> -c main --node-field-selector displayName=<step>
      kubectl logs <pod> -c main --previous

    [L] PRACTICE DRILL 🧪 (~15 min, do-it-now)
    ────────────────────────────────────────────
      1. Submit a Workflow with an onExit Slack-notify handler; run
         `argo terminate` on it — confirm the notify does NOT fire.
      2. Resubmit the same Workflow, run `argo stop` instead — confirm
         the notify DOES fire this time.
      3. Break one step so it fails; `argo retry` and confirm only the
         failed node re-runs (succeeded nodes keep their old timestamps).
      4. Edit the underlying WorkflowTemplate, then `argo resubmit` (not
         retry) the same scenario and confirm the change is picked up.

    [M] LAB QUESTIONS 🧪 (10, exam-style)
    ───────────────────────────────────────
    Q1. easypj's CI Workflow has an onExit handler that pages on-call on
        failure. An engineer needs to kill a runaway Workflow but still
        wants the page to fire. Which command?
    A1. argo stop <wf>
    Explanation: stop still lets onExit reconcile; terminate skips it
    entirely.

    Q2. You want to see just the `main` container's output for the
        `run-migration` step specifically, not the whole Workflow's log
        stream.
    A2. argo logs <wf> -c main --node-field-selector
        displayName=run-migration
    Explanation: -c selects the CONTAINER; --node-field-selector selects
    the STEP — combine both for a precise view.

    Q3. A shared WorkflowTemplate `easypj-ci-cd` was just fixed. You want
        the SAME failed Workflow to pick up that fix, not just retry the
        old broken template. Which command?
    A3. argo resubmit <wf>
    Explanation: resubmit creates a new Workflow object and re-resolves
    templateRef fresh; retry reuses the old object's frozen
    storedWorkflowTemplateSpec (Ch 10 [G]).

    Q4. `argo logs easypj-migrate-abc123` returns nothing, even though
        the step visibly failed in the UI. What's the reliable fallback?
    A4. kubectl logs easypj-migrate-abc123-<node-pod-suffix> -c main
        --previous
    Explanation: argo logs depends on the emissary sidecar capturing
    output; if main crashed before that happened, kubectl logs against
    the kubelet directly is the ground truth.

    Q5. Which CLI command would have caught a `depends`/`dependencies`
        field mix-up (Ch 10.3) BEFORE a real submit attempt?
    A5. argo lint file.yaml
    Explanation: lint validates the manifest without creating a real
    Workflow object.

    Q6. True or False: `argo retry` and `argo resubmit` both leave the
        ORIGINAL Workflow object's succeeded nodes untouched.
    A6. False — only argo retry does. argo resubmit creates an entirely
        NEW Workflow object; there is no "original object" being
        preserved at all, just a fresh run using the same spec source.
    Explanation: retry mutates state on the existing object; resubmit
    is a completely separate object from the start.

    Q7. A step's pod is stuck in ImagePullBackOff. Where do you look
        first, and what does `argo logs` show for it?
    A7. `kubectl describe pod <pod>` (read the Events section for the
        pull error); `argo logs` typically shows nothing useful since
        the container never started to produce output.
    Explanation: image-pull failures happen before any container logic
    runs — Argo's log capture has nothing to show yet.

    Q8. You need to `kubectl exec` into the exact pod environment right
        before a specific step runs, to debug interactively. What's the
        fastest legitimate way using Workflow features you already know?
    A8. Insert a temporary `suspend: {}` step immediately before the
        target step, submit, then `kubectl exec -it <pod> -c main --
        /bin/sh` once paused, and `argo resume <wf>` when done.
    Explanation: reuses the Suspend template type (Ch 10.2) purely as a
    debugging aid.

    Q9. Which Workflows UI view would you check FIRST to confirm that
        20.3's "parallel tests run in 6 min, not 12 min" optimization
        claim actually held true on a real run?
    A9. The Timeline view — it shows each step's duration side by side,
        making overlapping (parallel) execution visually obvious.
    Explanation: the DAG/Graph view shows STRUCTURE; Timeline shows
    actual elapsed TIME per node.

    Q10. A colleague runs `argo terminate` expecting the CI pipeline's
         "update-gitops-repo" step to still run as cleanup since it's
         near the end anyway. Will it?
    A10. No — terminate kills every running node immediately regardless
         of position in the DAG/Steps, and does not evaluate onExit;
         "near the end" has no bearing on whether it gets a chance to run.
    Explanation: terminate is an unconditional hard stop, not a
    graceful drain toward completion.

    [N] VISUAL SUMMARY
    ────────────────────
    ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
    │  CHAPTER 13 IN ONE PICTURE                                   │
    │                                                                │
    │  SUBMIT/RUN          MONITOR              MANAGE              │
    │  submit/lint/        list/get/watch/       stop (+onExit) vs   │
    │  resubmit vs retry   logs -c <container>   terminate (no exit) │
    │  (resolve-time        + --node-field-       suspend/resume      │
    │   differs)            selector <step>                          │
    │        │                    │                    │              │
    │        └────────────────────┴────────────────────┘              │
    │                    UI: list/DAG/timeline/logs                   │
    │                    each answers a DIFFERENT question             │
    └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
    PROSE RECAP: Chapter 13 is muscle memory for everything Chapters
    9-12 built — and specifically the 4 pairs of commands that LOOK
    interchangeable and aren't (stop/terminate, retry/resubmit, -c
    container/step selector, argo logs/kubectl logs). Get these
    reflexive before Chapter 20's full pipeline, where a wrong command
    under real incident pressure costs real minutes.

PART 4: ARGO ROLLOUTS — PROGRESSIVE DELIVERY                              🟡
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
    🎯 EASYPJ FOCUS: Canary-deploy new jobpost features (like search
       or filtering) to a small percentage of users, with automatic
       rollback if error rates spike.

Chapter 14: Introduction to Progressive Delivery
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
    🟢🟡 BEGINNER/INTERMEDIATE · ⏱️ ~2 hours · 🎯 CAPA domain: Argo Rollouts 18% (Understand Argo Rollouts Fundamentals) · 🏢 Daily-Work: high

    [B] WHY THIS MATTERS
    ────────────────────
    Part 3 automated HOW easypj's code gets built and shipped. Part 4
    is about the moment right after that — the instant a new version
    actually starts serving real jobpost users — and why a plain
    Kubernetes Deployment handles that moment badly. A Deployment has
    exactly one lever: replace pods gradually or all at once, with zero
    concept of "watch and see" or "small slice first." This chapter
    builds the vocabulary (Blue-Green, Canary, progressive delivery)
    that the rest of Part 4 turns into real YAML against jobpost.

    ┌───────────────────────────────┬───────────────────────────────┐
    │ BEFORE CHAPTER 14              │ AFTER CHAPTER 14                │
    ├───────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────┤
    │ "Deployment does rolling       │ "Deployment's rolling update   │
    │ updates, that's progressive    │ is availability-safe, not      │
    │ enough"                        │ RISK-safe — no traffic split,  │
    │                                 │ no metric gate, no small-slice │
    │                                 │ preview"                        │
    │ Blue-Green/Canary = buzzwords  │ Two distinct, precisely         │
    │                                 │ defined strategies, each with  │
    │                                 │ real tradeoffs                  │
    │ "Auto-rollback" sounds built-in│ Know it requires an explicit    │
    │                                 │ AnalysisTemplate — nothing      │
    │                                 │ watches metrics for free        │
    └───────────────────────────────┴───────────────────────────────┘

    [C] LEARNING OBJECTIVES — 🎯 What you'll be able to DO
    ────────────────────────────────────────────────────
      - Explain concretely why a Deployment's RollingUpdate is
        availability-safe but not risk-safe for a user-facing app.
      - Define progressive delivery and place Blue-Green and Canary as
        two different strategies solving it, not two names for one idea.
      - State that "auto-rollback" and "auto-promote" both require an
        explicitly-attached AnalysisTemplate — neither is automatic
        just because canary steps exist.
      - Explain the pod-ratio-vs-exact-traffic distinction for canary
        weight, and when it matters.
      - Compare Rollout to Deployment field-by-field and justify when
        the extra CRD is worth it for easypj/jobpost.

    [D] CAPA RELEVANCE
    ───────────────────
    This chapter is the SOLE teaching ground for "Understand Argo
    Rollouts Fundamentals" — the foundational competency underpinning
    the entire 18%-weighted Rollouts domain. Every later Rollouts
    chapter (15's install, 16's hands-on strategies) assumes you
    already hold the conceptual distinctions taught here, especially
    the "nothing is automatic without explicit wiring" theme that
    recurs across autoPromotionEnabled, AnalysisTemplate, and
    trafficRouting.

    [E] SECTION-MAP DIAGRAM — you are here
    ────────────────────────────────────
    ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
    │  14.1 The Problem  ──> 14.2 What IS Progressive Delivery?     │
    │  (Deployment's                (the general concept both        │
    │   all-or-nothing risk)         strategies below implement)      │
    │        │                              │                        │
    │        │              ┌───────────────┴───────────────┐        │
    │        │              v                                v        │
    │        │       14.3 Blue-Green                  14.4 Canary     │
    │        │       (instant cutover,                  (gradual %,   │
    │        │        two full environments)             staged)      │
    │        │              │                                │        │
    │        └──────────────┴────────────────┬───────────────┘        │
    │                                         v                        │
    │                              14.5 Rollout vs Deployment           │
    │                              (when is the extra CRD worth it?)   │
    └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
    PROSE RECAP: 14.1-14.2 build the MOTIVATION, 14.3-14.4 are the two
    concrete STRATEGIES, 14.5 is the decision framework for choosing
    Rollout over Deployment in the first place. Chapter 15 turns all of
    this into an actual installed CRD.

    14.1  The Problem with Kubernetes Deployments
          - Standard Deployment: all-or-nothing rollout
          - Risk: A bad version affects ALL users instantly
          - No way to test with a small percentage first
          [PAIN POINT CARDS: Risks of standard deployments]
          ┌───────────────────┐  ┌───────────────────┐  ┌───────────────────┐
          │ 💥 ALL-OR-NOTHING  │  │ 👥 100% OF USERS    │  │ 🚫 NO SMALL-SLICE  │
          │                    │  │                    │  │    TEST            │
          │ jobpost search v2  │  │ A bug in v2 hits    │  │ Can't say "show    │
          │ replaces v1 pods    │  │ EVERY easypj visitor│  │ v2 to 10% first"   │
          │ as fast as the      │  │ the instant rolling │  │ — Deployment has   │
          │ rolling update      │  │ update finishes,    │  │ exactly one lever: │
          │ allows — no pause,  │  │ not a gradual few    │  │ replace pods       │
          │ no review window    │  │                    │  │ gradually or all    │
          │                    │  │                    │  │ at once             │
          └───────────────────┘  └───────────────────┘  └───────────────────┘
          🎯 Part 4 exists to put a dimmer switch and a pressure sensor on
          top of this exact all-or-nothing behavior — without replacing
          the Deployment machinery underneath (Ch 14.5).

    14.2  What is Progressive Delivery?
          - Gradually shift traffic to new version
          - Monitor for errors before full rollout
          - Automated rollback if problems detected
          [DIAGRAM: Progressive delivery — 10% → 25% → 50% → 100%]
          ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
          │  PROGRESSIVE DELIVERY — jobpost search v2, one slice at a   │
          │  time                                                       │
          │                                                            │
          │  10%    ██░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░  ⏱️ watch error rate            │
          │           │                                                 │
          │           ▼ pass                                            │
          │  25%    █████░░░░░░░░░░░░░░  ⏱️ watch latency + error rate  │
          │           │                                                 │
          │           ▼ pass                                            │
          │  50%    ██████████░░░░░░░░░  ⏱️ watch ALL metrics            │
          │           │                                                 │
          │           ▼ pass                                            │
          │  100%   ████████████████████  ✅ fully rolled out            │
          │                                                            │
          │  AT ANY STEP: metrics fail → AUTO-ROLLBACK to v1, 0% of      │
          │  the remaining users ever see the bad version                │
          └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    14.3  Blue-Green Deployments
          - Blue = current/stable, Green = new version
          - Instant cutover (switch service selector)
          - Instant rollback (switch back to Blue)
          [DIAGRAM: Blue-Green deployment flow]
          ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
          │  BLUE-GREEN DEPLOYMENT                                     │
          │                                                            │
          │  BEFORE:                     AFTER CUTOVER:                │
          │  ┌─────────┐                ┌─────────┐ inactive          │
          │  │ BLUE (v1)│◀── traffic    │ BLUE (v1)│                  │
          │  │ 3 pods  │                │ 3 pods  │                  │
          │  └─────────┘                └─────────┘                   │
          │  ┌─────────┐                ┌─────────┐ active            │
          │  │GREEN (v2)│ inactive      │GREEN (v2)│◀── traffic      │
          │  │ 3 pods  │                │ 3 pods  │                  │
          │  └─────────┘                └─────────┘                   │
          │                                                            │
          │  ⏱️ Cutover: ~1 second (swap selector label)               │
          │  ↩️ Rollback: Swap label back (instant)                    │
          └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
          ⚠️ GOTCHA — "instant cutover" isn't automatic by default
          The diagram's "swap selector label" framing makes Blue-Green
          sound like it always waits for a human. In Argo Rollouts, the
          field controlling this is `autoPromotionEnabled`, and it
          DEFAULTS TO TRUE — the moment Green's pods report Ready, the
          Rollout controller flips the active Service to Green on its
          own, with NO pause and NO analysis unless you explicitly set
          `autoPromotionEnabled: false` (optionally paired with
          `prePromotionAnalysis`, Ch 16.1). "Blue-Green gives you a safe
          preview window" is only true if you configure it to.

    14.4  Canary Deployments
          - Send small percentage to new version
          - Increase gradually while monitoring
          - Auto-rollback if error rate exceeds threshold
          [DIAGRAM: Canary deployment stages]
          ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
          │  CANARY DEPLOYMENT — Gradual Traffic Shift                  │
          │                                                            │
          │  TIME ──────────────────────────────────────────────▶      │
          │                                                            │
          │  STEP 1: 10% canary     STEP 2: 25% canary                │
          │  ┌─────────────────┐    ┌─────────────────┐               │
          │  │ ██░░░░░░░░░░░░░ │    │ █████░░░░░░░░░░ │               │
          │  │ v2 ░░░░░░░░v1░░│    │  v2  ░░░░░v1░░░ │               │
          │  └─────────────────┘    └─────────────────┘               │
          │  ⏱️ Wait 30s             ⏱️ Wait 2 min                     │
          │  ✅ Check error rate     ✅ Check latency                  │
          │                                                            │
          │  STEP 3: 50% canary     STEP 4: 100% rollout              │
          │  ┌─────────────────┐    ┌─────────────────┐               │
          │  │ ██████████░░░░░ │    │ ███████████████ │               │
          │  │    v2   ░░v1░░ │    │      v2 (all)   │               │
          │  └─────────────────┘    └─────────────────┘               │
          │  ⏱️ Wait 5 min            ✅ v2 fully deployed              │
          │  ✅ Check all metrics                                      │
          │                                                            │
          │  AT ANY STEP: If error rate > 5% → AUTO-ROLLBACK to v1    │
          │                                                            │
          │  easypj EXAMPLE:                                           │
          │  Deploy new jobpost search feature to 10% of users:        │
          │  • Monitor: error rate, response time, DB query latency   │
          │  • If all green → increase to 25% → 50% → 100%            │
          │  • If ANY red → instant rollback, 0% user impact!         │
          └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
          ⚠️ GOTCHA — "AUTO-ROLLBACK if error rate > 5%" doesn't happen
          for free. The diagram's rollback arrow is the OUTCOME of an
          AnalysisTemplate you must explicitly write and attach (Ch
          16.3) — Argo Rollouts does not watch metrics or compute an
          error-rate threshold on its own just because you defined
          canary steps. Without an attached AnalysisTemplate, a canary
          step with no `pause.duration` waits INDEFINITELY for a human
          to run `kubectl argo rollouts promote`, and a step WITH a
          `pause.duration` auto-ADVANCES after that timer regardless of
          real error rate — "monitoring" is something you wire in, not
          a built-in safety net.

          ⚠️ GOTCHA — canary weight is only exact WITH a traffic mesh.
          Without `trafficRouting` configured (Istio/NGINX/ALB/Gateway
          API, Ch 16.4), Argo Rollouts approximates a weight like "10%"
          using POD COUNT RATIO — e.g. 10% of 5 replicas rounds up to 1
          canary pod, which is really 20% of TRAFFIC if a plain Service
          load-balances evenly across all 5+1 pods, not a precise 10%.
          Exact percentage traffic control requires a real traffic-
          routing provider; pod-ratio canary is a "best effort"
          approximation, good enough for many teams but not the precise
          split the 10%/25%/50% diagram numbers might imply.

    14.5  Argo Rollouts vs Standard Deployments
          [COMPARISON TABLE: Features, traffic control, rollback speed, metrics]
          ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
          │  ARGO ROLLOUTS vs STANDARD DEPLOYMENT                       │
          │                                                            │
          │  ┌──────────────────┬──────────────────┬──────────────────┐│
          │  │ FEATURE           │ Deployment       │ Rollout          ││
          │  ├──────────────────┼──────────────────┼──────────────────┤│
          │  │ Update strategy   │ RollingUpdate or │ BlueGreen or     ││
          │  │                   │ Recreate ONLY    │ Canary ONLY —    ││
          │  │                   │                  │ no Recreate      ││
          │  │                   │                  │ equivalent       ││
          │  │ Traffic control   │ None — kube-proxy│ Pod-ratio (best  ││
          │  │                   │ round-robins      │ effort) or exact ││
          │  │                   │ across all Ready  │ via a mesh/      ││
          │  │                   │ pods equally      │ Ingress provider ││
          │  │ Metric-gated      │ ❌ None           │ ✅ AnalysisTemplate││
          │  │ promotion         │                  │ + AnalysisRun     ││
          │  │                   │                  │ (must be wired)   ││
          │  │ Rollback speed    │ kubectl rollout   │ kubectl argo      ││
          │  │                   │ undo — full        │ rollouts undo —  ││
          │  │                   │ ReplicaSet swap    │ can be INSTANT   ││
          │  │                   │                    │ (Blue-Green) or  ││
          │  │                   │                    │ auto (Analysis)  ││
          │  │ kubectl rollout    │ ✅ works           │ ❌ no-ops — must ││
          │  │ status/undo works?│                    │ use kubectl argo ││
          │  │                   │                    │ rollouts instead ││
          │  │ Pause for manual  │ ❌ No native pause │ ✅ pause: {} or   ││
          │  │ approval          │                    │ pause:{duration}││
          │  │ CRD or built-in?  │ Built into apps/v1 │ CRD              ││
          │  │                   │ (core K8s)         │ (argoproj.io)    ││
          │  └──────────────────┴──────────────────┴──────────────────┘│
          │                                                            │
          │  easypj VERDICT: Deployment is fine for internal tools with │
          │  no real user-impact risk (e.g. a batch worker). Rollout is │
          │  worth the extra CRD for jobpost's user-facing app, where a │
          │  bad release visible to 100% of visitors instantly is the   │
          │  exact risk Part 4 exists to eliminate.                     │
          └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘


    [G] UNDER THE HOOD 🔧 — Why "Nothing Is Automatic" Is the Chapter's Theme
    ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
    Three separate mechanisms in this chapter share one root cause for
    their gotchas: Argo Rollouts is a CONTROLLER that reconciles fields
    you set — exactly the same pattern taught since Ch 2's [G]. It has
    no opinion about safety unless you encode that opinion as a field:

        autoPromotionEnabled: false   <- without this, Blue-Green
                                          promotes the instant pods
                                          are Ready (default: true)
        analysis: {templates: [...]} <- without this, canary steps
                                          are just a timer, not a
                                          safety gate
        trafficRouting: {...}         <- without this, "10% canary"
                                          is a POD-COUNT approximation,
                                          not a guaranteed traffic %

    PROSE RECAP: every "but I thought that was automatic" moment in
    progressive delivery traces back to the same fact — Rollout is a
    reconciler, not a risk-management product. Safety is a YAML field
    you add, not a default behavior you get for free. Chapter 16 shows
    exactly how to add each one for easypj/jobpost.

    [H] KEY TAKEAWAYS 🔑
    ─────────────────────
    - Deployment's RollingUpdate is availability-safe (never zero pods)
      but not risk-safe (100% of users hit a bad version eventually).
    - Blue-Green = two full environments, instant/reversible cutover;
      Canary = gradual traffic shift on ONE environment, staged risk.
    - autoPromotionEnabled defaults to TRUE on Blue-Green — a real gate
      requires setting it false explicitly.
    - "Auto-rollback"/"auto-promote" require an attached
      AnalysisTemplate — canary steps alone are just a timed sequence.
    - Canary weight is a POD-RATIO approximation without a configured
      trafficRouting provider — exact percentages need a real mesh/
      Ingress integration.
    - kubectl rollout (native) does NOT work on Rollout objects — only
      kubectl argo rollouts does, since Rollout is a separate CRD.
    🎯 EXAM RELEVANCE: this chapter is the sole teaching ground for
       "Understand Argo Rollouts Fundamentals" — its distinctions are
       assumed knowledge for every later Rollouts question.

    [I] EXAM GOTCHAS ⚠️
    ─────────────────────
    1. Assuming Blue-Green always pauses for review — it promotes
       automatically unless autoPromotionEnabled: false is set.
    2. Assuming canary steps auto-rollback on bad metrics with no
       AnalysisTemplate attached — they don't; a pause just times out
       and advances regardless of real health.
    3. Reading "10% canary" as an exact traffic percentage without
       checking whether trafficRouting is configured — without it,
       it's a best-effort pod-count ratio.
    4. Running `kubectl rollout status/undo` on a Rollout object — it
       silently no-ops; use `kubectl argo rollouts` instead.
    5. Assuming Rollout replaces Recreate strategy — it only supports
       BlueGreen and Canary, nothing else.

    [J] SPEED TIPS ⚡
    ───────────────────
    - When reading ANY Rollout YAML on the exam, check for
      autoPromotionEnabled, analysis:, and trafficRouting: FIRST — their
      presence or absence changes the entire behavior story.
    - `kubectl explain rollout.spec.strategy.canary` (if the CRD's
      OpenAPI schema is installed) beats guessing field names from memory.

    [K] COMMANDS TO REMEMBER 🔑
    ──────────────────────────────
      kubectl explain rollout.spec.strategy.blueGreen
      kubectl explain rollout.spec.strategy.canary
      # Full CLI surface arrives in Ch 15-16 once the controller is
      # actually installed — this chapter is concepts-only by design.

    [L] PRACTICE DRILL 🧪 (~10 min, do-it-now)
    ─────────────────────────────────────────
      1. Sketch (on paper or a text file) easypj's search-ranking
         feature as a Blue-Green release: what are Blue and Green,
         which Service is active, what triggers cutover?
      2. Re-sketch the SAME feature as a Canary release instead: what
         are the weight steps, what would you monitor at each one?
      3. For each sketch, write down explicitly: is autoPromotion off?
         Is there an AnalysisTemplate? Is trafficRouting configured?
         If you can't answer all three, the sketch isn't finished.

    [M] LAB QUESTIONS 🧪 (10, exam-style)
    ───────────────────────────────────────
    Q1. A Rollout uses strategy.blueGreen with activeService/
        previewService set but no other blueGreen fields. QA plans to
        test Green via previewService before it goes live. What
        actually happens the moment Green's pods are Ready?
    A1. Green is automatically promoted to active — autoPromotionEnabled
        defaults to true, so there's no review window unless it's
        explicitly set to false.
    Explanation: this is the single most common Blue-Green
    misconception this chapter exists to correct.

    Q2. A canary has steps [setWeight:10, pause:{duration:5m},
        setWeight:100] with NO analysis: block anywhere. Error rate
        spikes to 40% during the 10% step. What happens?
    A2. Nothing rollback-related — the Rollout simply waits out the 5
        minute pause and then advances to setWeight:100 regardless of
        the real error rate, since nothing is measuring it.
    Explanation: "auto-rollback" requires an attached AnalysisTemplate;
    a bare pause is just a timer, not a safety check.

    Q3. easypj-rollout has 5 replicas and `setWeight: 10` with no
        trafficRouting configured. How many pods actually run the
        canary version, and is traffic split exactly 10/90?
    A3. ceil(5 x 0.10) = 1 canary pod. Traffic is NOT exactly 10/90 —
        without a mesh/Ingress provider, a plain Service load-balances
        roughly evenly across all 6 pods (1 canary + 5 stable), closer
        to ~17% canary traffic by pod count, not the labeled 10%.
    Explanation: pod-ratio canary is a best-effort approximation, not
    guaranteed traffic percentage.

    Q4. `kubectl rollout undo deployment/easypj` is run against an
        object that was actually converted to `kind: Rollout`. What
        happens?
    A4. It fails or no-ops — native `kubectl rollout` commands only
        understand Deployment/StatefulSet/DaemonSet, not the Rollout
        CRD.
    Explanation: use `kubectl argo rollouts undo` instead — a distinct
    CLI plugin for the distinct CRD.
    Q5. Compare rollback SPEED: a plain Deployment vs. a Blue-Green
        Rollout that has NOT yet auto-promoted.
    A5. Deployment rollback (kubectl rollout undo) triggers a full new
        RollingUpdate back to the prior ReplicaSet — takes real time.
        A not-yet-promoted Blue-Green Rollout's "rollback" is simply
        never flipping the active Service — effectively already
        instant/zero-cost since Blue never stopped serving.
    Explanation: this is Blue-Green's core safety property — the
    rollback window exists BEFORE promotion, not after.

    Q6. Which strategy — Blue-Green or Canary — requires roughly 2x the
        compute resources during a release, and why?
    A6. Blue-Green — both Blue and Green run their full replica count
        simultaneously until cutover (and often for a preview window
        after), unlike Canary which only runs a small extra slice.
    Explanation: a real cost/tradeoff, not just a technical one — worth
    weighing against Blue-Green's instant-rollback benefit.

    Q7. Does Argo Rollouts support a Recreate-equivalent strategy (kill
        all old pods, then start all new ones)?
    A7. No — only strategy.blueGreen and strategy.canary exist; there is
        no Recreate strategy on the Rollout CRD.
    Explanation: a workload genuinely needing Recreate semantics (e.g.
    exclusive-lock singleton apps) should stay a plain Deployment.

    Q8. easypj's Rollout has `analysis: {templates: [...]}` attached
        at the canary's 25% step, with a Prometheus provider watching
        error rate. Where does that AnalysisTemplate come from — is it
        built into Argo Rollouts?
    A8. No — it's a separate object (AnalysisTemplate/
        ClusterAnalysisTemplate, Ch 16.3) that YOU author and reference
        by name; Argo Rollouts ships the MECHANISM to attach and
        evaluate it, not any pre-built metric logic.
    Explanation: reinforces the "nothing is automatic without explicit
    wiring" theme — even the analysis LOGIC is user-authored.

    Q9. For an internal batch-processing worker with zero real-time
        user traffic, does 14.5's comparison table suggest Rollout or
        plain Deployment?
    A9. Plain Deployment — the extra CRD's value (traffic-split safety,
        metric-gated promotion) is aimed at user-facing risk that a
        purely internal batch worker with no live traffic doesn't have.
    Explanation: matches 14.5's easypj verdict — Rollout is deliberately
    chosen where it earns its complexity, not applied everywhere by
    default.

    Q10. True or False: converting a Deployment to a Rollout changes
         zero runtime behavior until you explicitly add a
         strategy.blueGreen or strategy.canary block.
    A10. True (practically) — a Rollout with no strategy specified
         behaves like a basic rolling update; the progressive-delivery
         behavior only activates once you configure blueGreen/canary
         fields (Ch 15.4 covers the actual conversion mechanics).
    Explanation: reinforces that Rollout is a strict superset of
    Deployment's capability, not a different default behavior.

    [N] VISUAL SUMMARY
    ────────────────────
    ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
    │  CHAPTER 14 IN ONE PICTURE                                    │
    │                                                                │
    │  Deployment: ALL pods replaced, ALL users affected, NO gate   │
    │        │                                                      │
    │        v  Argo Rollouts adds a GATE — but you must BUILD it   │
    │                                                                │
    │  Blue-Green: 2 full envs      Canary: 1 env, gradual %        │
    │  instant cutover (if you      staged (if you attach an        │
    │  set autoPromotionEnabled:    AnalysisTemplate — else it's    │
    │  false first)                  just a timer)                   │
    │        │                              │                        │
    │        └──────────────┬───────────────┘                        │
    │                       v                                        │
    │            kubectl argo rollouts, NOT kubectl rollout          │
    └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
    PROSE RECAP: Chapter 14's real lesson is a mindset, not a diagram —
    every safety property progressive delivery promises (a review
    window, a metric gate, an exact traffic split) is a field you set,
    not a default you inherit. Chapter 15 installs the CRD that makes
    setting those fields possible; Chapter 16 sets them for real.


Chapter 15: Argo Rollouts — Installation and Core Concepts
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
    🟡 INTERMEDIATE · ⏱️ ~2.5 hours · 🎯 CAPA domain: Rollouts 18% · 🏢 Daily-Work: high

    [B] WHY THIS MATTERS
    ────────────────────
    easypj's jobpost app just shipped a search-ranking change straight to
    production last chapter using a plain Deployment — and it went to
    100% of users the instant the rollout finished, with no way to test
    on a slice of traffic and no automatic safety net if the new ranking
    algorithm turned out to be buggy. Argo Rollouts exists to close that
    gap: it swaps in for the Deployment you already know, then adds a
    pressure sensor (metric analysis) and a dimmer switch (gradual
    traffic shifting) on top of the exact same Pod-management machinery.

    BEFORE / AFTER:
    ┌────────────────────────────────┬────────────────────────────────┐
    │ BEFORE (Deployment)             │ AFTER (Rollout)                 │
    ├────────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────┤
    │ kubectl apply -f deploy.yaml    │ kubectl apply -f rollout.yaml   │
    │ 100% traffic to v2, instantly   │ 10% traffic to v2 -> pause ->   │
    │ NO automatic rollback           │ AnalysisRun checks error rate ->│
    │ Only fix: manual undo           │ auto-promote OR auto-rollback   │
    └────────────────────────────────┴────────────────────────────────┘

    [C] LEARNING OBJECTIVES — 🎯 What you'll be able to DO
    ────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
      - Explain what problem Argo Rollouts solves that a plain
        Deployment cannot.
      - Install the Argo Rollouts controller and kubectl plugin, and
        verify both are working.
      - Read and write a Rollout manifest, distinguishing blueGreen
        fields from canary fields.
      - Safely migrate an existing easypj Deployment to a Rollout
        without downtime — including knowing which commands (kubectl
        rollout vs kubectl argo rollouts) work on which resource type.
      - State the real caveats behind "drop-in replacement" (Recreate
        strategy, live-migration risk, kubectl compatibility).

    [D] CAPA RELEVANCE
    ───────────────────
    Maps to the official CAPA "Understand Argo Rollouts Fundamentals"
    competency (Argo Rollouts domain, 18% of the exam). Specifically
    tested: identifying the Rollout CRD and its relationship to
    Deployment; installation and CLI plugin usage; reading/writing
    strategy.blueGreen and strategy.canary spec fields; the mechanics of
    converting a Deployment to a Rollout.

    15.1  What is Argo Rollouts?
          - Kubernetes controller and CRD that replaces Deployment
          - Adds blue-green and canary strategies
          - Integrates with service meshes and ingress controllers
          - Metric-driven promotion and rollback
          ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
          │  ROLLOUT = DEPLOYMENT + A STRATEGY FIELD                    │
          │                                                            │
          │  apps/v1 Deployment          argoproj.io/v1alpha1 Rollout  │
          │  ┌─────────────────┐        ┌─────────────────┐          │
          │  │ replicas: 5      │   =    │ replicas: 5      │          │
          │  │ selector: {...}  │  SAME  │ selector: {...}  │          │
          │  │ template: {...}  │        │ template: {...}  │          │
          │  │                  │        │ strategy:         │ ◀── the │
          │  │ strategy:        │   ≠    │   canary: {...}   │   ONLY  │
          │  │   RollingUpdate  │        │   OR blueGreen:{} │   real  │
          │  │   (only option)  │        │                    │   diff  │
          │  └─────────────────┘        └─────────────────┘          │
          │                                                            │
          │  🎯 Nothing else changes — Pods, ReplicaSets, Services all  │
          │  work identically. The strategy field is WHY Chapter 15.4   │
          │  can convert one to the other with a handful of YAML lines. │
          └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    15.2  Installing Argo Rollouts
          [CODE BLOCK: Install commands]
          ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
          │  kubectl create namespace argo-rollouts                     │
          │  kubectl apply -n argo-rollouts -f \                        │
          │    https://github.com/argoproj/argo-rollouts/releases/      │
          │    latest/download/install.yaml                             │
          │                                                              │
          │  # Verify:                                                  │
          │  kubectl get pods -n argo-rollouts                          │
          └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
          - Verifying installation
          - Argo Rollouts kubectl plugin (kubectl argo rollouts)
          ⚠️ GOTCHA — the kubectl-argo-rollouts plugin has NO official
             Krew-index entry (an open, unresolved feature request) and
             NO official native Windows binary — only macOS (`brew
             install argoproj/tap/kubectl-argo-rollouts`) and Linux (curl
             the release binary, chmod +x, move to PATH) are officially
             published. Windows/Docker-Desktop learners should use WSL.
             Verify with `kubectl argo rollouts version` — check the
             plugin and controller versions track together.

    15.3  The Rollout CRD — Deep Dive
          - Rollout spec: replicas, selector, template (same as Deployment!)
          - strategy: blueGreen or canary
          - BlueGreen strategy fields: activeService, previewService, autoPromotion
          - Canary strategy fields: steps, analysis, trafficRouting
          [YAML ANATOMY: Rollout manifest with every field annotated]
          ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
          │  ANATOMY OF AN ARGO ROLLOUT                                │
          │                                                            │
          │  apiVersion: argoproj.io/v1alpha1                          │
          │  kind: Rollout                     ← Replaces Deployment!  │
          │  metadata:                                                 │
          │    name: easypj-rollout                                    │
          │  spec:                                                     │
          │    replicas: 5                      ← Same as Deployment   │
          │    selector:                        ← Same as Deployment   │
          │      matchLabels:                                          │
          │        app: easypj                                         │
          │    template:                         ← Same as Deployment   │
          │      metadata:                                             │
          │        labels:                                             │
          │          app: easypj                                       │
          │      spec:                                                 │
          │        containers:                                         │
          │          - name: easypj                                    │
          │            image: registry.io/easypj:v2.0                 │
          │    strategy:                        ← THE ARGO MAGIC!      │
          │      canary:                        ← or blueGreen        │
          │        steps:                       ← Gradual traffic steps│
          │          - setWeight: 10            ← 10% to new version   │
          │          - pause: {duration: 30s}   ← Wait 30 seconds      │
          │          - setWeight: 25            ← 25% to new version   │
          │          - pause: {duration: 2m}    ← Wait 2 minutes       │
          │          - setWeight: 50            ← 50% to new version   │
          │          - pause: {duration: 5m}    ← Wait 5 minutes       │
          │  ────────────────────────────────────────────────────────  │
          │  BLUE-GREEN VARIANT:                                       │
          │    strategy:                                               │
          │      blueGreen:                                            │
          │        activeService: easypj-active    ← Main service      │
          │        previewService: easypj-preview  ← Preview service   │
          │        autoPromotionEnabled: false     ← Manual promote    │
          │        prePromotionAnalysis:           ← Pre-switch tests  │
          │          templates:                                        │
          │            - templateName: smoke-test                      │
          │  ────────────────────────────────────────────────────────  │
          │  CANARY WITH ANALYSIS:                                     │
          │    strategy:                                               │
          │      canary:                                               │
          │        steps:                                              │
          │          - setWeight: 10                                   │
          │          - analysis:                     ← Metric check!   │
          │              templates:                                    │
          │                - templateName: error-rate-check            │
          │          - setWeight: 100                                  │
          │                                                            │
          │  KEY DIFFERENCE from Deployment:                           │
          │  Deployment = all-or-nothing rollout                       │
          │  Rollout = controlled, gradual, metric-verified rollout    │
          └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    15.4  Converting Deployments to Rollouts
          - There is NO `kubectl argo rollouts convert` command — it does
            not exist in the CLI (verified: 17 real subcommands, no
            "convert"). The real, documented process is a manual 3-field
            edit: apiVersion apps/v1 -> argoproj.io/v1alpha1, kind
            Deployment -> Rollout, add a strategy block (blueGreen or
            canary) — metadata/selector/template/containers stay identical
          - Alternative (recommended for live traffic): reference the
            EXISTING, untouched Deployment via workloadRef instead of
            rewriting it at all (scaleDown: never/onsuccess/progressively)
          - What changes and what stays the same
          - Rollout is a drop-in replacement for Deployment for MOST
            purposes, with 3 real caveats: (1) only Blue-Green/Canary
            strategies are supported — NOT Recreate; (2) converting a
            Deployment already serving LIVE traffic in place risks
            downtime — official guidance says run the Rollout alongside
            the Deployment first, then scale down/delete the Deployment
            only once the Rollout is confirmed healthy; (3) Kubernetes'
            built-in `kubectl rollout status/history/undo/pause/resume/
            restart` commands do NOT work on the Rollout CRD — you must
            use the separate `kubectl argo rollouts <verb>` plugin
          [CODE EXAMPLE: Before/after conversion]


    [G] UNDER THE HOOD 🔧 — HPA + Argo Rollouts
    ────────────────────────────────────────────
    Rollout exposes a /scale subresource (autoscaling/v2, K8s 1.23+)
    exactly like Deployment, so a standard HorizontalPodAutoscaler with
    scaleTargetRef.kind: Rollout works out of the box — HPA writes
    desired spec.replicas, the controller reconciles ReplicaSets.

    BlueGreen + HPA: both active and preview ReplicaSets scale in
    lock-step until promotion (doubles cost while both are live) —
    previewReplicaCount can pin preview to save cost.

    Canary + HPA WITHOUT a traffic mesh: pod ratio approximates the
    weight — canary_replicas = ceil(desired x weight/100), stable_
    replicas = desired - canary_replicas. On abort/rollback, stable
    pods do NOT automatically scale back up — must be done manually.

    Canary + HPA WITH a traffic mesh: traffic weight is decoupled from
    pod count via the mesh, BUT a single shared HPA has no scaling
    isolation — a leaky/CPU-spiking canary pod inflates the average
    metric and scales the WHOLE fleet up. setCanaryScale pins the
    canary pod count so only stable pods respond to HPA, restoring
    isolation.

      HPA --writes spec.replicas--> Rollout --splits by weight-->
        stable ReplicaSet
        canary ReplicaSet
      (no trafficRouting: pod COUNT ratio = traffic ratio)
      (with trafficRouting: mesh weight != pod count; shared HPA metric
       can still over-scale from a sick canary unless setCanaryScale
       pins it)

    [H] KEY TAKEAWAYS 🔑
    ─────────────────────
    - Rollout = same replicas/selector/template as Deployment, PLUS a
      strategy block that's either blueGreen or canary.
    - Install = create namespace + apply the official install.yaml
      release URL; CLI plugin is a separate download (macOS/Linux only).
    - There is no `kubectl argo rollouts convert` command — conversion
      is a manual 3-field YAML edit, or better, a workloadRef reference
      to the untouched Deployment.
    🎯 CAPA relevance: expect direct questions on field names
       (activeService vs canaryService/stableService), on which CLI
       namespace a given command belongs to, and on safe-migration
       sequencing.

    [I] EXAM GOTCHAS ⚠️
    ─────────────────────
    1. `kubectl argo rollouts convert` does not exist — don't write it
       on the exam.
    2. `kubectl rollout undo/status/restart` do not work on a Rollout
       object — only Deployment/StatefulSet/DaemonSet. Use `kubectl argo
       rollouts undo/status/restart` instead.
    3. Rollout supports only Blue-Green and Canary — not Recreate.
    4. Migrating a live-traffic Deployment by editing it in place risks
       downtime — run the Rollout alongside it first.
    5. Under HPA + canary without a traffic mesh, an aborted rollout
       leaves stable under-scaled — manual restore required.
    6. A single HPA shared across canary and stable pods has no scaling
       isolation unless setCanaryScale is set.

    [J] SPEED TIPS ⚡
    ───────────────────
    - `kubectl argo rollouts lint -f rollout.yaml` — validate before
      apply, faster than fixing a failed apply mid-lab.
    - `kubectl argo rollouts get rollout easypj-rollout -w` — live-
      updating view of steps/weights/analysis instead of polling.
    - `kubectl argo rollouts version` right after install — cheapest
      sanity check that plugin and controller are compatible.

    [K] COMMANDS TO REMEMBER 🔑
    ──────────────────────────────
      kubectl create namespace argo-rollouts
      kubectl apply -n argo-rollouts -f https://github.com/argoproj/
        argo-rollouts/releases/latest/download/install.yaml
      kubectl argo rollouts version
      kubectl argo rollouts lint -f rollout.yaml
      kubectl argo rollouts get rollout easypj-rollout -w
      kubectl argo rollouts list rollouts

    [L] PRACTICE DRILL 🧪 (do-it-now, ~10 min)
    ─────────────────────────────────────────
    1. Install the controller into a scratch cluster using the two
       commands above.
    2. Confirm `kubectl get pods -n argo-rollouts` shows the controller
       Running.
    3. Hand-edit your existing easypj Deployment YAML into a Rollout
       (apiVersion/kind/add a minimal canary.steps with one setWeight:
       20), and `kubectl argo rollouts lint -f` it before applying.
    4. Apply it, then `kubectl argo rollouts get rollout easypj-rollout
       -w` and observe it pause at 20%.

    [M] LAB QUESTIONS 🧪 (10, easypj/jobpost-specific)
    ────────────────────────────────────────
    Q1. You run `kubectl argo rollouts convert deploy/easypj-rollout`
        expecting a Rollout manifest, but the CLI returns "unknown
        command." What's wrong, and the correct approach?
    A1. The convert subcommand does not exist. Manually change
        apiVersion/kind and add a strategy block, or use workloadRef.
    Explanation: the real plugin ships 17 subcommands — no conversion
    automation exists.

    Q2. Your team wants zero-downtime migration of the live, traffic-
        serving easypj Deployment to a Rollout. Describe the safe
        sequence.
    A2. Deploy a new Rollout alongside the existing Deployment; verify
        healthy; only then scale down/delete the original.
    Explanation: official guidance warns editing a live-traffic
    Deployment in place "might result in downtime."

    Q3. After converting, a teammate runs `kubectl rollout undo
        rollout/easypj-rollout`. Does this work?
    A3. No — kubectl rollout is wired only to Deployment/StatefulSet/
        DaemonSet. Use `kubectl argo rollouts undo easypj-rollout`.
    Explanation: Rollout is a CRD; native kubectl rollout doesn't
    operate on CRDs.

    Q4. Give the exact two-command sequence to install the Argo
        Rollouts controller.
    A4. kubectl create namespace argo-rollouts; kubectl apply -n
        argo-rollouts -f <install.yaml release URL>.
    Explanation: the controller must live in its own namespace before
    any Rollout can be reconciled.

    Q5. A Windows teammate wants to `kubectl krew install argo-
        rollouts`. Will this work?
    A5. No — no official Krew entry, and no official Windows binary;
        only macOS (Homebrew) and Linux (curl) are officially published.
    Explanation: a documented exception to the "every popular plugin is
    on Krew" assumption.

    Q6. blueGreen.activeService/previewService are set but
        autoPromotionEnabled isn't. Default behavior?
    A6. Defaults to true — automatically promotes once the new
        ReplicaSet is healthy, no manual approval wait.
    Explanation: only autoPromotionEnabled: false forces manual
    promotion.

    Q7. Canary with setWeight steps only (no trafficRouting), HPA
        desired=8, at a 25% weight step. Canary vs stable pod count?
    A7. canary = ceil(8 x 0.25) = 2; stable = 6.
    Explanation: without a mesh, Rollouts approximates traffic % using
    pod-count ratio.

    Q8. In that scenario, the canary step fails and the Rollout aborts.
        What must the on-call engineer do differently than with
        trafficRouting configured?
    A8. Manually verify/restore stable's full desired capacity — pod-
        ratio canary doesn't auto-restore stable's scale after abort.
    Explanation: documented limitation of the no-mesh pod-ratio
    approach.

    Q9. Canary uses trafficRouting.nginx with a single shared HPA. The
        canary pod develops a memory leak. What happens to stable pods,
        and how do you prevent it?
    A9. The whole fleet scales up since HPA averages the metric across
        both. Prevent with setCanaryScale to pin canary replica count.
    Explanation: "lack of scaling isolation" caveat for canary+HPA+
    shared-metric setups.

    Q10. True or False: any Deployment YAML, including one using
         strategy: Recreate, can be re-kinded to Rollout with no other
         changes.
    A10. False.
    Explanation: Rollout supports only blueGreen and canary — Recreate
    has no equivalent; its strategy must be redesigned.

    [N] VISUAL SUMMARY
    ────────────────────
    easypj Deployment --(add strategy field, keep replicas/selector/
    template)--> easypj Rollout
      kubectl rollout ... (native, apps/v1 only)
      kubectl argo rollouts ... (plugin, CRD-aware)
        v                                    v
      all-or-nothing update    gradual, metric-verified, HPA-aware
                                update (canary pod-ratio OR mesh-
                                decoupled; watch isolation + abort-
                                restore gotchas)
    PROSE RECAP: Chapter 15 established that a Rollout is structurally a
    Deployment plus a strategy block, installed via two kubectl commands
    and operated by a SEPARATE CLI plugin whose verbs don't overlap with
    native kubectl rollout. Converting is a manual, careful process —
    not a single command — and HPA continues to work, but its
    interaction with canary/blue-green pod ratios has two concrete
    gotchas (manual restore after abort; scaling isolation) that Chapter
    16 builds directly on top of.


Chapter 16: Argo Rollouts for easypj — Progressive Delivery in Action
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
    🟡 INTERMEDIATE · ⏱️ ~3.5 hours · 🎯 CAPA domain: Argo Rollouts 18% (Use Common Progressive Rollout Strategies + Describe Analysis Template and AnalysisRun) · 🏢 Daily-Work: very high

    [B] WHY THIS MATTERS
    ────────────────────
    Chapter 15 gave you the Rollout CRD in the abstract — fields on a
    page. This chapter is where it becomes real: you point Argo Rollouts
    at jobpost, and instead of a Deployment dumping a new version on
    every user at once, you get to CHOOSE how brave to be — an instant,
    reversible Blue-Green cutover for a low-risk config change, or a
    slow, metric-gated Canary for the new jobpost search feature that
    genuinely might break something. This is also where the second half
    of the Rollouts exam domain lives: an AnalysisTemplate isn't a
    diagram, it's a real object you write, and an AnalysisRun isn't a
    concept, it's a thing you'll `kubectl describe` at 2am when a canary
    won't promote. By the end, easypj/jobpost ships changes the way a
    real production job board would — gradually, watched, and with an
    automatic escape hatch.

    ┌───────────────────────────────┬───────────────────────────────┐
    │ BEFORE CHAPTER 16              │ AFTER CHAPTER 16                │
    ├───────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────┤
    │ Rollout CRD understood in      │ jobpost actually blue-green'd  │
    │ theory only (Ch15)             │ AND canary'd, both for real     │
    │ "Promote/rollback" = 2 words   │ promote/undo/abort/retry are   │
    │ from a diagram                 │ muscle-memory CLI commands      │
    │ AnalysisTemplate = a field     │ You write a Prometheus-backed  │
    │ list you memorized             │ AnalysisTemplate that actually │
    │                                 │ gates or kills a rollout        │
    │ "Which mesh?" is an unknown    │ You can pick Istio/NGINX/ALB/  │
    │                                 │ Gateway API for easypj's stack │
    │                                 │ and explain why NOT SMI in 2026│
    │ Dashboard/CLI monitoring =     │ You can diagnose a stuck        │
    │ unfamiliar                     │ Paused rollout in under a minute│
    └───────────────────────────────┴───────────────────────────────┘

    [C] LEARNING OBJECTIVES — 🎯 By the end of this chapter you can:
    ────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
      1. Configure and execute a full Blue-Green release of jobpost —
         activeService/previewService, a prePromotionAnalysis smoke-test
         gate, promote, and instant rollback via undo.
      2. Write a multi-step Canary strategy (setWeight/pause) for
         jobpost, sized correctly against replica count (maxSurge/
         maxUnavailable), and avoid the "no final setWeight:100" trap.
      3. Write an AnalysisTemplate with a Prometheus provider — correct
         successCondition/failureCondition syntax (including the
         result[0] vector gotcha) — and explain the Failure vs Error vs
         Inconclusive outcome model tested on CAPA.
      4. Attach analysis to a canary two ways (inline step-blocking vs
         background) and know when each is the right tool.
      5. Choose a correct, CURRENT traffic-routing provider for easypj's
         infrastructure (Istio/NGINX/ALB/Gateway API) and explain why
         SMI is a legacy choice in 2026, not the default.
      6. Monitor a live Rollout via CLI and dashboard, and run a
         symptom → cause → fix diagnosis when one gets stuck.

    This chapter is the APPLIED half of the Rollouts domain (18% of
    the exam). Chapter 15 covered "Understand Rollouts Fundamentals";
    16.1/16.2/16.4 map directly to the official competency "Use Common
    Progressive Rollout Strategies" (Blue-Green, Canary, and the
    traffic-routing mechanisms that make fine-grained weights possible

    questions get concrete (exact YAML fields, exact outcome states).

    one question describing a stuck/paused Rollout and asking you to
    identify the cause from symptoms.
