    PROSE RECAP: 16.1-16.2 are the two STRATEGIES (all-or-nothing
    cutover vs staged percentages). 16.3 is the BRAIN that can drive
    either strategy's decisions from real metrics instead of a human
    watching a dashboard. 16.4 is the PLUMBING that makes "10% traffic"
    a real, enforceable number rather than a comment in a YAML file.
    16.5 closes the loop — how you actually watch and intervene in all
    of the above once it's running against jobpost.

    16.1  Blue-Green Deployment for easypj
          - Creating activeService and previewService
          - Deploying a new version to Green
          - Promoting: kubectl argo rollouts promote easypj
          - Rollback: kubectl argo rollouts undo easypj
          [COMPLETE YAML + CLI: Full blue-green workflow]
          ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
          │  BLUE-GREEN FOR easypj — BEFORE / DURING / AFTER PROMOTE     │
          │                                                            │
          │  BEFORE:                    DURING (previewService only):   │
          │  easypj-active ──▶ jobpost   easypj-active ──▶ jobpost      │
          │                     v1 (5)                      v1 (5)      │
          │  easypj-preview ──▶ (none)   easypj-preview ──▶ jobpost      │
          │                                                  v2 (5)      │
          │                              QA hits easypj-preview to test │
          │                              v2 — real users still see v1   │
          │                                                            │
          │  AFTER `kubectl argo rollouts promote easypj`:               │
          │  easypj-active ──▶ jobpost v2 (5)   ◀── traffic swap, ~1s   │
          │  easypj-preview ──▶ jobpost v1 (5)   ◀── old version kept    │
          │                                          (instant rollback)  │
          └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
          ⚠️ GOTCHA — autoPromotionEnabled DEFAULTS TO true
          The moment jobpost-green's ReplicaSet becomes Ready, Argo Rollouts
          flips activeService to it AUTOMATICALLY — there is no gate unless you
          set it yourself:
              strategy:
                blueGreen:
                  activeService: easypj-active
                  previewService: easypj-preview
                  autoPromotionEnabled: false   <- REQUIRED for a real QA gate
          Forget this line and your "smoke-test Green via previewService first"
          plan never happens — jobpost-green is live to real users the instant
          its pods report Ready, whether or not anyone looked at it.

          🏢 DAILY-WORK — Gate the promote on a real health check
          strategy:
            blueGreen:
              activeService: easypj-active
              previewService: easypj-preview
              autoPromotionEnabled: false
              prePromotionAnalysis:
                templates:
                  - templateName: jobpost-smoke-test
                args:
                  - name: preview-service
                    value: easypj-preview
          In production you don't eyeball Green — jobpost-smoke-test is an
          AnalysisTemplate whose provider.web hits
          http://easypj-preview/jobpost/health/ and asserts a 200 + a DB-
          connectivity check BEFORE `kubectl argo rollouts promote easypj` is
          even allowed to matter (autoPromotionEnabled:false still requires
          someone/something to call promote — pairing it with
          prePromotionAnalysis means promote only succeeds if the smoke test
          already passed).

          ⚡ SPEED TIP
          kubectl argo rollouts promote easypj --full skips prePromotionAnalysis
          entirely and force-promotes NOW. Reach for it in a genuine incident
          ("Green is fine, the smoke test is just slow/flaky, ship it") — never
          as a habit, since it bypasses the exact safety gate you configured.

    16.2  Canary Deployment for easypj
          - Step 1: 10% traffic to new version, pause
          - Step 2: 25% traffic, pause for 5 minutes
          - Step 3: 50% traffic, pause
          - Step 4: 100% traffic (full rollout)
          [COMPLETE YAML: Canary rollout with steps]
          📖 KEY TERM — maxSurge / maxUnavailable during a canary step
          Defaults: maxSurge=25%, maxUnavailable=25%. Applied to easypj-rollout
          (replicas: 5) at Step 1 (setWeight: 10):
            ┌───────────────────┬──────────────────────────────────────────┐
            │ Target canary pods │ ceil(5 x 10%) = 1 pod running v2          │
            │ maxSurge allows    │ up to 1 extra pod (25% of 5, rounded)     │
            │ maxUnavailable     │ up to 1 stable pod may be down meanwhile  │
            └───────────────────┴──────────────────────────────────────────┘
          This is WHY a 5-replica easypj-rollout can briefly show 6 pods total
          during a canary step — it's surging a new one in, not always killing
          an old one first.

          ⚠️ GOTCHA — Canary steps that don't end at setWeight: 100
              steps:
                - setWeight: 10
                - pause: {duration: 30s}
                - setWeight: 25
                - pause: {duration: 5m}
                - setWeight: 50
                - pause: {duration: 5m}
                ❌ (no final setWeight: 100 step)
          Once the last listed step finishes, easypj-rollout is marked Healthy
          — but a traffic-routing provider (Istio/NGINX/ALB) is only guaranteed
          a "best effort" split at that LAST weight. Result: jobpost can sit
          stuck splitting 50/50 between v1 and v2 forever even though
          `kubectl argo rollouts get rollout` says everything's fine. ALWAYS
          end canary steps with an explicit `- setWeight: 100`.

          ⚡ SPEED TIP
          kubectl argo rollouts set image easypj-rollout easypj=registry.io/easypj:v2.1
          starts a new canary run without hand-editing YAML — the CLI equivalent
          of bumping spec.template.spec.containers[].image. Follow it with
          kubectl argo rollouts status easypj-rollout --watch to babysit it live.

    16.3  Metric-Driven Promotion with Analysis
          - AnalysisTemplate and AnalysisRun
          - Monitoring error rate, latency, CPU during rollout
          - Auto-promote if metrics pass, auto-rollback if they fail
          [DIAGRAM: Analysis-driven canary workflow]
          ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
          │  METRIC-DRIVEN CANARY — Auto-Promote or Auto-Rollback      │
          │                                                            │
          │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
          │  │  AnalysisTemplate: "error-rate-check"                │ │
          │  │                                                      │ │
          │  │  metrics:                                            │ │
          │  │    - name: error-rate                                │ │
          │  │      interval: 30s        ← Check every 30 seconds   │ │
          │  │      count: 10            ← Run 10 times (5 min)     │ │
          │  │      successCondition:    ← GREEN if true            │ │
          │  │        result < 0.01     ← Less than 1% errors      │ │
          │  │      failureCondition:    ← RED if true              │ │
          │  │        result >= 0.05    ← 5% or more errors        │ │
          │  │      provider:                                        │ │
          │  │        prometheus:       ← Query Prometheus          │ │
          │  │          address: http://prometheus:9090             │ │
          │  │          query: |                                     │ │
          │  │            sum(rate(http_errors_total{app="easypj",  │ │
          │  │            version="v2"}[1m])) /                     │ │
          │  │            sum(rate(http_requests_total{app="easypj",│ │
          │  │            version="v2"}[1m]))                       │ │
          │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
          │                                                            │
          │  HOW IT WORKS:                                             │
          │  ┌──────────┐    ┌──────────┐    ┌──────────┐            │
          │  │ Canary   │───▶│ Analysis │───▶│ Promote? │            │
          │  │ 10% v2   │    │ Run      │    │ or abort?│            │
          │  └──────────┘    └────┬─────┘    └──────────┘            │
          │                      │                                    │
          │            ┌─────────┴─────────┐                          │
          │            │                   │                          │
          │            ▼                   ▼                          │
          │      ┌──────────┐       ┌──────────┐                    │
          │      │ ✅ PASS  │       │ ❌ FAIL  │                    │
          │      │ Promote  │       │ Abort +  │                    │
          │      │ to 25%   │       │ Rollback │                    │
          │      └──────────┘       │ to v1    │                    │
          │                         └──────────┘                    │
          │                                                            │
          │  easypj EXAMPLE:                                           │
          │  Step 1: 10% v2 → Analysis: error rate < 1% for 5 min    │
          │  Step 2: If pass → promote to 25% → Analysis again        │
          │  Step 3: If pass → promote to 100%                        │
          │  ANY failure → instant rollback to 100% v1                │
          └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
          ⚠️ GOTCHA — `result` is a VECTOR, not a scalar
          The official docs are explicit: prometheus queries return results in
          the form of a vector, so it is common to access index 0 of the
          returned array.
              ❌ successCondition: result < 0.01
              ✅ successCondition: result[0] < 0.01
          Even a fully-aggregated query like error-rate-check's
          sum(rate(...))/sum(rate(...)) still comes back wrapped in a vector —
          write result[0], not bare result, for jobpost's error-rate and
          latency AnalysisTemplates. (The diagram above simplifies this for
          readability — treat this box as the exam-accurate version.)

          📊 FAILURE vs ERROR vs INCONCLUSIVE — the AnalysisRun outcome model
            ┌────────────┬───────────────────────────┬──────────────────────┐
            │ Outcome    │ What happened              │ jobpost example       │
            ├────────────┼───────────────────────────┼──────────────────────┤
            │ Failure    │ Query ran fine, value      │ error-rate genuinely  │
            │            │ failed successCondition/   │ hit 6% — a REAL v2    │
            │            │ hit failureCondition       │ bug                    │
            │ Error      │ Controller COULDN'T reach  │ Prometheus pod        │
            │            │ the provider at all        │ restarting mid-check  │
            │ Inconclusive│ Neither success nor       │ result landed at      │
            │            │ failure condition matched  │ exactly 2% (between   │
            │            │                            │ your 1%/5% thresholds)│
            └────────────┴───────────────────────────┴──────────────────────┘
              metrics:
                - name: error-rate
                  failureLimit: 3            <- 3 real Failures = analysis fails
                  inconclusiveLimit: 1        <- default 0; raise to tolerate 1
                  consecutiveErrorLimit: 4    <- default 4; CONSECUTIVE, resets on
                                                 any success (errors expected to
                                                 be transient — a flaky Prometheus
                                                 scrape shouldn't kill your canary)

          🧩 TWO WAYS TO ATTACH ANALYSIS TO easypj-rollout
            INLINE (blocks the step until it resolves — used above for the
            10%/25% gates):
              steps:
                - setWeight: 10
                - analysis:
                    templates:
                      - templateName: error-rate-check
            BACKGROUND (runs continuously alongside ALL steps, doesn't block
            progression, watches jobpost's p95 latency for the WHOLE rollout):
              strategy:
                canary:
                  analysis:
                    templates:
                      - templateName: latency-check
                    startingStep: 1          <- begin at step index 1 (0-based)
                  steps: [...]
            Use inline for a hard gate before increasing weight; use background
            for "abort immediately if latency ever spikes, regardless of which
            step we're on."

    16.4  Traffic Routing with Service Meshes
          - Integration with Istio, Linkerd, NGINX, AWS ALB, SMI
          - Traffic splitting at the mesh level
          [COMPARISON TABLE: Traffic routing options]
          📊 TRAFFIC ROUTING PROVIDERS — what Argo Rollouts actually integrates
            ┌────────────┬─────────────────────────┬───────────────────────┐
            │ Provider   │ CR it manages            │ Status (2026)          │
            ├────────────┼─────────────────────────┼───────────────────────┤
            │ Istio      │ VirtualService/DestRule  │ First-class, current   │
            │ NGINX      │ Ingress canary annotat.  │ First-class, current   │
            │ AWS ALB    │ ALB Ingress weighted     │ First-class, current   │
            │            │ target groups            │                        │
            │ SMI        │ TrafficSplit (CRD)       │ First-class BUT LEGACY │
            │            │                          │ — CNCF archived the    │
            │            │                          │ SMI spec               │
            │ Ambassador │ Mapping weight           │ First-class, current   │
            │ APISIX     │ ApisixRoute              │ First-class, current   │
            │ Kong       │ Ingress/canary plugin    │ First-class, current   │
            │ Traefik    │ TraefikService weighted  │ First-class, current   │
            │ Google     │ Gateway API (GKE)        │ First-class, current   │
            │ Cloud      │                          │                        │
            │ Gateway    │ HTTPRoute weighted       │ Plugin (alpha), the    │
            │ API plugin │ backendRefs              │ CURRENT recommended    │
            │            │                          │ path for Linkerd       │
            └────────────┴─────────────────────────┴───────────────────────┘
          "Linkerd" is NOT a first-class provider name — historically you'd
          reach Linkerd through the SMI provider (Linkerd implemented SMI's
          TrafficSplit via its linkerd-smi extension).

          ⚠️ GOTCHA — Don't route jobpost's Linkerd traffic through SMI in 2026
          Two independent deprecations stack here:
            1. CNCF has ARCHIVED the SMI Spec outright.
            2. Linkerd's OWN linkerd-smi extension is deprecated, and its repo
               was archived Oct 28, 2025 — Linkerd now does traffic splitting
               natively via Gateway API HTTPRoute/GRPCRoute weighted backendRefs.
          If easypj runs on Linkerd today, do NOT reach for
          strategy.canary.trafficRouting.smi — install the Argo Rollouts Gateway
          API plugin (rollouts-plugin-trafficrouter-gatewayapi) and point
          trafficRouting.plugins at Linkerd's HTTPRoute instead. Exam-wise, know
          that SMI still technically WORKS in Argo Rollouts (it's not removed)
          — it's just a dead end upstream, not the current recommendation.

          🧭 WHICH TRAFFIC ROUTER FOR easypj?
            Already behind NGINX Ingress? ──> NGINX provider (canary
                                               annotations, zero new infra)
            Already running Istio mesh?  ──> Istio provider (VirtualService/
                                               DestinationRule, most mature
                                               integration, header/mirror routing)
            On AWS with ALB Ingress?     ──> AWS ALB provider (weighted target
                                               groups, no mesh required)
            On Linkerd or want a         ──> Gateway API plugin (HTTPRoute) —
            vendor-neutral standard?          the forward-compatible choice;
                                               avoid the SMI provider for new
                                               easypj deployments in 2026.

    16.5  Monitoring Rollouts
          - kubectl argo rollouts get rollout easypj --watch
          - kubectl argo rollouts dashboard (rich UI)
          - Understanding rollout status and events
          [SCREENSHOT GUIDE: Rollout dashboard]
          🔑 MONITORING & INTERVENTION COMMANDS
            kubectl argo rollouts list rollouts -A          list every Rollout
            kubectl argo rollouts status easypj-rollout      current phase, blocks
                                                              until healthy/failed
            kubectl argo rollouts get rollout easypj-rollout -w    live TUI view
            kubectl argo rollouts dashboard                 web UI, default :3100
            kubectl argo rollouts abort easypj-rollout       stop + revert traffic
                                                              to previous ReplicaSet
                                                              (spec.template still
                                                              points at new version!)
            kubectl argo rollouts retry rollout easypj-rollout  restart after abort

          🩺 easypj-rollout SHOWS "Paused" AND WON'T MOVE — diagnose:
            1. kubectl argo rollouts get rollout easypj-rollout
               → Look at "Status" and "Step" fields.
            2. Is the current step a `pause` with NO duration?
               YES → expected — it's an indefinite pause. Run
                     `kubectl argo rollouts promote easypj-rollout` to advance.
            3. Is the current step an `analysis`?
               → kubectl get analysisrun -l rollouts-pod-template-hash=<hash>
               → kubectl describe analysisrun <name>
               Phase=Inconclusive → thresholds too tight, or the metric legit
                 landed in the dead zone between success/failure conditions.
               Phase=Error → check consecutiveErrorLimit; look at whether
                 Prometheus itself was reachable (kubectl logs on the analysis
                 controller, or the provider address is wrong/unreachable).
               Phase=Failed → a real Failure — check the actual metric value
                 against successCondition/failureCondition math.

          🏢 DAILY-WORK — Don't just watch, get PAGED
          Argo Rollouts emits Kubernetes Events (RolloutStepCompleted,
          RolloutPaused, RolloutAborted, AnalysisRunFailed) that
          `kubectl describe rollout easypj-rollout` surfaces at the bottom. In
          production, feed these into Argo Rollouts' notification integration
          (Slack/webhook subscriptions on the Rollout object) so the team is
          paged the moment jobpost's canary auto-rollback fires — nobody should
          have to be staring at `--watch` when the AnalysisRun fails at 2am.


    [G] UNDER THE HOOD 🔧 — How an AnalysisRun Actually Gets Evaluated
    ────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
    An AnalysisTemplate is inert — like every CRD in this guide (see
    Ch2's [G]), nothing happens until a controller acts on it. When a
    canary step hits `- analysis: {templates: [...]}`, or a background
    analysis starts, the Rollout controller instantiates a real
    AnalysisRun object (a concrete copy of the template, with args
    resolved — e.g. podTemplateHashValue substituted for the actual
    hash of jobpost's new ReplicaSet). The ANALYSIS controller (a
    separate reconcile loop, same pattern as Ch2 taught) then, per
    metric, on the `interval` clock:

        AnalysisRun created
              │
              v
        for each metric: query provider (Prometheus/Web/Job/...)
              │
              v
        store one Measurement in status.metricResults[].measurements
              │
              v
        evaluate successCondition/failureCondition against THIS
        measurement → Successful / Failed / Error / Inconclusive
              │
              v
        repeat until `count` measurements done, or failureLimit /
        inconclusiveLimit / consecutiveErrorLimit tripped early
              │
              v
        aggregate all metrics' outcomes → AnalysisRun.status.phase
              │
              v
        Rollout controller polls THAT phase:
          Successful    → advance to next canary step / finish
          Failed        → abort rollout, revert traffic to stable
          Inconclusive  → PAUSE the rollout, wait for a human
          Error (after consecutiveErrorLimit exceeded) → treated as Failed

    PROSE RECAP: the Rollout controller never talks to Prometheus
    itself — it delegates entirely to the AnalysisRun/analysis
    controller pair, then just reads back one field: status.phase.
    Everything you configure in an AnalysisTemplate (interval, count,
    the three limit fields, successCondition/failureCondition) exists
    to answer one question for the Rollout controller: keep going,
    stop and roll back, or stop and wait for a human?

    [H] KEY TAKEAWAYS 🔑
    ─────────────────────
    - Blue-Green: activeService/previewService are the two Services
      that make the swap instant; autoPromotionEnabled defaults to
      TRUE — set it false yourself for a real manual/analysis gate.
    - Canary: steps are setWeight/pause/analysis in any order; ALWAYS
      end with setWeight: 100 or traffic can stay split forever even
      once the Rollout reports Healthy.
    - AnalysisTemplate metrics: name/interval/count/successCondition/
      failureCondition/provider are the core fields; failureLimit,
      inconclusiveLimit (default 0), and consecutiveErrorLimit
      (default 4) control tolerance.
    - Prometheus provider `result` is a VECTOR — write result[0] for
      typical aggregated instant queries, per the official docs.
    - Failure (bad value) ≠ Error (couldn't even measure) ≠
      Inconclusive (landed between thresholds) — three different
      AnalysisRun outcomes with three different tolerances.
    - Traffic routing providers: Istio/NGINX/ALB/Ambassador/APISIX/
      Kong/Traefik/Google Cloud are first-class; SMI is first-class
      but CNCF-archived and a dead end; Linkerd's own SMI extension is
      also archived (Oct 2025) — use the Gateway API plugin instead.
    - kubectl argo rollouts promote / promote --full / undo
      [--to-revision N] / abort / retry rollout are the core
      intervention commands; dashboard runs a web UI on :3100.
    🎯 Exam relevance: 16.3's field names and the Failure/Error/
       Inconclusive model are the single highest-yield content in the
       whole Rollouts domain — expect them tested directly.

    [I] EXAM GOTCHAS ⚠️
    ─────────────────────
    1. autoPromotionEnabled on blueGreen defaults to TRUE — a Rollout
       with no gate configured is NOT paused, it just looks paused
       briefly while pods become Ready.
    2. Prometheus successCondition/failureCondition: write result[0],
       not bare result — queries return a vector even when aggregated
       to one series.
    3. A canary's last step should be setWeight: 100 — omitting it
       leaves traffic-routing providers only "best effort" at the last
       explicit weight, even though the Rollout shows Healthy.
    4. Error ≠ Failure: consecutiveErrorLimit (default 4, CONSECUTIVE,
       resets on any success) tolerates transient provider outages;
       failureLimit does NOT reset the same way — real bad values
       accumulate toward it.
    5. SMI is still technically supported by Argo Rollouts but is
       CNCF-archived AND Linkerd's own SMI extension is archived
       (2025) — do not present it as the current default for a
       Linkerd/vendor-neutral setup; that's the Gateway API plugin.
    6. `kubectl argo rollouts abort` reverts ACTIVE TRAFFIC to the
       previous ReplicaSet but does NOT revert spec.template — if the
       Rollout later exits the aborted state it will retry the same
       new version again unless you also edit the spec back.

    [J] SPEED TIPS ⚡
    ───────────────────
    1. `kubectl argo rollouts set image easypj-rollout
        easypj=registry.io/easypj:v2.1` — start a new rollout without
       hand-editing YAML.
    2. `kubectl argo rollouts promote easypj-rollout --full` —
       force-complete a rollout in a genuine incident, skipping all
       remaining analysis/pauses/steps.
    3. `kubectl argo rollouts status easypj-rollout` — blocks in your
       terminal until the Rollout reaches Healthy/Degraded/Aborted;
       great for CI pipelines instead of manually polling.

    [K] COMMANDS TO REMEMBER 🔑
    ─────────────────────────────
      Strategy control: kubectl argo rollouts promote <name> [--full] ·
                 kubectl argo rollouts undo <name> [--to-revision N] ·
                 kubectl argo rollouts abort <name> ·
                 kubectl argo rollouts retry rollout <name>
      Watch:     kubectl argo rollouts get rollout <name> --watch ·
                 kubectl argo rollouts status <name> ·
                 kubectl argo rollouts list rollouts -A ·
                 kubectl argo rollouts dashboard [--port 3100]
      Analysis:  kubectl get analysistemplate,analysisrun ·
                 kubectl describe analysisrun <name>
      Convert:   kubectl argo rollouts set image <name> <container>=<image>

    [L] PRACTICE DRILL 🧪 — ⏱️ 20-Minute Progressive Delivery Lab
    ────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
      1. (5 min) Convert easypj to a Rollout (from Ch15.4) with a
         3-step canary: setWeight 20 → pause 30s → setWeight 100.
         Confirm `kubectl argo rollouts get rollout easypj-rollout`
         shows the pause.
      2. (5 min) `kubectl argo rollouts promote easypj-rollout` to
         advance past the pause; watch it reach Healthy.
      3. (5 min) Write a minimal AnalysisTemplate (provider: web,
         hitting jobpost's own /health/ endpoint, successCondition:
         result[0] == 200) and attach it inline at the 20% step.
      4. (5 min) Deliberately break the health check (wrong URL) and
         watch the AnalysisRun go Error, not Failed — confirm you can
         tell the two apart from `kubectl describe analysisrun`.
      SUCCESS CRITERIA: you triggered and correctly diagnosed an Error-
      phase AnalysisRun without looking anything up.

    [M] LAB QUESTIONS 🧪 (10)
    ───────────────────────────
    Q1. jobpost's blueGreen strategy sets activeService/previewService
        but nothing else. QA is about to test Green via previewService
        when they notice active traffic already flipped to it. Why?
    A1. autoPromotionEnabled defaults to true with no analysis gate
        configured — add autoPromotionEnabled: false (and ideally
        prePromotionAnalysis) to require an explicit gate.
    Explanation: a Rollout without an explicit gate isn't "paused for
    review," it's just briefly slow while pods become Ready.

    Q2. Write the corrected successCondition for:
        query: sum(rate(http_errors_total{app="easypj"}[1m])) /
               sum(rate(http_requests_total{app="easypj"}[1m]))
        successCondition should mean "error rate under 1%."
    A2. successCondition: result[0] < 0.01
    Explanation: Prometheus queries always return a vector even when
    fully aggregated to one series; the docs teach indexing [0] for
    exactly this pattern.

    Q3. jobpost-rollout's canary steps end at `- setWeight: 50` with no
        further steps. Two weeks later the Rollout shows Healthy but
        production traffic is STILL visibly ~50/50 between v1 and v2.
        Diagnose and fix.
    A3. No final setWeight: 100 step — the traffic-routing provider
        only guarantees "best effort" at the last explicit weight.
        Fix: add `- setWeight: 100` as the final step and re-roll, or
        edit the step list and let it re-reconcile.
    Explanation: "Healthy" reflects the Rollout controller's own state
    machine completing its step list, not that traffic reached 100%
    at the routing layer.

    Q4. An AnalysisRun for jobpost's error-rate-check shows phase=Error
        three times in a row, then phase=Successful on try #4. Did the
        analysis fail?
    A4. No — consecutiveErrorLimit (default 4) wasn't reached, and it
        resets on any success; this is exactly the transient-outage
        tolerance the Error/Failure split exists for.
    Explanation: distinguishes Error (couldn't measure — e.g. a
    Prometheus scrape blip) from Failure (measured fine, value was
    bad) — Error uses a resetting consecutive counter on purpose.

    Q5. You need easypj's error-rate-check AnalysisTemplate reused by
        every namespace's Rollout (dev/staging/prod) without copying
        the YAML three times. What object do you use instead of
        AnalysisTemplate?
    A5. ClusterAnalysisTemplate — identical schema, not namespace-
        scoped, referenced the same way from any Rollout cluster-wide.
    Explanation: same relationship as ClusterRole vs Role in plain K8s
    RBAC — cluster-scoped variant of a namespaced object.

    Q6. easypj runs on Linkerd. A teammate proposes
        strategy.canary.trafficRouting.smi for weighted traffic
        splitting. Is this the right 2026 recommendation? Why or why
        not, and what should you use instead?
    A6. Not recommended — CNCF has archived the SMI Spec, and
        Linkerd's own linkerd-smi extension is deprecated/archived
        (Oct 2025). Use the Argo Rollouts Gateway API plugin
        (HTTPRoute weighted backendRefs) instead.
    Explanation: SMI still technically works in Argo Rollouts (not
    removed), but it's a dead end upstream on both the spec side and
    the Linkerd side — Gateway API is the current path.

    Q7. `kubectl argo rollouts abort easypj-rollout` is run mid-canary.
        Traffic reverts to v1 immediately. An hour later, without
        anyone touching the Rollout, it starts progressing toward v2
        again. What happened?
    A7. abort only reverts ACTIVE TRAFFIC to the previous ReplicaSet —
        it does NOT revert spec.template. If the Rollout exits the
        aborted state (e.g. via a later spec touch or automation), it
        resumes progressing toward the version still named in the spec.
    Explanation: abort is a traffic-level emergency brake, not a full
    undo — full rollback needs `undo` (or manually reverting the spec)
    too.

    Q8. jobpost's AnalysisTemplate has inconclusiveLimit: 0 (the
        default) and one measurement lands exactly between
        successCondition and failureCondition. What happens to the
        canary?
    A8. The AnalysisRun goes Inconclusive, and the Rollout PAUSES
        (does not auto-promote OR auto-rollback) — it waits for a
        human, since inconclusiveLimit: 0 means zero tolerance for an
        ambiguous result.
    Explanation: Inconclusive is deliberately treated differently from
    both Successful and Failed — it's a "we genuinely don't know,
    don't guess" signal.

    Q9. Compare: attaching error-rate-check as steps[].analysis at the
        10% step, vs attaching it as strategy.canary.analysis
        (background) with startingStep: 0. For jobpost, which one lets
        a metric spike at the 50% step trigger a rollback even though
        the analysis technically only started gating the 10% step?
    A9. Background analysis (strategy.canary.analysis) — it runs
        continuously across ALL steps once started, independent of
        which individual step is currently paused, so a spike at 50%
        still triggers it. Inline (steps[].analysis) only blocks the
        ONE step it's attached to.
    Explanation: inline = one-time gate before advancing; background =
    continuous safety net for the whole rollout's duration.

    Q10. `kubectl argo rollouts promote easypj-rollout --full` is run
         during a canary that has an inline analysis step in progress.
         What happens to that AnalysisRun?
    A10. The rollout skips directly to fully promoted (100% stable),
         bypassing the remaining analysis, pauses, and steps entirely
         — the in-progress AnalysisRun is not waited on.
    Explanation: --full is an explicit override for "I know what I'm
    doing, ship it now" — it intentionally discards the safety gates
    you configured, which is why it's a Speed Tip for incidents, not a
    habit.

    [N] VISUAL SUMMARY
    ────────────────────
    ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
    │  CHAPTER 16 IN ONE PICTURE — jobpost v2 Release, Start to Finish   │
    │                                                                    │
    │  strategy: blueGreen          OR      strategy: canary            │
    │  activeService/previewService         steps: [setWeight/pause/    │
    │  autoPromotionEnabled: false           analysis]...setWeight: 100 │
    │       │                                     │                      │
    │       v                                     v                      │
    │  prePromotionAnalysis                 inline OR background        │
    │  (smoke test Green)                   AnalysisTemplate             │
    │       │                                     │                      │
    │       └──────────────┬──────────────────────┘                      │
    │                      v                                             │
    │            AnalysisRun (per metric, per interval)                 │
    │            Successful → advance   Failed → abort + revert         │
    │            Error (transient) → retry   Inconclusive → PAUSE       │
    │                      │                                             │
    │                      v                                             │
    │            Traffic split enforced by a REAL router:                │
    │            Istio / NGINX / ALB / Gateway API                       │
    │            (NOT SMI — CNCF-archived, Linkerd's SMI ext. archived)  │
    │                      │                                             │
    │                      v                                             │
    │            kubectl argo rollouts get/status/dashboard              │
    │            promote · undo · abort · retry                          │
    └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
    PROSE RECAP: Chapter 16 turned Chapter 15's Rollout CRD into a real
    jobpost release process — Blue-Green for instant, reversible
    cutovers and Canary for gradual, monitored ones, both optionally
    driven by an AnalysisTemplate that turns "promote or rollback" from
    a human judgment call into a measured decision (with three distinct
    outcomes: Failed/Error/Inconclusive, each handled differently).
    Traffic routing is the plumbing that makes any of this precise —
    and in 2026, that plumbing is Istio/NGINX/ALB/Gateway API, not the
    now-archived SMI path. Chapter 17 shifts to Argo Events, which will
    eventually trigger THIS exact Rollout automatically on every
    GitHub push to jobpost.

       CSV job uploads from S3, and run nightly maintenance calendars.

Chapter 17: Introduction to Event-Driven Automation
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
    🟢🟡 BEGINNER/INTERMEDIATE · ⏱️ ~2 hours · 🎯 CAPA domain: Argo Events 12% (Understand Argo Events Fundamentals) · 🏢 Daily-Work: high

    [B] WHY THIS MATTERS
    ────────────────────
    Parts 2-4 gave easypj a full deploy-and-release pipeline — but every
    single one of those pipelines still starts with a HUMAN typing
    `argo submit` or `git push` and then watching. Argo Events is the
    piece that removes that human: a GitHub push, a CSV landing in a
    bucket, or 2 AM arriving on the clock all become the trigger that
    starts everything else in this guide automatically. This chapter
    builds the vocabulary — EventSource, Sensor, Trigger, EventBus —
    that Chapters 18-19 turn into real, running automation.

    ┌───────────────────────────────┬───────────────────────────────┐
    │ BEFORE CHAPTER 17              │ AFTER CHAPTER 17                │
    ├───────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────┤
    │ Every Workflow starts with a   │ Understand the 4-part pipeline  │
    │ human running `argo submit`    │ (EventSource→EventBus→Sensor→   │
    │                                 │ Trigger) that removes that human│
    │ "NATS" is just a word in a     │ Know EventBus has 3 real        │
    │ diagram                        │ backends and which one to use   │
    │ 20-30 event source types feels │ Know CloudEvents is WHY one      │
    │ like an arbitrary list          │ filtering language works        │
    │                                 │ across all of them              │
    └───────────────────────────────┴───────────────────────────────┘

    [C] LEARNING OBJECTIVES — 🎯 What you'll be able to DO
    ────────────────────────────────────────────────────
      - Explain what Argo Events is and place it relative to the other
        3 Argo tools — it triggers Workflows, it does not run them.
      - Name the 4 architecture components (EventSource, EventBus,
        Sensor, Trigger) and state each one's single responsibility.
      - Explain why every EventSource, regardless of source type, emits
        the same CloudEvents envelope shape.
      - Choose JetStream over NATS Streaming for a new EventBus and
        explain why (Streaming is legacy/deprecated upstream).
      - Install Argo Events (namespace + CRDs + controller + EventBus)
        and verify it with `kubectl get eventbus`.

    [D] CAPA RELEVANCE
    ───────────────────
    This chapter is the SOLE teaching ground for "Understand Argo
    Events Fundamentals" — the smallest of the 4 CAPA domains (12%) but
    still directly graded. Chapter 18 covers the sibling competency
    ("Understand Argo Event Components and Architecture") in depth;
    this chapter's job is making sure the vocabulary is solid before
    18's YAML gets real.

    [E] SECTION-MAP DIAGRAM — you are here
    ────────────────────────────────────
    ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
    │  17.1 What IS it?  --defines-->  event-driven trigger layer    │
    │            │                     for Argo Workflows            │
    │            v                                                   │
    │  17.2 Architecture  --names-->  EventSource -> EventBus ->      │
    │            │                    Sensor -> Trigger                │
    │            v                                                   │
    │  17.3 Install  --stands up-->  argo-events namespace,           │
    │                                 controller + EventBus running    │
    └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
    PROSE RECAP: this chapter is short and linear on purpose — define
    it (17.1), name its 4 moving parts (17.2), stand it up (17.3).
    Chapter 18 spends far more time on the SAME 4 parts, now as real
    YAML you write yourself.

    17.1  What is Argo Events?
          - Event-driven workflow automation for Kubernetes
          - Trigger workflows based on external events
          - 27 officially documented event sources as of v1.9 (Webhook,
            GitHub, GitLab, Bitbucket, S3/Minio, SQS, SNS, GCP PubSub,
            Azure Event Hub, Kafka, NATS, MQTT, Redis, Calendar, File,
            Resource (K8s), Slack, Stripe, Generic, etc.) — newer
            additions (Gerrit, SFTP, Azure Service Bus, Redis Streams)
            push the real total past 30
          📖 KEY TERM — CloudEvents: every EventSource, regardless of
             source type, transforms the raw event into the CNCF
             CloudEvents envelope (a standard {id, source, type, data}
             shape) before publishing it. That's WHY one Sensor
             filtering language works no matter which of the 27+ source
             types produced the event.
          [DIAGRAM: External events → Argo Events → Argo Workflows]
          ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
          │  EXTERNAL EVENTS → ARGO EVENTS → ARGO WORKFLOWS              │
          │                                                            │
          │  🐙 GitHub push     ☁️ S3 upload     ⏰ 2 AM clock tick        │
          │       │                  │                  │                │
          │       ▼                  ▼                  ▼                │
          │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
          │  │      ARGO EVENTS (translates EVERY source into the     │  │
          │  │      SAME CloudEvents envelope — Ch 17's KEY TERM)      │  │
          │  └────────────────────────┬─────────────────────────────┘  │
          │                           │                                  │
          │                           ▼                                  │
          │              🔃 ARGO WORKFLOWS submits a Workflow            │
          │              (clone→test→build, or a CSV import, or a       │
          │               nightly pg_dump — same trigger mechanism,      │
          │               three completely different jobs)               │
          └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
          PROSE RECAP: no matter which of the 27+ source types fires,
          the SAME downstream shape applies — Argo Events is the
          universal adapter; Argo Workflows is always what actually
          does the work. Chapter 18 opens this box up into its real
          parts (EventSource/EventBus/Sensor/Trigger).

    17.2  Argo Events Architecture
          - EventSource: Listens to external events
          - Sensor: Filters, transforms, and triggers actions
          - Trigger: Action to take (usually submit a Workflow)
          - EventBus: Internal event transport (NATS)
          [ARCHITECTURE DIAGRAM: EventSource → EventBus → Sensor → Trigger]
          ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
          │  ARGO EVENTS ARCHITECTURE                                  │
          │                                                            │
          │  ┌──────────┐   ┌──────────┐   ┌──────────┐              │
          │  │ GitHub   │   │   S3     │   │  Kafka   │   (external) │
          │  │ Webhook  │   │  Event   │   │  Topic   │              │
          │  └────┬─────┘   └────┬─────┘   └────┬─────┘              │
          │       │              │              │                     │
          │       ▼              ▼              ▼                     │
          │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────┐            │
          │  │           EventSource (listeners)         │            │
          │  └────────────────────┬─────────────────────┘            │
          │                       │                                   │
          │                       ▼                                   │
          │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────┐            │
          │  │        EventBus (NATS streaming)          │            │
          │  └────────────────────┬─────────────────────┘            │
          │                       │                                   │
          │                       ▼                                   │
          │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────┐            │
          │  │    Sensor (filter + transform + trigger)  │            │
          │  └────────────────────┬─────────────────────┘            │
          │                       │                                   │
          │                       ▼                                   │
          │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────┐            │
          │  │          Argo Workflow (execute)          │            │
          │  └──────────────────────────────────────────┘            │
          └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
          ⚠️ GOTCHA — the diagram's "EventBus (NATS streaming)" label is
             the LEGACY option. EventBus actually supports 3 backends:
             NATS Streaming (native, deprecated — upstream NATS project
             EOL'd it), JetStream (native, CURRENT recommended default),
             Kafka (exotic, bring-your-own cluster). The official
             quick-start still demos NATS Streaming for simplicity —
             don't copy that habit into production or onto the exam.

    17.3  Installing Argo Events
          [CODE BLOCK: Install commands]
          ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
          │  # 1. Namespace + controller + CRDs (cluster-wide)          │
          │  kubectl create namespace argo-events                       │
          │  kubectl apply -f https://raw.githubusercontent.com/        │
          │    argoproj/argo-events/stable/manifests/install.yaml       │
          │  kubectl apply -f https://raw.githubusercontent.com/        │
          │    argoproj/argo-events/stable/manifests/                   │
          │    install-validating-webhook.yaml                          │
          │                                                              │
          │  # 2. EventBus — RECOMMENDED: JetStream, not native.yaml    │
          │  apiVersion: argoproj.io/v1alpha1                            │
          │  kind: EventBus                                             │
          │  metadata: {name: default}                                  │
          │  spec: {jetstream: {version: latest}}                       │
          │                                                              │
          │  # 3. Verify                                                │
          │  kubectl get pods -n argo-events                            │
          │  kubectl get eventbus -n argo-events   # shortname: eb      │
          └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
          - kubectl apply install manifest (namespace + CRDs + controller)
          - Installing EventBus (JetStream recommended, NATS Streaming
            is legacy — see 17.2 gotcha)
          - Verifying installation
          ⚡ SPEED TIP — confirm CRDs registered in one line:
             `kubectl api-resources | grep argoproj.io` should list
             eventbus (eb), eventsources (es), sensors (sn).


    [G] UNDER THE HOOD 🔧 — Why CloudEvents Is the Quiet Hero of This Chapter
    ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
    27+ EventSource types speak wildly different native protocols — a
    GitHub webhook's JSON payload looks nothing like an S3 bucket
    notification, which looks nothing like a Kafka message. If Sensors
    had to understand each protocol individually, every new EventSource
    type would mean rewriting the Sensor's filtering logic. Instead,
    EVERY EventSource — regardless of source — transforms its raw event
    into the same CNCF CloudEvents envelope (`{id, source, type, data}`)
    before publishing to the EventBus. The Sensor's `filters.data`
    (Ch 18.3) always reads `data.body...`, no matter whether that data
    came from GitHub, S3, or a cron tick. This one abstraction is WHY
    Chapter 18's filter syntax is identical across every EventSource
    type you'll ever configure.

        GitHub push JSON ──┐
        S3 notification ───┼──> EventSource wraps in CloudEvents envelope
        Kafka message ─────┘         {id, source, type, data: {body: ...}}
                                              │
                                              v
                                   EventBus (transport, protocol-agnostic)
                                              │
                                              v
                                   Sensor filters ALWAYS read data.body...
                                   — same syntax, any source type

    PROSE RECAP: the 27+ EventSource types are 27+ TRANSLATORS into one
    common shape — that's the architectural reason Argo Events scales to
    so many integrations without the Sensor/Trigger layer ever changing.

    [H] KEY TAKEAWAYS 🔑
    ─────────────────────
    - Argo Events triggers Workflows (or other actions); it does not
      run them — that's still the Workflow Controller's job (Ch 9).
    - 4 components: EventSource (listens), EventBus (transports),
      Sensor (filters+decides), Trigger (acts) — each with exactly one
      responsibility.
    - Every EventSource emits the same CloudEvents envelope, which is
      why one filter syntax works across 27+ different source types.
    - EventBus has 3 real backends: NATS Streaming (legacy, EOL'd
      upstream), JetStream (current recommended default), Kafka
      (exotic, bring-your-own cluster) — don't default to Streaming.
    - Install order: namespace + CRDs + controller first, THEN EventBus
      — a Sensor created before its EventBus exists sits NotReady.
    🎯 EXAM RELEVANCE: sole teaching chapter for "Understand Argo Events
       Fundamentals" — expect direct definitional questions on these 4
       components and the CloudEvents rationale.

    [I] EXAM GOTCHAS ⚠️
    ─────────────────────
    1. Assuming "EventBus" means NATS Streaming specifically — the
       official quick-start still demos it, but JetStream is the
       current recommended default; Streaming is deprecated upstream.
    2. Believing a Sensor talks to the EventSource directly — it
       subscribes to the EventBus; the two never communicate directly.
    3. Assuming Argo Events RUNS the triggered Workflow itself — it
       only creates the Workflow object; the Workflow Controller (a
       completely separate component, Ch 9) does the actual running.
    4. Thinking each EventSource type needs its own Sensor filter
       syntax — CloudEvents normalization means one syntax fits all.

    [J] SPEED TIPS ⚡
    ───────────────────
    - `kubectl api-resources | grep argoproj.io` right after install to
      confirm eventbus (eb), eventsources (es), sensors (sn) all
      registered before building anything on top.
    - `kubectl get eventbus -n argo-events` — a healthy EventBus is the
      #1 prerequisite to check before debugging ANY EventSource/Sensor
      pair that "isn't working."

    [K] COMMANDS TO REMEMBER 🔑
    ──────────────────────────────
      kubectl create namespace argo-events
      kubectl apply -f <install.yaml> && kubectl apply -f
        <install-validating-webhook.yaml>
      kubectl apply -f <EventBus JetStream manifest>
      kubectl get pods -n argo-events
      kubectl get eventbus -n argo-events   # shortname: eb
      kubectl api-resources | grep argoproj.io

    [L] PRACTICE DRILL 🧪 (~10 min, do-it-now)
    ─────────────────────────────────────────
      1. Install Argo Events into a scratch namespace using the 17.3
         commands, choosing JetStream (not NATS Streaming) for EventBus.
      2. `kubectl get pods -n argo-events` — confirm the controller pods
         reach Running.
      3. `kubectl get eventbus -n argo-events` — confirm STATUS shows
         Deployed/Ready before moving to Chapter 18.
      4. In one sentence, explain why a Sensor never talks to an
         EventSource directly.

    [M] LAB QUESTIONS 🧪 (10, exam-style)
    ───────────────────────────────────────
    Q1. Which component is responsible for actually CREATING the
        Workflow object when an event matches?
    A1. The Sensor (via its Trigger).
    Explanation: EventSource only listens/publishes; EventBus only
    transports; the Sensor evaluates filters and its Trigger performs
    the action (commonly submitting a Workflow).

    Q2. True or False: a GitHub EventSource and an S3 EventSource
        require different Sensor filter syntax because their native
        payloads are structured differently.
    A2. False.
    Explanation: both are normalized into the same CloudEvents envelope
    before reaching the EventBus — Sensor filters always read
    data.body..., regardless of source type.

    Q3. Your team is standing up a new EventBus in 2026. Which backend
        should you choose, and why not the one shown in most quick-
        start tutorials?
    A3. JetStream — NATS Streaming (the quick-start default) is native
        but LEGACY; the upstream NATS project has EOL'd Streaming.
    Explanation: tutorials optimize for simplicity, not currency —
    verified fact-check in this chapter.

    Q4. A Sensor is created before its EventBus exists. What state does
        it sit in?
    A4. NotReady — indefinitely, until the EventBus is created.
    Explanation: install order matters: namespace/CRDs/controller, then
    EventBus, THEN EventSource/Sensor.

    Q5. Does Argo Events itself execute the triggered Workflow's steps?
    A5. No — it only creates the Workflow object (or calls another
        trigger type); the Workflow Controller (Ch 9), a completely
        separate component, does the actual reconciliation and
        execution.
    Explanation: reinforces the "4 thermostats, not one furnace" model
    used throughout this guide (Ch 20 [G]).

    Q6. Name the 4 core Argo Events components and each one's single
        responsibility in one phrase.
    A6. EventSource (listens to external events), EventBus (transports
        events internally), Sensor (filters and decides), Trigger
        (performs the resulting action).
    Explanation: direct recall question — the architecture skeleton
    every later chapter builds on.

    Q7. Roughly how many EventSource types does Argo Events officially
        document as of v1.9, and is that number fixed?
    A7. 27+ officially documented, with newer additions (Gerrit, SFTP,
        Azure Service Bus, Redis Streams) pushing the real total past
        30 — the list grows over releases, it isn't fixed.
    Explanation: matches the fact-checked count in this chapter's 17.1.

    Q8. What does the CNCF CloudEvents envelope guarantee about every
        event Argo Events processes, regardless of source?
    A8. A standard {id, source, type, data} shape — a consistent
        structure the Sensor's filter language can always rely on.
    Explanation: the architectural reason 27+ wildly different source
    protocols can share one filtering syntax.

    Q9. Which EventBus backend requires you to bring your own external
        cluster, unlike the other two?
    A9. Kafka — it's the "exotic, bring-your-own cluster" option, unlike
        NATS Streaming/JetStream which run as in-cluster components
        Argo Events manages itself.
    Explanation: a real operational tradeoff worth knowing before
    picking a backend for a new environment.

    Q10. After installing Argo Events, which single command most
         directly confirms the CRDs registered correctly?
    A10. kubectl api-resources | grep argoproj.io — should list eventbus
         (eb), eventsources (es), sensors (sn).
    Explanation: the fastest one-line sanity check before building
    anything on top, called out as a Speed Tip in 17.3.

    [N] VISUAL SUMMARY
    ────────────────────
    ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
    │  CHAPTER 17 IN ONE PICTURE                                    │
    │                                                                │
    │  EXTERNAL EVENT          EventSource         EventBus          │
    │  (GitHub/S3/Kafka/  ───> (listens,      ───> (JetStream        │
    │   Calendar/27+ more)      CloudEvents         recommended,      │
    │                            envelope)           NATS Streaming    │
    │                                                 legacy)          │
    │                                                     │            │
    │                                                     v            │
    │                                                  Sensor          │
    │                                            (filters data.body,   │
    │                                             decides + triggers)  │
    │                                                     │            │
    │                                                     v            │
    │                                                  Trigger         │
    │                                            (usually: submit a    │
    │                                             Workflow — Ch 9)     │
    └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
    PROSE RECAP: Argo Events is the piece that removes the human from
    "start the pipeline" — 4 simple components, one shared envelope
    format, and an install order that must go namespace-controller-
    EventBus-before-anything-else. Chapter 18 turns every box in this
    diagram into real YAML.


Chapter 18: EventSources and Sensors
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
    🟡 INTERMEDIATE · ⏱️ ~3 hours · 🎯 CAPA domain: Argo Events 12% (Understand Argo Event Components and Architecture) · 🏢 Daily-Work: high

    [B] WHY THIS MATTERS
    ────────────────────
    Right now, easypj's automation is imaginary: a recruiter uploads a CSV of
    job postings to S3 and NOTHING happens until a human remembers to run the
    import script; a developer pushes to `main` and NOTHING happens until
    someone manually redeploys; the nightly database backup depends on
    someone not forgetting. EventSources and Sensors are the two objects that
    turn "someone has to remember" into "it just happens" — this chapter is
    where Argo Events stops being an architecture diagram (Ch 17) and becomes
    YAML you can write.

      ┌─────────────────────────────┬─────────────────────────────┐
      │ ❌ BEFORE (manual)            │ ✅ AFTER (Argo Events)        │
      ├─────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────┤
      │ CSV lands in S3 → sits there │ S3 ObjectCreated → EventSource│
      │ until someone remembers      │ → Sensor → Workflow imports   │
      │                               │ it within seconds              │
      │ git push main → Slack ping   │ GitHub webhook → EventSource  │
      │ "someone please deploy"      │ → Sensor → build Workflow      │
      │                               │ auto-submitted                 │
      │ 2 AM backup = whoever's on   │ Calendar EventSource fires    │
      │ call sets a personal alarm   │ the backup Workflow, no human  │
      └─────────────────────────────┴─────────────────────────────┘

    [C] LEARNING OBJECTIVES  🎯 What you'll be able to DO
    ────────────────────────────────────────────────────
      ✅ Pick the correct EventSource type for a real trigger (webhook, S3,
         Kafka, Calendar, Resource) and justify it in one sentence.
      ✅ Write a complete, working webhook EventSource manifest for easypj.
      ✅ Write a complete Sensor with a dependency, a data filter, and a
         trigger (k8s or argoWorkflow) — from a blank file, no copy-paste.
      ✅ Name all 5 filter categories and explain when each beats the others.
      ✅ Parameterize a triggered Workflow with real event data (e.g. inject
         a GitHub commit SHA as a Workflow argument).
      ✅ Diagnose the two most common Sensor failure modes (missing RBAC,
         wrong dataKey path) from pod logs alone.

    [D] CAPA RELEVANCE  🎯
    ────────────────────
    Maps directly to "Understand Argo Event Components and Architecture"
    (Events domain, 12% of the CAPA exam). This is the SINGLE most
    YAML-field-dense chapter in the Events domain: EventSource type
    configuration (18.1-18.2), the Sensor spec — dependencies, filters,
    triggers (18.3), and trigger parameterization (18.4). Expect exam items
    to test exact field names and locations (spec.webhook.<name>.port,
    spec.dependencies[].filters.data[].comparator,
    spec.triggers[].template.k8s.parameters[].src.dataKey) more than
    conceptual questions — know the YAML shape cold.

    [E] SECTION-MAP DIAGRAM
    ────────────────────────
      ┌──────────────┐   ┌──────────────┐   ┌──────────────┐   ┌──────────────┐
      │ 18.1          │──>│ 18.2         │──>│ 18.3         │──>│ 18.4         │
      │ CHOOSE the    │   │ BUILD your   │   │ Sensor:      │   │ PARAMETERIZE │
      │ EventSource   │   │ first        │   │ dependency + │   │ the trigger  │
      │ type          │   │ EventSource  │   │ filter +     │   │ with event   │
      │ (webhook, S3, │   │ (webhook,    │   │ trigger      │   │ data (commit │
      │ Kafka, ...)   │   │ for GitHub)  │   │ (k8s/argoWF) │   │ SHA, etc.)   │
      └──────────────┘   └──────────────┘   └──────────────┘   └──────────────┘
      You are building ONE pipeline across all four sections: a GitHub push to
      easypj → webhook EventSource → filtered Sensor → parameterized build
      Workflow. Chapter 19 reuses this exact pattern for S3, Calendar, and
      Resource triggers.

    18.1  EventSource Types Reference
          - Webhook: HTTP endpoint for external services
          - GitHub/GitLab: Repository events (push, PR, release)
          - S3: Bucket notifications
          - SQS/SNS: AWS queue notifications
          - Kafka: Topic messages
          - Calendar: Cron-based events
          - Resource: Kubernetes resource changes
          - File: File system watcher
          [TABLE: All EventSource types with use cases]
          ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
          │  ARGO EVENTS — COMPLETE EVENTSOURCE TYPES                  │
          │                                                            │
          │  ┌──────────────────┬────────────────────────────────────┐ │
          │  │ EVENTSOURCE TYPE │ USE CASE + EXAMPLE                 │ │
          │  ├──────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────┤ │

          │  │                  │ service to POST events             │ │
          │  │                  │ (GitHub, Jenkins, custom apps)     │ │

          │  │ AMQP/RabbitMQ    │ Queue message consumption          │ │

          │  ├──────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────┤ │

          │  ├──────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────┤ │
