          │  ├──────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────┤ │
          │  │ Stripe           │ Payment webhook events             │ │
          │  ├──────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────┤ │
          │  │ Slack            │ Slack message events               │ │
          │  ├──────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────┤ │
          │  │ Pulsar           │ Apache Pulsar topics               │ │
          │  ├──────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────┤ │
          │  │ Emitter          │ Emitter cloud pub/sub              │ │
          │  └──────────────────┴────────────────────────────────────┘ │
          │                                                            │
          │  CAPA EXAM FOCUS (12%): Webhook, GitHub, S3, Calendar,    │
          │  Resource, Kafka — know these 6 types best!               │
          └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
          📖 KEY TERM + ⚠️ GOTCHA — "S3" is really `spec.minio`
              There is no `s3:` field. AWS S3 buckets are configured under `spec.minio`
              (the EventSource uses the S3-compatible minio-go client, so the SAME
              field talks to real AWS S3 OR a self-hosted MinIO — just point `endpoint`
              at s3.amazonaws.com vs your MinIO host). On the exam, if you see a
              question about "S3 bucket notifications," the YAML key to write is
              `minio:`, not `s3:`.

          🧭 THE FULL LIST — this table isn't all 32
              This guide's table covers the ~20 EventSource types you'll actually
              touch. Argo Events ships 32 total (verified against the current CRD
              schema). The 12 you won't see on the CAPA exam but exist in production:
              Bitbucket Server, Redis Streams, Azure Queue Storage, Azure Service Bus,
              HDFS, NetApp StorageGrid, Generic (custom gRPC source), Gerrit,
              AlibabaCloud MNS, SFTP, NSQ, MQTT. 🎯 CAPA FOCUS stays on the 6 the
              table already calls out (Webhook, GitHub, S3/minio, Calendar, Resource,
              Kafka).

          ⚠️ GOTCHA — same name, opposite direction
              Slack, Pulsar, and the Azure services exist as BOTH an EventSource
              (inbound) AND a Trigger (outbound) type. `spec.slack` (EventSource)
              RECEIVES Slack Events API callbacks; `triggers[].template.slack`
              (Trigger) SENDS a Slack message. Don't confuse "listen for a Slack
              event" with "notify Slack when something happens" — they're opposite
              directions using similarly-named blocks in different objects. Bonus
              trap: the EventSource field is spelled `azureEventsHub` (Events-Hub)
              but the Trigger field is spelled `azureEventHubs` (Event-Hubs) — one
              letter's position differs.

    18.2  Creating Your First EventSource
          - Webhook EventSource for GitHub pushes
          - Exposing the webhook (Ingress, port-forward)
          - Testing with curl
          [COMPLETE YAML: Webhook EventSource]
          ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
          │  WEBHOOK EVENTSOURCE — REQUEST PATH, END TO END              │
          │                                                            │
          │  🐙 GitHub POST /easypj/push                                 │
          │        │                                                    │
          │        ▼                                                    │
          │  Service: easypj-github-webhook-eventsource-svc              │
          │  (AUTO-CREATED — not the EventSource's own metadata.name!)   │
          │        │                                                    │
          │        ▼                                                    │
          │  EventSource Pod, container port 12000, endpoint /easypj/push│
          │  wraps the payload in a CloudEvents envelope                 │
          │        │                                                    │
          │        ▼                                                    │
          │  EventBus (NATS) ──▶ waiting Sensors (Ch 18.3)               │
          └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
          📐 ANATOMY CALLOUT — verified webhook EventSource for easypj
              apiVersion: argoproj.io/v1alpha1
              kind: EventSource
              metadata:
                name: easypj-github-webhook          #<- Sensors reference this name
                namespace: argo-events
              spec:
                service:                             #<- optional: exposes a ClusterIP Service
                  ports:
                    - port: 12000
                      targetPort: 12000
                webhook:
                  easypj-push:                       #<- this KEY is the "eventName"
                    port: "12000"                    #<- container port, quoted string
                    endpoint: /easypj/push            #<- HTTP path
                    method: POST

          ⚠️ GOTCHA — the auto-created Service name
              The controller auto-creates a K8s Service called
              `<eventsource-name>-eventsource-svc` (here:
              `easypj-github-webhook-eventsource-svc`). Point your Ingress or
              `kubectl port-forward` at THAT name, not the EventSource's own name —
              a very common "why can't curl reach it" support question.

          ⚡ SPEED TIP
              Before wiring a real Ingress/GitHub webhook, prove the pipe works with:
                kubectl port-forward svc/easypj-github-webhook-eventsource-svc 12000:12000
                curl -X POST localhost:12000/easypj/push -d '{"ref":"refs/heads/main"}'
              then check the EventSource pod log for the received payload.

    18.3  Sensor Deep Dive
          - Dependencies: Which events to listen for
          - Filters: Data and context filtering
          - Triggers: What to do when events match
          - Trigger types: Argo Workflow, HTTP, Kafka, AWS Lambda, Slack, etc.
          [YAML ANATOMY: Sensor with all sections explained]
          🔧 UNDER THE HOOD — the 5 filter categories + 2-tier logic
              A dependency's `filters` block has FIVE independent categories, each
              optional:
                • data    — inspect the payload JSON: {path, type, value, comparator}
                • context — inspect the CloudEvents envelope (source/type/subject),
                            not the body
                • exprs   — expr-lang boolean expressions over payload fields (the
                            newer, more flexible sibling of `data`)
                • script  — an embedded Lua script returning true/false
                • time    — a start/stop time-of-day window (+ optional timezone)
              Two logical-operator knobs control combination:
                • WITHIN a category:  dataLogicalOperator / exprLogicalOperator (and/or)
                • ACROSS categories:  filtersLogicalOperator on the dependency (and/or)
              An event only "passes" a dependency once every filter category it
              defines evaluates true under these operators.

          ⚠️ GOTCHA — comparator is `=`, not `==`
              DataFilter comparator values are: >=  >  =  !=  <  <=
              A single `=` means equality — programmers instinctively type `==` and
              get a validation error or a silently-non-matching filter.

          📐 YAML ANATOMY — verified Sensor skeleton (from real Argo Events example)
              apiVersion: argoproj.io/v1alpha1
              kind: Sensor
              metadata:
                name: easypj-push-sensor
              spec:
                template:
                  serviceAccountName: easypj-trigger-sa   #<- ONLY needed for k8s/argoWorkflow triggers
                dependencies:
                  - name: push-dep
                    eventSourceName: easypj-github-webhook
                    eventName: easypj-push                #<- must match the EventSource's map KEY
                    filters:
                      data:
                        - path: body.ref
                          type: string
                          value: ["refs/heads/main"]
                          comparator: "="
                triggers:
                  - template:
                      name: easypj-build-trigger
                      k8s:
                        operation: create
                        source:
                          resource:                        #<- full embedded Workflow manifest
                            apiVersion: argoproj.io/v1alpha1
                            kind: Workflow
                            metadata: { generateName: easypj-build- }
                            spec: { entrypoint: build, ... }
                        parameters:
                          - src:
                              dependencyName: push-dep
                              dataKey: body.head_commit.id  #<- commit SHA from payload
                            dest: spec.arguments.parameters.0.value

          🧭 DECISION GUIDE — `k8s` trigger vs `argoWorkflow` trigger
              k8s (StandardK8STrigger)   → generic: create/patch/update/delete ANY
                                           K8s resource, one-shot. Use for "spin up
                                           a new Workflow from scratch."
              argoWorkflow (ArgoWorkflowTrigger) → Workflow-lifecycle aware:
                                           operation ∈ {submit, submit-from, suspend,
                                           resubmit, retry, resume, terminate, stop}
                                           (default submit). Use for "resume a
                                           suspended pipeline" or "retry the failed
                                           nightly build" — things k8s can't express.
              🎯 Full 14-type trigger roster: k8s, argoWorkflow, http, awsLambda,
              kafka, nats, slack, openWhisk, log, azureEventHubs, azureServiceBus,
              pulsar, custom (gRPC), email. CAPA tests k8s + argoWorkflow hardest;
              the rest are 🏢 Daily-Work breadth.

          ⚠️ GOTCHA — the Sensor needs its OWN RBAC
              `spec.template.serviceAccountName` is required only for `k8s`/
              `argoWorkflow` triggers. Minimum RBAC: `create`+`list` on
              workflows.argoproj.io to submit; `update`+`get` to resubmit/retry/
              resume/suspend. Missing RBAC does NOT look like a filter problem —
              the EventSource/Sensor logs show the event arrived and the filter
              matched, but the trigger call fails with a Forbidden error. This is
              the #1 real-world "my event fired but nothing happened" cause.

    18.4  Parameterization in Triggers
          - Passing event data to workflow parameters
          - Transforming event payloads
          - Example: GitHub commit SHA → workflow input parameter
          [CODE EXAMPLE: Sensor with parameterized workflow trigger]
          📐 ANATOMY CALLOUT — the full TriggerParameter shape
              parameters:
                - src:
                    dependencyName: push-dep      # which dependency's event to read
                    dataKey: body.head_commit.id  # dot-path into the event JSON
                    dataTemplate: "{{ .Input.body.head_commit.id }}"  # optional Go-template transform
                    value: "unknown-sha"          # optional static fallback if path resolves empty
                  dest: spec.arguments.parameters.0.value
                    # dest also accepts an operation for the destination, e.g.
                    # concatenating instead of overwriting (append) — useful for
                    # building a URL or tag string from multiple event fields.

          ⚠️ GOTCHA — the `body.` prefix
              Most EventSource payloads wrap the actual HTTP/event data under a
              `body` key (that's what the receiving webhook/message literally
              contained). Writing `dataKey: head_commit.id` instead of
              `dataKey: body.head_commit.id` is the #1 reason parameterization
              "does nothing" — always confirm the exact payload shape from the
              Sensor pod's debug log (LOG_LEVEL=debug) before assuming the path.


    [G] UNDER THE HOOD 🔧  — How a Sensor really evaluates a match
    ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
    1. EventBus (NATS) delivers a CloudEvent to every Sensor subscribed to
       that EventSource+eventName pair.
    2. For EACH dependency, the Sensor evaluates up to 5 filter categories
       (data/context/exprs/script/time) independently, combining multiple
       filters WITHIN a category via dataLogicalOperator/exprLogicalOperator
       (default AND), then combining the categories THEMSELVES via
       filtersLogicalOperator (default AND).
    3. Once enough dependencies have "passed," the Sensor evaluates each
       trigger's optional `conditions` string (a boolean expression over
       dependency NAMEs, e.g. "push-dep && s3-dep") — this is how ONE Sensor
       with multiple dependencies can drive MULTIPLE triggers, each firing on
       a different combination of events (fan-in/fan-out inside one object).
    4. The trigger fires using the Sensor's own ServiceAccount (k8s/
       argoWorkflow triggers only) — RBAC failures happen HERE, after the
       filter already matched, which is why they look like "nothing happened"
       rather than "filter rejected."
    5. Optional `retryStrategy` (steps/duration/factor/jitter — exponential
       backoff) retries a failed trigger call; optional `policy` asserts the
       created resource actually reached a successful state before the
       Sensor considers the round complete.

    [H] KEY TAKEAWAYS 🔑
    ─────────────────────
      ✅ An EventSource's config is a MAP — the key you choose under
         spec.webhook / spec.calendar / etc. becomes the `eventName` a Sensor
         dependency must reference exactly.
      ✅ Filters have 5 categories (data, context, exprs, script, time), not
         just "data and context" — each solves a different matching problem.
      ✅ Comparator is `=`, never `==`.
      ✅ `k8s` trigger = generic one-shot resource create/patch/update/delete;
         `argoWorkflow` trigger = Workflow-lifecycle aware (submit/resubmit/
         retry/resume/suspend/terminate/stop).
      ✅ Parameterization flows src.dataKey (event JSON, usually `body.…`) →
         dest (dot/index path into the triggered resource).
      🎯 CAPA relevance: 18.3's dependency/filter/trigger anatomy is the
         single densest source of Events-domain exam questions in this guide.

    [I] EXAM GOTCHAS ⚠️
    ─────────────────────
      1. Comparator syntax is `=` not `==` (DataFilter).
      2. The S3 EventSource field is `minio`, not `s3`.
      3. `dataKey` paths usually need a `body.` prefix — the raw HTTP/event
         payload is nested under `body` in most EventSource types.
      4. Sensor RBAC is invisible until the trigger fires — a missing
         ServiceAccount/Role shows up as a Forbidden error in the Sensor pod
         log, not a filter mismatch.
      5. `k8s` and `argoWorkflow` triggers have DIFFERENT `operation` enums —
         don't assume `resubmit`/`retry` work under a `k8s` trigger.
      6. EventSource map key ≠ EventSource object name. A Sensor's
         `eventName` must match the inner map key (e.g. `easypj-push`), not
         `metadata.name` of the EventSource.

    [J] SPEED TIPS ⚡
    ───────────────────
      • `kubectl port-forward svc/<name>-eventsource-svc <port>:<port>` to
        test a webhook locally before wiring a real Ingress.
      • Set `LOG_LEVEL: debug` on the EventSource or Sensor container to see
        the RAW payload shape before writing `dataKey` paths — stop guessing.
      • Start every new Sensor with a `log` trigger (writes the matched event
        to the Sensor's own log, no side effects) to prove filters match
        before wiring the real k8s/argoWorkflow trigger.

    [K] COMMANDS TO REMEMBER 🔑
    ─────────────────────────────
      kubectl get eventsource,sensor -n argo-events
      kubectl logs -n argo-events <eventsource-pod>       # confirm event received
      kubectl logs -n argo-events <sensor-pod>             # confirm filter + trigger
      kubectl describe sensor <name> -n argo-events        # check trigger status
      kubectl port-forward svc/<name>-eventsource-svc 12000:12000
      curl -X POST localhost:12000/<endpoint> -d '{"test":"payload"}'

    [L] PRACTICE DRILL 🧪
    ────────────────────────
    Timed, 15 minutes, no copy-paste:
      1. Write a webhook EventSource named `easypj-drill` listening on
         `:13000/drill`.
      2. Write a Sensor with one dependency on it, a data filter requiring
         `body.action == "test"` (remember: `=`, not `==`!), and a `log`
         trigger.
      3. `kubectl apply` both, curl the endpoint with `{"action":"test"}`,
         and confirm the Sensor log shows the trigger fired.
      4. Change the filter value so it does NOT match, re-curl, and confirm
         nothing fires — you should see the event arrive but no trigger log.

    [M] LAB QUESTIONS 🧪  (10, exam-style)
    ────────────────────────────────────────

    Q1. You need easypj's job-board to react to CSV files landing in an S3
        bucket. Which EventSource spec field do you configure — `s3:` or
        `minio:`?
    A1. `minio:` — the CRD field is literally named `minio`, not `s3`.
    Explanation: Argo Events' S3 support is implemented via the S3-compatible
    `minio-go` client under `spec.minio`, which also works against real AWS
    S3 by pointing its `endpoint` at `s3.amazonaws.com`. There is no `s3:`
    field in the schema — writing one produces a validation/apply error.

    Q2. A Sensor dependency has `filters.data[0].comparator: "=="`. What
        happens when you `kubectl apply` this Sensor?
    A2. It fails validation (or the filter silently never matches, depending
        on version) — `==` is not a valid Comparator value.
    Explanation: The documented Comparator enum is `>=  >  =  !=  <  <=`
    (plus `""` defaulting to `=`). Equality is a single `=`. This is one of
    the most common hand-written-YAML mistakes for developers coming from
    other languages.

    Q3. Name the 5 filter categories available on an EventDependency, and
        give one scenario each would uniquely solve.
    A3. data (payload field value match — e.g. `body.ref == refs/heads/main`),
        context (CloudEvents envelope metadata match — e.g. only events FROM
        a specific EventSource type, ignoring body), exprs (expr-lang boolean
        logic — e.g. `body.size > 1000 && body.type == "csv"` in one
        expression), script (embedded Lua — arbitrary custom logic no other
        filter can express), time (only accept events between 09:00-17:00 in
        a given timezone — e.g. business-hours-only triggers).
    Explanation: Filters compose via `dataLogicalOperator`/`exprLogicalOperator`
    (within-category) and `filtersLogicalOperator` (across-category), both
    AND/OR — five independent tools, not two.

    Q4. Your Sensor's `dependencies[].filters.data[].path` is
        `head_commit.id`, but the trigger's parameter never resolves. The
        EventSource is a GitHub webhook. What's the most likely bug?
    A4. Missing the `body.` prefix — it should be `body.head_commit.id`.
    Explanation: EventSource payloads wrap the actual received data under a
    `body` key. Confirm the exact shape by setting `LOG_LEVEL: debug` and
    reading the raw event before writing paths — this is the #1 real-world
    parameterization failure.

    Q5. You need to RESUME a Workflow that was suspended pending manual
        approval, triggered by an event. Which trigger type — `k8s` or
        `argoWorkflow` — and which `operation` value?
    A5. `argoWorkflow` trigger, `operation: resume`.
    Explanation: `StandardK8STrigger` (`k8s`) only supports
    create/patch/update/delete on an embedded resource — it has no concept of
    Workflow lifecycle. `ArgoWorkflowTrigger` (`argoWorkflow`) exposes
    Workflow-specific operations: submit, submit-from, suspend, resubmit,
    retry, resume, terminate, stop (default `submit`).

    Q6. Your event arrives, the Sensor log shows the filter matched, but no
        Workflow is created and the log shows a "Forbidden" error. What's
        missing?
    A6. RBAC — the Sensor's ServiceAccount (`spec.template.serviceAccountName`)
        lacks permission on `workflows.argoproj.io`.
    Explanation: `k8s`/`argoWorkflow` triggers execute AS the Sensor's own
    ServiceAccount. Minimum grants: `create`+`list` to submit; `update`+`get`
    to resubmit/retry/resume/suspend. Other trigger types (HTTP, Slack,
    Kafka, etc.) need no ServiceAccount at all — this is a k8s/argoWorkflow-
    specific gotcha.

    Q7. True or False: Argo Events currently ships exactly the ~20
        EventSource types shown in a typical reference table (Webhook,
        GitHub, S3, Kafka, Calendar, Resource, etc.), and that's the complete
        list.
    A7. False.
    Explanation: The current `EventSourceSpec` defines 32 distinct type
    fields. Beyond the common ~20, production-real types include Bitbucket
    Server, Redis Streams, Azure Queue Storage, Azure Service Bus, HDFS,
    NetApp StorageGrid, Generic (custom gRPC), Gerrit, AlibabaCloud MNS,
    SFTP, NSQ, and MQTT.

    Q8. You want easypj's nightly DB backup Workflow to run every night at
        2 AM Eastern, skipping US federal holidays you list explicitly. Which
        EventSource fields do you need?
    A8. `spec.calendar.<name>.schedule: "0 2 * * *"`,
        `timezone: "America/New_York"`, and `exclusionDates` listing each
        holiday in `EXDATE:YYYYMMDDTHHMMSSZ` format.
    Explanation: Calendar EventSources support `schedule` (cron) OR
    `interval` (duration, mutually exclusive), plus optional `timezone` and
    `exclusionDates` for date-specific skips — exactly the fields needed for
    a "every night except holidays" backup trigger.

    Q9. A Sensor has TWO dependencies (`github-dep`, `s3-dep`) and needs
        trigger A to fire on `github-dep` alone, and trigger B to fire only
        when BOTH have occurred. What field enables per-trigger gating like
        this?
    A9. Each `triggers[].conditions` field — a boolean string expression over
        dependency names, e.g. trigger A: `conditions: "github-dep"`;
        trigger B: `conditions: "github-dep && s3-dep"`.
    Explanation: A single Sensor can hold multiple dependencies and multiple
    triggers; `conditions` on the TRIGGER (not the dependency) is what lets
    different triggers respond to different combinations of the same
    dependency set — true fan-in/fan-out logic inside one object.

    Q10. You're building a webhook EventSource named `easypj-hook` with an
         inner map key `push-event`. What `eventName` must the consuming
         Sensor's dependency use, and what determines the auto-created
         Service's name?
    A10. `eventName: push-event` (the inner map KEY, not `easypj-hook`, the
         `metadata.name`). The Service is auto-created as
         `easypj-hook-eventsource-svc` (`<EventSource metadata.name>-eventsource-svc`).
    Explanation: Two different names are in play and commonly confused: the
    EventSource's `metadata.name` identifies the OBJECT (used in
    `eventSourceName` and the Service naming convention); the inner
    `spec.webhook.<key>` identifies the specific EVENT (used in `eventName`).
    Mixing them up is a common reason a Sensor "never sees" an event that is
    actually arriving fine.

    [N] VISUAL SUMMARY
    ────────────────────
      ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
      │  CHAPTER 18 IN ONE PICTURE                                      │
      │                                                                  │
      │  spec.webhook.push-event ──"eventName: push-event"──> dependency │
      │       (EventSource)                                   (Sensor)  │
      │                                                            │      │
      │                                          filters: data/context/  │
      │                                          exprs/script/time       │
      │                                          (5 categories, 2-tier   │
      │                                           AND/OR logic)          │
      │                                                            │      │
      │                                          conditions: "dep-a"     │
      │                                          (which trigger fires)   │
      │                                                            v      │
      │                                          triggers[].template.    │
      │                                          k8s OR argoWorkflow     │
      │                                          (RBAC via Sensor's SA)  │
      │                                                            │      │
      │                                          parameters: src.dataKey │
      │                                          (body.…) → dest (path)  │
      │                                                            v      │
      │                                          Workflow created/       │
      │                                          submitted/resumed       │
      └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
      Prose recap: One EventSource key becomes one Sensor eventName. The
      Sensor's filters (5 categories, AND/OR at two levels) decide IF an
      event counts; per-trigger `conditions` decide WHICH trigger(s) fire;
      the trigger's `k8s`/`argoWorkflow` template (running as the Sensor's
      own RBAC-scoped ServiceAccount) decides WHAT happens; and
      `parameters[].src.dataKey → dest` decides what real event data gets
      injected into that action. Every failure in this chapter traces back
      to one of these four links being subtly wrong.


Chapter 19: Argo Events for easypj — Real-World Automation
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
    🟡 INTERMEDIATE · ⏱️ ~2.5 hours · 🎯 CAPA domain: Argo Events 12% (applied) · 🏢 Daily-Work: high

    [B] WHY THIS MATTERS
    ────────────────────
    Chapters 17-18 gave you the vocabulary and the anatomy: EventSource,
    EventBus, Sensor, Trigger, filters, parameterization. This chapter is
    where that vocabulary earns its keep — four real automations that a
    GitOps team actually runs against easypj/jobpost every day: deploy the
    instant someone pushes to main, import a CSV of new listings the
    instant marketing drops one in a bucket, run nightly maintenance
    without a fragile CronJob, and react the instant a new JobPost object
    lands in the cluster. Each of these four maps directly to the CAPA
    exam's own "know these best" list from Ch18.1 (Webhook/GitHub, S3,
    Calendar, Resource) — so this chapter is both your best exam-prep
    chapter for Events AND the most reusable one at your next job.

    ┌───────────────────────────────┬───────────────────────────────┐
    │ BEFORE CHAPTER 19              │ AFTER CHAPTER 19                │
    ├───────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────┤
    │ Know what EventSource/Sensor   │ Have shipped 4 working         │
    │ ARE, in the abstract           │ EventSource+Sensor pairs       │
    │ "Push to deploy" is manual     │ git push → live in dev,        │
    │                                 │ zero manual steps              │
    │ CSV imports = someone's        │ Upload to bucket → validated → │
    │ Tuesday afternoon               │ imported → Slack notified      │
    │ Nightly jobs = a brittle       │ Calendar EventSource with      │
    │ CronJob nobody trusts           │ timezone + catch-up            │
    │ New listings only enter via    │ A JobPost CRD triggers          │
    │ the Django admin UI             │ auto-validation on `kubectl    │
    │                                 │ apply`                         │
    └───────────────────────────────┴───────────────────────────────┘

    [C] LEARNING OBJECTIVES — 🎯 By the end of this chapter you can:
    ────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
      1. Wire a GitHub push webhook (EventSource) through a Sensor into
         an Argo Workflow that clones, tests, builds, and updates
         easypj's GitOps repo for Argo CD to auto-sync.
      2. Configure the S3-compatible (`minio`) EventSource to detect a
         CSV upload and drive an ETL Workflow that imports it into
         easypj's PostgreSQL job-post database — and explain when you'd
         need the AWS-SNS EventSource instead for real AWS S3.
      3. Replace a fragile CronJob with a Calendar EventSource for
         easypj's nightly backup and weekly report, using timezone and
         catch-up correctly.
      4. Watch a custom `JobPost` Kubernetes resource with the Resource
         EventSource and trigger a validation/enrichment Workflow the
         moment one is created — including the RBAC it requires.
      5. Diagnose the top failure mode for each of the four EventSource
         types (unreachable webhook, wrong bucket protocol, timezone
         drift, missing `list` RBAC verb).
      6. Read Sensor `filters.data` and trigger `parameters` YAML fast
         enough to answer CAPA's "will this fire?" scenario questions.

    [D] CAPA RELEVANCE
    ───────────────────
    Argo Events is 12% of the exam, and Ch18.1's own reference table
    flags Webhook/GitHub, S3, Calendar, and Resource as the 4 types (of
    ~17) to "know best" — this chapter is built as exactly those 4, each
    demonstrated end-to-end on easypj. Ch17-18 taught the concepts CAPA
    grades directly (EventSource/Sensor/EventBus definitions, filter
    syntax, trigger types); Ch19 is where CAPA's applied questions live
    — "given this EventSource+Sensor pair, what fires and what doesn't."
    Expect scenario questions that hinge on exactly the traps called out
    here: EventSource-level `events` vs Sensor-level `filters.data`,
    RBAC verbs for Resource watches, and Calendar's schedule/interval
    precedence.

    [E] SECTION MAP — you are here
    ────────────────────────────────
    ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
    │        19.1 Auto-Deploy on Git Push (Webhook/GitHub)               │
    │        push → EventSource → Sensor → Workflow → GitOps → CD sync   │
    │                            │                                       │
    │        19.2 S3-Triggered Data Processing (minio/S3)                │
    │        CSV upload → EventSource → Sensor → ETL Workflow → Postgres │
    │                            │                                       │
    │        19.3 Scheduled Maintenance (Calendar)                       │
    │        cron/interval → EventSource → Sensor → backup/report Wf     │
    │                            │                                       │

    │        kubectl apply JobPost CR → EventSource → Sensor → validate  │
    │                            │                                       │
    │                            v                                       │
    │              all 4 of CAPA's "know best" EventSource types,        │
    │              each demonstrated live on easypj/jobpost              │
    └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
    PROSE RECAP: each of the 4 sections is independent (you can read them
    in any order) but shares one shape: an external trigger → an
    EventSource that speaks that trigger's protocol → a Sensor that
    filters and parameterizes → an Argo Workflow that does the real work.
    19.1 and 19.2 are push-driven (webhook), 19.3 is time-driven
    (no external actor at all), and 19.4 is watch-driven (K8s API
    Informer) — Under the Hood below contrasts all three delivery
    mechanisms.

    19.1  Auto-Deploy on Git Push
          - GitHub webhook → EventSource → Sensor → Argo Workflow
          - Workflow: Clone → Test → Build → Update GitOps repo → Argo CD syncs
          [COMPLETE YAML: End-to-end push-to-deploy automation]
          [YAML: EventSource + Sensor for easypj push-to-deploy]
          apiVersion: argoproj.io/v1alpha1
          kind: EventSource
          metadata:
            name: easypj-github
            namespace: argo-events
          spec:
            service:
              ports:
                - name: webhook
                  port: 12000
                  targetPort: 12000
            github:
              push-to-main:
                repositories:
                  - owner: yourorg
                    names: ["easypj"]
                webhook:
                  endpoint: /push
                  port: "12000"
                  method: POST
                  url: https://events.easypj.example.com/push   # must be reachable from GitHub
                events: ["push"]
                apiToken:                      # lets Argo Events register the hook via GitHub's API
                  name: github-access
                  key: token
                insecure: false
                active: true
                contentType: json
          ---
          apiVersion: argoproj.io/v1alpha1
          kind: Sensor
          metadata:
            name: easypj-deploy-sensor
            namespace: argo-events
          spec:
            template:
              serviceAccountName: operate-workflow-sa
            dependencies:
              - name: push-main
                eventSourceName: easypj-github
                eventName: push-to-main
                filters:
                  data:
                    - path: body.ref
                      type: string
                      value: ["refs/heads/main"]
            triggers:
              - template:
                  name: submit-ci-cd
                  argoWorkflow:
                    operation: submit
                    source:
                      resource:
                        apiVersion: argoproj.io/v1alpha1
                        kind: Workflow
                        metadata:
                          generateName: easypj-push-
                        spec:
                          workflowTemplateRef:
                            name: easypj-ci-cd        # Clone->Test->Build->Push->Update GitOps repo
                          arguments:
                            parameters:
                              - name: commit-sha
                              - name: commit-msg
                    parameters:
                      - src:
                          dependencyName: push-main
                          dataTemplate: "{{ .Input.body.head_commit.id | substr 0 7 }}"
                        dest: spec.arguments.parameters.0.value
                      - src:
                          dependencyName: push-main
                          dataKey: body.head_commit.message
                        dest: spec.arguments.parameters.1.value
                retryStrategy:
                  steps: 3

          ⚠️ GOTCHA: `events: ["push"]` on the EventSource is what GitHub
          actually sends; the Sensor's `filters.data` on `body.ref` is a
          SECOND, independent narrowing — a push to a feature branch still
          arrives at the EventSource and is only dropped at the Sensor.
          Conflating "GitHub-side subscription" with "Sensor-side filter"
          is the #1 mistake here.

    19.2  S3-Triggered Data Processing
          - User uploads CSV to S3 → EventSource detects → Sensor triggers
          - Workflow: Download CSV → Transform → Load to easypj database
          [WORKFLOW DIAGRAM: S3 upload to database pipeline]
          ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
          │  S3-TRIGGERED DATA PIPELINE for easypj                     │
          │                                                            │
          │  ┌──────────┐     ┌──────────────┐     ┌──────────────┐  │
          │  │ 👤 User  │────▶│  ☁️ AWS S3    │────▶│ 🔔 Argo      │  │
          │  │ Uploads  │     │  Bucket:      │     │    Events    │  │
          │  │ jobpost  │     │  easypj-data  │     │  S3 EventSrc │  │
          │  │ .csv     │     │  ObjectCreated│     │              │  │
          │  └──────────┘     └──────────────┘     └──────┬───────┘  │
          │                                               │           │
          │                                               ▼           │
          │                                        ┌──────────────┐  │
          │                                        │ 📡 EventBus  │  │
          │                                        │   (NATS)     │  │
          │                                        └──────┬───────┘  │
          │                                               │           │
          │                                               ▼           │
          │                                        ┌──────────────┐  │
          │                                        │ 🎯 Sensor    │  │
          │                                        │ Filter:      │  │
          │                                        │ *.csv only   │  │
          │                                        │ Transform:   │  │
          │                                        │ extract bucket│  │
          │                                        │ + key        │  │
          │                                        └──────┬───────┘  │
          │                                               │           │
          │                                               ▼           │
          │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
          │  │  🔄 ARGO WORKFLOW — ETL Pipeline                    │  │
          │  │                                                    │  │
          │  │  Step 1: Download CSV from S3                      │  │
          │  │    → artifact: /tmp/jobpost-data.csv               │  │
          │  │                                                    │  │
          │  │  Step 2: Validate + Transform CSV                  │  │
          │  │    → Python script: clean, normalize, validate     │  │
          │  │    → artifact: /tmp/jobpost-clean.json             │  │
          │  │                                                    │  │
          │  │  Step 3: Load into easypj PostgreSQL               │  │
          │  │    → python manage.py import_jobs /tmp/jobpost-clean.json│
          │  │                                                    │  │
          │  │  Step 4: Send notification                         │  │
          │  │    → Slack: "✅ 150 new job posts imported!"       │  │
          │  │                                                    │  │
          │  │  RESULT: CSV → Clean → DB → Notify                 │  │
          │  │  TRIGGER: Automatic on S3 upload                    │  │
          │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
          └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
          📖 KEY TERM — Argo Events has no EventSource literally named "S3."
          The CRD field is `spec.minio`, built on the S3-compatible API. It
          works out of the box against a self-hosted MinIO bucket (what
          easypj's dev/staging lab uses for `easypj-data`). ⚠️ GOTCHA: real
          AWS S3 does NOT speak this protocol — S3 can only notify Lambda/
          SNS/SQS, never an arbitrary webhook. 🏢 Daily-Work: in a production
          AWS deployment, wire `S3 bucket → SNS topic → Argo Events AWS-SNS
          EventSource` instead; keep `minio` for in-cluster/self-hosted
          object storage.

          [YAML: minio EventSource + import Sensor]
          apiVersion: argoproj.io/v1alpha1
          kind: EventSource
          metadata:
            name: easypj-s3
            namespace: argo-events
          spec:
            minio:
              csv-upload:
                bucket:
                  name: easypj-data
                endpoint: minio-service.argo-events:9000   # in-cluster MinIO
                events: ["s3:ObjectCreated:Put"]
                filter:
                  suffix: ".csv"
                insecure: true
                accessKey:
                  name: easypj-data-minio-creds
                  key: accesskey
                secretKey:
                  name: easypj-data-minio-creds
                  key: secretkey
          ---
          apiVersion: argoproj.io/v1alpha1
          kind: Sensor
          metadata:
            name: easypj-import-sensor
            namespace: argo-events
          spec:
            template:
              serviceAccountName: operate-workflow-sa
            dependencies:
              - name: csv-dep
                eventSourceName: easypj-s3
                eventName: csv-upload
            triggers:
              - template:
                  name: import-csv-wf
                  argoWorkflow:
                    operation: submit
                    source:
                      resource:
                        apiVersion: argoproj.io/v1alpha1
                        kind: Workflow
                        metadata:
                          generateName: jobpost-import-
                        spec:
                          workflowTemplateRef:
                            name: easypj-csv-import
                          arguments:
                            parameters:
                              - name: bucket
                              - name: key
                    parameters:
                      - src:
                          dependencyName: csv-dep
                          dataKey: body
                        dest: spec.arguments.parameters.0.value
                retryStrategy:
                  steps: 2
          # ⚠️ GOTCHA: `filter.suffix` is a SIBLING of `bucket:`, not nested
          # inside it — nesting it under bucket: means the API ignores it
          # (every upload triggers the pipeline, not just .csv files).

    19.3  Scheduled Maintenance with Calendar Events
          - Calendar EventSource triggers nightly backup workflow
          - Weekly report generation
          [CRON ALTERNATIVE: Calendar-based scheduling]
          [YAML: Calendar EventSource — nightly backup + weekly report]
          apiVersion: argoproj.io/v1alpha1
          kind: EventSource
          metadata:
            name: easypj-calendar
            namespace: argo-events
          spec:
            calendar:
              nightly-backup:
                schedule: "0 2 * * *"        # 02:00 every day
                timezone: "America/New_York"
              weekly-report:
                schedule: "0 6 * * 1"        # 06:00 every Monday
                timezone: "America/New_York"
                persistence:
                  catchup:
                    enabled: true            # replay a missed Monday if the cluster was down
                    maxDuration: "10m"
                  configMap:
                    createIfNotExist: true
                    name: easypj-calendar-catchup

          ⚠️ GOTCHA: `schedule` beats `interval` beats `recurrence` if more
          than one is set — SILENTLY, no validation error. Also: unlike a
          bare CronJob, `timezone` is a first-class field on Calendar
          EventSources — a real advantage over hand-rolling a K8s CronJob
          for the same job, plus `persistence.catchup` replays a missed
          fire after downtime (backed by a ConfigMap), which CronJob has no
          equivalent for.

    19.4  Kubernetes Resource Watch Automation
          - Watch for new JobPost resources
          - Auto-validate and enrich new job posts
          [EXAMPLE: Resource EventSource for easypj objects]
          [YAML: JobPost CRD watch — validate + import on kubectl apply]
          apiVersion: rbac.authorization.k8s.io/v1
          kind: Role
          metadata:
            name: jobpost-watcher-role
            namespace: easypj-content
          rules:
            - apiGroups: ["easypj.io"]
              resources: ["jobposts"]
              verbs: ["get", "list", "watch"]      # list+watch = the Informer's ListAndWatch
          ---
          apiVersion: argoproj.io/v1alpha1
          kind: EventSource
          metadata:
            name: jobpost-crd-watch
            namespace: argo-events
          spec:
            template:
              serviceAccountName: jobpost-watcher-sa
            resource:
              jobpost-created:
                namespace: easypj-content
                group: easypj.io
                version: v1
                resource: jobposts
                eventTypes: ["ADD"]
                filter:
                  afterStart: true            # ignore JobPosts that already existed before boot
          ---
          apiVersion: argoproj.io/v1alpha1
          kind: Sensor
          metadata:
            name: jobpost-validate-sensor
            namespace: argo-events
          spec:
            template: {serviceAccountName: operate-workflow-sa}
            dependencies:
              - name: new-jobpost
                eventSourceName: jobpost-crd-watch
                eventName: jobpost-created
            triggers:
              - template:
                  name: validate-and-import
                  argoWorkflow:
                    operation: submit
                    source:
                      resource:
                        apiVersion: argoproj.io/v1alpha1
                        kind: Workflow
                        metadata: {generateName: jobpost-validate-}
                        spec:
                          entrypoint: validate-import
                          arguments:
                            parameters: [{name: cr-name}, {name: cr-namespace}]
                          templates:
                            - name: validate-import
                              inputs:
                                parameters: [{name: cr-name}, {name: cr-namespace}]
                              container:
                                image: registry.example.com/easypj-tools:v1.0
                                command: [python, manage.py]
                                args: ["import_jobpost_cr", "--name={{inputs.parameters.cr-name}}",
                                       "--namespace={{inputs.parameters.cr-namespace}}"]
                    parameters:
                      - {src: {dependencyName: new-jobpost, dataKey: body.metadata.name}, dest: spec.arguments.parameters.0.value}
                      - {src: {dependencyName: new-jobpost, dataKey: body.metadata.namespace}, dest: spec.arguments.parameters.1.value}

          📖 KEY TERM — the Resource EventSource's dispatched event nests
          the ENTIRE watched Kubernetes object under `data.body` — so any
          field you want is addressed as `body.<path-into-the-object>`
          (e.g. `body.metadata.name`, `body.spec.title`), exactly like
          navigating the object's own JSON.

          ⚠️ GOTCHA: granting only `watch` (not `list`) on the
          ServiceAccount breaks the underlying Informer's initial LIST —
          missing `list` is a common real bug that leaves the EventSource
          silently never firing, since the informer's sync never completes.


    [G] UNDER THE HOOD 🔧 — Three Different Ways an "Event" Actually Arrives
    ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
    It's tempting to assume every EventSource "polls." It doesn't — this
    chapter's four sections use THREE distinct delivery mechanisms:

        PUSH (19.1, 19.2): the EventSource runs an HTTP server inside its
        Pod (`<name>-eventsource-svc`); GitHub/MinIO calls IN. Nothing
        happens between calls — if the caller never fires, the
        EventSource is idle. This is why 19.1/19.2 need an Ingress: the
        cluster must be reachable FROM the outside, not the other way
        around.

        TIMER (19.3): the EventSource process itself holds a Go
        time.Ticker (interval) or a cron scheduler (schedule) and
        generates the event internally — no external caller at all. This
        is the only one of the four with zero network dependency on
        anything outside the cluster.

        WATCH (19.4): the EventSource opens a long-lived K8s API
        connection using client-go's Informer (ListWatch) against the
        `easypj.io/v1 jobposts` endpoint. On boot it does one `LIST` (a
        full snapshot — this is why `list` RBAC is mandatory, not
        optional) then holds a `WATCH` (a streaming diff feed). Every ADD
        that arrives on that stream becomes an event. `filter.afterStart`
        works by comparing each object's `creationTimestamp` against the
        EventSource Pod's own start time, discarding anything older.

    kubectl apply -f jobpost.yaml
          |
          v
    kube-apiserver stores it, bumps resourceVersion
          |
          v
    Resource EventSource's Informer (LIST+WATCH on jobposts.easypj.io)
    already has a long-lived watch open --> diff event fires instantly
          |
          v
    EventSource publishes to EventBus (NATS) --> Sensor's dependency
    matches --> parameters resolved from data.body --> Workflow submitted

    PROSE RECAP: push = someone calls you, timer = you call yourself,
    watch = you keep a standing connection to etcd's change feed via the
    apiserver. All three end the same way — an event lands on the shared
    EventBus and every Sensor with a matching `dependencies[].eventName`
    wakes up.

    [H] KEY TAKEAWAYS 🔑
    ─────────────────────
    - The CRD field for S3-style triggers is `spec.minio`, not `spec.s3`
      — and it is officially MinIO-only; real AWS S3 needs the AWS-SNS
      EventSource instead.
    - EventSource-level `events`/`eventTypes` decides what gets INTO the
      EventBus at all; Sensor-level `filters.data` decides what actually
      FIRES a trigger. They are two independent gates.
    - Resource EventSource RBAC needs `get`, `list`, AND `watch` — `list`
      backs the Informer's initial sync; skip it and nothing ever fires,
      with no error anywhere.
    - Calendar's `schedule` beats `interval` beats `recurrence` if more
      than one is set, silently.
    - The Resource EventSource's full watched object arrives under
      `data.body` — reference nested fields as `body.metadata.name`,
      `body.spec.title`, etc.
    - `argoWorkflow` trigger + `workflowTemplateRef` beats a generic
      `k8s` trigger with an inlined Workflow spec — don't duplicate a
      WorkflowTemplate you already built in Part 3/Ch20.
    🎯 Exam relevance: this chapter is the applied half of the 12%
       Events domain; expect "given this YAML, does it fire" questions
       built from exactly these five gotchas.

    [I] EXAM GOTCHAS ⚠️
    ─────────────────────
    1. Writing `spec.s3:` on an EventSource is not a real field — it is
       silently ignored by the API's OpenAPI validation the moment it's
       not `spec.minio:`; the object still creates with zero events ever
       fired, no error.
    2. Assuming a `minio` EventSource pointed at `s3.amazonaws.com` will
       receive live notifications the way it does against a real MinIO
       server — AWS S3 has no equivalent webhook-registration API; this
       silently never fires.
    3. Granting only `watch` (not `list`) on a Resource EventSource's
       ServiceAccount — the informer's initial LIST fails, and depending
       on client-go version this can either error loudly or just never
       populate, wasting exam time chasing a "why nothing happens" bug.
    4. Setting both `schedule` and `interval` on the same Calendar entry
       expecting the interval to also apply — only `schedule` runs.
    5. Reordering `spec.arguments.parameters` in a WorkflowTemplate
       without updating the Sensor's `dest: spec.arguments.parameters.N.value`
       index — the positional index silently binds to the wrong
       parameter instead of erroring.

    [J] SPEED TIPS ⚡
    ───────────────────
    1. Test a Sensor's filter/trigger logic without touching GitHub:
       `curl -X POST http://localhost:12000/push -H "X-GitHub-Event: push"
        -H "Content-Type: application/json" -d @sample-push-payload.json`
       against a `kubectl port-forward` of the EventSource's Service.
    2. `argo submit --from workflowtemplate/easypj-ci-cd -p commit-sha=abc1234`
       — manually replay what the Sensor would have submitted, for fast
       Workflow-side debugging isolated from the trigger path.
    3. `kubectl logs -n argo-events -l eventsource-name=<name> -f` and
       `-l sensor-name=<name> -f` — the fastest way to see whether an
       event even reached the EventBus vs. reached the Sensor but failed
       a filter.
    4. `kubectl get eventbus -n argo-events` before debugging anything
       else — a down EventBus is a silent black hole for every
       EventSource/Sensor pair in the namespace at once.

    [K] COMMANDS TO REMEMBER 🔑
    ─────────────────────────────
      Inspect:   kubectl get eventsource,sensor,eventbus -n argo-events ·
                 kubectl describe sensor <name> -n argo-events
      Logs:      kubectl logs -n argo-events -l eventsource-name=<name> -f ·
                 kubectl logs -n argo-events -l sensor-name=<name> -f
      Test:      kubectl port-forward svc/<name>-eventsource-svc 12000:12000 ·
                 curl -X POST localhost:12000/push -d @payload.json
      Replay:    argo submit --from workflowtemplate/<name> -p key=value
      RBAC:      kubectl auth can-i list jobposts.easypj.io
                 --as=system:serviceaccount:argo-events:jobpost-watcher-sa
                 -n easypj-content

    [L] PRACTICE DRILL 🧪 — ⏱️ 20-Minute Lab Stand-Up
    ────────────────────────────────────────────────────
      1. (5 min) Apply the 19.1 EventSource+Sensor; port-forward the
         webhook Service; curl a synthetic push payload with
         `X-GitHub-Event: push` and confirm a Workflow is submitted.
      2. (5 min) Apply the 19.3 Calendar EventSource with
         `interval: 30s` instead of the nightly schedule; watch
         `kubectl get workflows -w` fire every 30 seconds; then delete it.
      3. (5 min) Apply the 19.4 CRD + RBAC + EventSource + Sensor;
         `kubectl apply` a sample JobPost CR; confirm the validation
         Workflow is submitted within a couple seconds.
      4. (5 min) Break it: remove `list` from the Role's verbs, re-apply,
         re-create a JobPost CR, and confirm nothing fires — then fix it.
      SUCCESS CRITERIA: all 3 automations fire on demand; the RBAC break
      in step 4 reproduces the silent-failure gotcha from [I].3.

    [M] LAB QUESTIONS 🧪 (10)
    ───────────────────────────
    Q1. Your GitHub EventSource has `events: ["push"]` and no
        `filters.data` on the Sensor. A push lands on the `feature/x`
        branch. Does the Workflow submit?
    A1. Yes.
    Explanation: `events: ["push"]` only controls which GitHub event
    TYPES are delivered to the EventBus at all; with no Sensor-side
    `filters.data` on `body.ref`, every push to every branch reaches the
    trigger. Branch scoping must be added explicitly (see 19.1's
    `body.ref == refs/heads/main` filter).

    Q2. You configure `spec.minio` pointed at `s3.amazonaws.com:443` for
        easypj's real production S3 bucket, expecting CSV uploads to
        trigger the import Workflow. It never fires. Why?
    A2. The `minio` EventSource only works against MinIO (or a fully
        MinIO-notification-compatible) server; real AWS S3 has no
        equivalent webhook-registration mechanism.
    Explanation: per official docs, production AWS S3 notifications must
    route through the AWS-SNS EventSource (S3 bucket notification → SNS
    topic → Argo Events `sns` EventSource) — `minio` is documented as
    MinIO-exclusive.

    Q3. Your `minio` EventSource has `filter: {suffix: ".csv"}` under
        `bucket:`, but events never fire for any upload. What's wrong?
    A3. `filter` is a sibling of `bucket` (same indent level as
        `bucket:`, `endpoint:`, `events:`), not nested inside it.
    Explanation: this is a real, easy-to-make YAML-nesting mistake;
    nesting `filter` under `bucket:` means the API simply ignores it
    (unknown field), and — worse — with no filter at all every object
    upload (not just `.csv`) triggers the pipeline.

    Q4. A Resource EventSource's ServiceAccount has this Role:
        `verbs: ["watch"]` only, on `jobposts.easypj.io`. You
        `kubectl apply` a new JobPost. Nothing happens, no error
        anywhere. Diagnose and fix.
    A4. Missing `list` verb — add `verbs: ["get","list","watch"]`.
    Explanation: the underlying client-go Informer performs an initial
    LIST (full snapshot) before opening its WATCH stream; without `list`
    permission the Informer's sync fails, and depending on RBAC
    enforcement this can fail silently from the EventSource's own logs'
    perspective unless you check `kubectl logs` closely for a 403.

    Q5. Your Calendar EventSource entry has both `schedule: "0 2 * * *"`
        and `interval: "1h"` set. How often does it actually fire?
    A5. Once a day at 02:00 — `interval` is ignored.
    Explanation: CalendarEventSource precedence is schedule > interval >
    recurrence; only the highest-precedence field present is honored,
    with no validation warning about the ignored one.

    Q6. Design the Sensor `dataKey` to extract the JobPost CR's
        `spec.title` field from a Resource EventSource event, to pass
        into a Workflow parameter.
    A6. `dataKey: body.spec.title`
    Explanation: the Resource EventSource's dispatched event nests the
    ENTIRE watched Kubernetes object under `data.body` — so any field
    you want must be addressed as `body.<path-into-the-object>`, exactly
    like you'd navigate the object's own JSON.

    Q7. easypj's nightly backup Calendar EventSource has no `timezone`
        field. The cluster's nodes run UTC. Your team is in
        `America/New_York`. When does "0 2 * * *" actually fire, in
        local team time?
    A7. 02:00 UTC = 21:00 or 22:00 the previous evening in New York
        (depending on DST) — not 2 AM local.
    Explanation: `schedule` is evaluated in UTC unless `timezone` is
    explicitly set; this is the single most common "why did my backup
    run at a weird time" support ticket for Calendar EventSources.

    Q8. Two teammates both wire a Sensor trigger for the same
        WorkflowTemplate: one uses a generic `k8s: {operation: create}`
        trigger with the full Workflow spec inlined; the other uses
        `argoWorkflow: {operation: submit, source: {resource:
        {spec: {workflowTemplateRef: {name: easypj-ci-cd}}}}}`. Which is
        the better practice, and why?
    A8. The `argoWorkflow` + `workflowTemplateRef` version.
    Explanation: it references the WorkflowTemplate instead of
    duplicating its full step definition inside the Sensor — one source
    of truth; editing the CI pipeline later means touching Ch20's
    WorkflowTemplate once, not every Sensor that submits it.

    Q9. Your GitHub EventSource has no `apiToken` configured. What must
        you do differently to make the webhook actually work, versus
        the `apiToken`-configured version?
    A9. Manually create the webhook in GitHub's own UI/API pointing at
        the EventSource's exposed URL, and configure `webhookSecret`
        (not `apiToken`) so GitHub's HMAC signature can be validated.
    Explanation: `apiToken` lets Argo Events call GitHub's API to
        register the hook FOR you; without it, registration is a manual
        step and `webhookSecret` becomes mandatory for security since
        there's no other proof the POST actually came from GitHub.

    Q10. A Sensor trigger has:
         `parameters: [{src: {dependencyName: dep, dataKey: body.head_commit.title}, dest: spec.arguments.parameters.0.value}]`
         but the payload has no `head_commit.title` field (only
         `head_commit.message`). What happens when the trigger fires?
    A10. The trigger still fires; `parameters.0.value` is set to an
         empty string (or left at its template default) rather than
         raising an error.
    Explanation: a missing `dataKey` path is not a validation failure —
    Argo Events resolves what it can and silently no-ops the rest, which
    is why typos like Q3/Q10's (`title` vs `message`) are a classic
    silent-bug class worth defensive-checking in real pipelines.

    [N] VISUAL SUMMARY
    ────────────────────
    ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
    │  CHAPTER 19 IN ONE PICTURE — easypj's Automation Control Plane      │
    │                                                                    │
    │  🐙 git push main ──> 🔔 github EventSource ──> 🎯 Sensor          │
    │       (19.1)              (webhook, PUSH)         (filter: main)   │
    │                                                       │             │
    │  👤 upload .csv ────> 🔔 minio EventSource ────> 🎯 Sensor          │
    │       (19.2)              (webhook, PUSH)         (filter: .csv)   │
    │                                                       │             │
    │  ⏰ 02:00 daily ─────> 🔔 calendar EventSource ──> 🎯 Sensor          │
    │       (19.3)              (TIMER, no caller)     (no filter needed)│
    │                                                       │             │
    │  kubectl apply CR ──> 🔔 resource EventSource ───> 🎯 Sensor          │
    │       (19.4)              (WATCH, Informer)       (eventTypes:ADD) │
    │                                                       │             │
    │                              all 4 ────────────> 🔃 Argo Workflow  │
    │                                                  (build/import/    │
    │                                                   backup/validate) │
    └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
    PROSE RECAP: four different triggers, three different delivery
    mechanisms (push/timer/watch — see [G]), one consistent shape:
    EventSource speaks the external protocol, Sensor filters + extracts
    the data that matters, Workflow does the real work. Master this
    shape and every future EventSource type in the CAPA's 20+ list
    (Kafka, SQS, Redis, Slack, Stripe...) is the same pattern with a
    different `spec.<type>` block.

Chapter 20: End-to-End CI/CD Pipeline with Argo
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
    🟡🔴 INTERMEDIATE/ADVANCED · ⏱️ ~4 hours · 🎯 CAPA domain: ALL 4 (synthesis) · 🏢 Daily-Work: very high

    [B] WHY THIS MATTERS
    ─────────────────────
    Chapters 1-19 each gave you ONE Argo tool in isolation: Argo CD
    synced a single Application, Workflows ran a DAG by hand, Rollouts
    canaried a Deployment you `kubectl apply`'d yourself, Events fired
    a webhook into a Slack message. In the real world nobody runs any
    of these alone — a developer pushes code ONCE, and every single
    step from "git push" to "5% of prod traffic is safely on the new
    version" has to happen with no human touching a keyboard in
    between. This chapter is where the four separate skill-sets you've
    built become the ONE pipeline that is the actual reason GitOps
    shops adopt Argo in the first place — and it's the closest this
    guide gets to a full CAPA hands-on exam simulation.

    ┌─────────────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────────┐
    │  BEFORE (Ch 1-19, siloed)   │  AFTER (Ch 20, wired together)   │
    ├─────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────┤
    │  You manually run `argo     │  A GitHub push is the ONLY human  │
    │  submit` after every push   │  action; Events submits the       │
    │                              │  Workflow for you                │
    │  You manually `docker push` │  The Workflow builds, tests, and  │
    │  and edit the GitOps repo   │  commits the new image tag itself │
    │  You manually `argocd app   │  Argo CD's own reconcile loop     │
    │  sync` after editing YAML   │  picks up the Git change alone    │
    │  You manually promote a     │  Rollouts runs canary steps with  │
    │  canary or watch dashboards │  Prometheus-gated auto-promotion  │
    └─────────────────────────────┴──────────────────────────────────┘

    [C] LEARNING OBJECTIVES — 🎯 What you'll be able to DO
    ────────────────────────────────────────────────────
      - Draw and explain the full push-to-production data flow across
        all 4 Argo tools for easypj, naming which controller owns each
        hop and where Git is the handoff point.
      - Build the easypj-ci-pipeline WorkflowTemplate, the
        github-webhook EventSource, the easypj-ci-trigger Sensor, and
        wire them so a real push submits a real Workflow with zero
        manual `argo submit`.
      - Explain why the CI build step should use kaniko instead of a
        mounted Docker socket, and write that step correctly.
      - Apply at least 3 concrete pipeline-optimization techniques
        (parallel DAG, registry mirror, dependency-cache PVC) and
        justify each with a measurable before/after.
      - Expose, scrape, and query Prometheus metrics for all 4 Argo
        components, and write a PromQL query that answers "is my
        PIPELINE healthy" as distinct from "is easypj healthy."
      - Trace a single failure (e.g. a stuck AnalysisRun) back to the
        ONE specific controller responsible, without guessing.
