    20.1  The Complete Pipeline Architecture
          - Developer pushes code → GitHub webhook → Argo Events
          - Argo Workflows: Test → Build → Push image → Update GitOps repo
          - Argo CD detects Git change → Syncs to cluster
          - Argo Rollouts: Canary deploy with metric analysis
          [FULL PIPELINE DIAGRAM: All Argo components working together]
          ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
          │  END-TO-END CI/CD FOR easypj                               │
          │                                                            │
          │  👨‍💻 Dev Push       🔔 Events       🔄 Workflows            │
          │  ┌──────┐       ┌──────────┐    ┌──────────────────────┐  │
          │  │GitHub│──────▶│Webhook   │───▶│1. Clone repo         │  │
          │  │PR    │       │EventSrc  │    │2. Run tests          │  │
          │  └──────┘       └──────────┘    │3. Build Docker image │  │
          │                                 │4. Push to registry   │  │
          │                                 │5. Update GitOps repo │  │
          │                                 └──────────┬───────────┘  │
          │                                            │              │
          │  📦 Argo CD                                  │              │
          │  ┌──────────────────────┐                   │              │
          │  │Detects Git change    │◀──────────────────┘              │
          │  │Syncs easypj to:      │                                  │
          │  │  dev → immediate     │                                  │
          │  │  staging → after test│                                  │
          │  │  prod → canary       │                                  │
          │  └──────────┬───────────┘                                  │
          │             │                                              │
          │  🚀 Argo Rollouts                                          │
          │  ┌──────────────────────┐                                  │
          │  │Canary: 10% → 25% →   │                                  │
          │  │50% → 100% with       │                                  │
          │  │metric analysis       │                                  │
          │  │Auto-rollback on error│                                  │
          │  └──────────────────────┘                                  │
          └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
          PROSE RECAP: A push to team-easypj/easypj fires the github-webhook
          EventSource, which the easypj-ci-trigger Sensor turns into a run of
          the easypj-ci-pipeline WorkflowTemplate. That Workflow's LAST step
          commits a new image tag into the SEPARATE team-easypj/easypj-k8s
          GitOps repo — it never touches the cluster directly. Argo CD notices
          that commit on its own poll/webhook loop and applies the updated
          easypj-rollout object. Argo Rollouts — a THIRD independent
          controller — then drives that object's canary. Four controllers,
          zero direct API calls between them; Git and Kubernetes objects are
          the only "wires."

          ⚠️ GOTCHA: TWO GIT REPOS, NOT ONE
          easypj source code lives in team-easypj/easypj (what the WEBHOOK
          watches). Deployed manifests live in team-easypj/easypj-k8s (what
          ARGO CD watches). The CI Workflow's job is to bridge them — test/
          build the FIRST repo, then commit into the SECOND. Pointing Argo CD
          at the source repo (or the webhook at the GitOps repo) is the most
          common first-time pipeline misconfiguration.

          🧠 MENTAL MODEL: four independent thermostats, not one furnace.
          Each Argo tool runs its OWN watch/reconcile loop against its OWN
          CRDs (Ch 8.3's reconcile loop, Ch 14's Rollout loop, Ch 17's event
          loop). This pipeline diagram isn't one program calling the next —
          it's four thermostats, each reacting only to a change in ITS target
          temperature (state), chained by shared objects (Git commits, the
          Rollout resource) instead of function calls.

    20.2  Setting Up the Pipeline — Step by Step
          - Prerequisites: Argo CD, Workflows, Events, Rollouts installed
          - Creating the CI WorkflowTemplate
          - Creating the webhook EventSource and Sensor
          - Creating the Rollout + AnalysisTemplate
          - Testing end-to-end: push to GitHub → watch deployment
          [HANDS-ON: Complete pipeline setup with all YAML files]
          INSTALL ORDER MATTERS
          1. Argo CD          (no dependency on the others)
          2. Argo Rollouts     (needs Argo CD's Application CRD only if you
                                 deploy Rollouts via GitOps — install CRDs first)
          3. Argo Workflows     (independent; needs an artifact repo — MinIO/S3
                                 — configured before F.6's WorkflowTemplate runs)
          4. Argo Events:  EventBus → EventSource → Sensor, IN THAT ORDER.
             A Sensor created before its EventBus exists will sit "NotReady"
             forever (Appendix G, error #11) — this is the single most common
             Events setup failure and it's purely an ordering mistake.

          TRACE: ONE PUSH, START TO FINISH (dev environment)
          ┌───┬──────────────────────────────────────┬─────────────┐
          │ t │ WHAT HAPPENS                          │ OWNED BY    │
          ├───┼──────────────────────────────────────┼─────────────┤
          │0s │ git push main → GitHub webhook fires  │ GitHub      │
          │1s │ github-webhook EventSource receives   │ Argo Events │
          │1s │ easypj-ci-trigger Sensor matches,     │ Argo Events │
          │   │ submits Workflow from easypj-ci-      │             │
          │   │ pipeline WorkflowTemplate             │             │
          │~1m│ clone → parallel(unit,lint,SAST,      │ Argo        │
          │   │ integration) → build → push image     │ Workflows   │
          │~6m│ Workflow commits new tag to           │ Argo        │
          │   │ easypj-k8s                            │ Workflows   │
          │~9m│ argocd-repo-server/application-       │ Argo CD     │
          │   │ controller detects commit, syncs      │             │
          │~9m│ easypj-rollout updated → canary       │ Argo        │
          │   │ begins (dev: no canary, Recreate)     │ Rollouts    │
          └───┴──────────────────────────────────────┴─────────────┘
          Matches Ch 21.3's SLA targets: dev <2 min sync-side, staging <10 min
          total, prod <1 hour including the canary's pauses.

          ⚠️ GOTCHA: "Creating the CI WorkflowTemplate" step literally running
          `docker build` needs a Docker daemon the Workflow pod usually does
          NOT have. Mounting /var/run/docker.sock or running privileged
          docker:dind works but grants the build step root-equivalent access
          to the NODE — flagged as a real risk in Ch 22.3's hardening list.
          Production-correct: build with kaniko or rootless BuildKit instead,
          neither needs a daemon or --privileged:
            - name: build-image
              container:
                image: gcr.io/kaniko-project/executor:latest
                args:
                  - --dockerfile=Dockerfile
                  - --context=dir:///workspace/easypj
                  - --destination=registry.io/easypj:{{workflow.parameters.commit-sha}}
                  - --cache=true
                  - --cache-repo=registry.io/easypj/cache

    20.3  Pipeline Optimization
          - Caching Docker layers in Workflows
          - Parallel test execution
          - Image registry best practices
          [OPTIMIZATION TIPS: Speed up your pipeline]
          ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
          │  CI/CD PIPELINE OPTIMIZATION TECHNIQUES                     │
          │                                                            │
          │  1. ⚡ PARALLELIZE TESTS (DAG)                              │
          │     ┌──────────┐                                          │
          │     │  Clone   │  1 min                                   │
          │     └────┬─────┘                                          │
          │          │                                                │
          │     ┌────┼────┬────────────┐                               │
          │     │    │    │            │                               │
          │     ▼    ▼    ▼            ▼                               │
          │  ┌────┐┌────┐┌────┐   ┌──────────┐                       │
          │  │Unit││Lint││SAST│   │Integration│                       │
          │  │ 2m ││ 1m ││ 3m │   │   Tests   │                       │
          │  └────┘└────┘└────┘   │    5m     │                       │
          │                       └──────────┘                       │
          │  Total: 1m + max(2,1,3,5) = 6 min (not 1+2+1+3+5=12!)   │
          │                                                            │
          │  2. 🗜️ DOCKER LAYER CACHING                               │
          │     COPY requirements.txt .  ← Cache if unchanged         │
          │     RUN pip install ...       ← Only runs if reqs change  │
          │     COPY . .                  ← Only runs if code changes │
          │     Use --cache-from in docker build                      │
          │                                                            │
          │  3. 📦 IMAGE REGISTRY PROXIMITY                            │
          │     Pull from local registry (not Docker Hub)             │
          │     Use registry mirror/cache in cluster                  │
          │     Smaller base images = faster pulls                    │
          │     (python:3.12-slim vs python:3.12 = 10x size diff!)   │
          │                                                            │
          │  4. 🔄 SMART RETRY STRATEGIES                              │
          │     Don't retry on permanent failures (bad code)          │
          │     Retry on transient failures (network, registry)       │
          │     Limit retryAttempts to 3 with exponential backoff     │
          │                                                            │
          │  5. 💾 PERSISTENT VOLUME FOR DEPENDENCIES                  │
          │     Mount PVC at /root/.cache/pip                         │
          │     pip packages cached across workflows!                 │
          │     Reduces pip install from 2 min → 10 seconds           │
          └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
          📖 KEY TERM: with kaniko (20.2's build step), "layer caching" means
          --cache-repo, not --cache-from. Each unchanged layer is checked
          against a REMOTE registry cache (network round-trip per layer) —
          still a huge win over a full rebuild, but the win comes from
          SKIPPING the pip install layer entirely, not from local disk reuse.
          Order still matters exactly as shown: requirements.txt copied and
          installed BEFORE application code, so a code-only change never
          invalidates the pip layer.

          🏢 DAILY-WORK: "registry proximity" in practice means configuring a
          PULL-THROUGH CACHE / registry mirror in containerd (not just picking
          a geographically-close registry). On most managed K8s, this is a
          containerd `[plugins."io.containerd.grpc.v1.cri".registry.mirrors]`
          config pointing node pulls at an in-cluster or in-VPC mirror, so
          repeat pulls of unchanged base-image layers (python:3.12-slim) never
          leave the cluster's network at all.

          💡 KEY INSIGHT: don't tune blind — Argo Workflows exposes
          argo_workflows_workflowtemplate_runtime (execution duration by
          WorkflowTemplate) on the metrics endpoint set up in 20.4. Graph THAT
          before and after any optimization in this section — the "6 min vs
          12 min" DAG math above is the THEORY; the metric is the PROOF.

    20.4  Monitoring and Observability
          - Prometheus metrics for Argo components
          - Grafana dashboards for pipeline monitoring
          - Alerting on pipeline failures
          [DIAGRAM: Monitoring stack for Argo]
          [TABLE: Where each Argo component's metrics actually live]
          ┌──────────────┬──────┬────────────────────────────────────┐
          │ COMPONENT    │ PORT │ NOTE                                │
          ├──────────────┼──────┼────────────────────────────────────┤
          │ Argo CD ctlr │ 8082 │ Service ships by default            │
          │ Argo CD srv  │ 8083 │ Service ships by default            │
          │ Repo-server  │ 8084 │ Service ships by default            │
          │ Workflows    │ 9090 │ Service NOT created by default —    │
          │              │      │ you must add workflow-controller-   │
          │              │      │ metrics Service + enable in the     │
          │              │      │ workflow-controller-configmap       │
          │ Rollouts     │ 8090 │ On by default, zero config          │
          │ Events ctlrs │ 7777 │ eventsource-/sensor-/eventbus-      │
          │              │      │ controller each expose here          │
          └──────────────┴──────┴────────────────────────────────────┘
          Every row needs a matching Prometheus Operator ServiceMonitor (or
          prometheus.io/scrape annotations) to actually get scraped.

          easypj EXAMPLE — 3 dashboards, 3 different questions:
            App health (16.3's query, reused):
              sum(rate(http_errors_total{app="easypj"}[5m]))
              / sum(rate(http_requests_total{app="easypj"}[5m]))
            Pipeline health (NEW — is CI itself reliable?):
              sum(rate(argo_workflows_count{phase="Failed",
                workflowtemplate="easypj-ci-pipeline"}[1h]))
            Delivery lag (NEW — how long from push to live?):
              argo_workflows_workflowtemplate_runtime{
                workflowtemplate="easypj-ci-pipeline"}
          The first tells you if easypj is broken; the second and third tell
          you if your PIPELINE is broken — a distinction the exam tests via
          "which component is unhealthy" scenario questions.

          ⚠️ GOTCHA: Prometheus/Grafana (this section) alert on METRICS —
          Argo CD Notifications (Ch 6.3) alert on STATE TRANSITIONS
          (on-sync-failed, on-health-degraded). They're complementary, not
          redundant: Notifications tell #deployments a sync just failed in
          real time; Prometheus/Grafana tell you the error rate has been
          climbing for 20 minutes even though every individual sync "succeeded."
          Production pipelines wire BOTH — Ch 22.1's HA checklist assumes it.


    [G] UNDER THE HOOD 🔧 — Four controllers, zero direct calls
    ────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
    Trace what NEVER happens in this pipeline, because it's the
    part learners assume must happen and it doesn't:

    1. eventsource-controller runs a webhook HTTP server; on POST
       /push it publishes the payload onto the EventBus (NATS) —
       it does NOT know a Sensor, Workflow, or Argo CD exists.
    2. sensor-controller's Sensor pod subscribes to that EventBus
       subject, evaluates its filters (push to main), and calls the
       Kubernetes API to CREATE a Workflow object — it does NOT call
       the Workflows controller's API; there IS no such API. The
       Workflow object itself is the only "message."
    3. workflow-controller's normal watch loop (same loop from every
       Workflow you've ever run in Ch 9-13) picks up that new object
       because it matches its watch, not because anything "invoked"
       it. Its LAST DAG step does a plain `git commit && git push`
       against easypj-k8s using a mounted deploy key — a Git
       operation, not an Argo API call.
    4. argocd-repo-server's independent poll (or webhook, if you wired
       one to argocd-server — a SEPARATE webhook from step 1's) diffs
       easypj-k8s against the live cluster and finds the easypj-rollout
       object's image tag changed. application-controller applies it.
    5. rollout-controller's watch loop (same loop from Ch 14-16)
       reconciles that ONE Rollout object, running the canary steps
       and creating an AnalysisRun that polls Prometheus directly —
       Prometheus is scraping the LIVE easypj Pods, which have no
       awareness any of the previous 4 steps happened.

    MENTAL MODEL: this is 5 SEPARATE reconcile loops, chained ONLY by
    (a) the EventBus, (b) a Kubernetes object write, (c) a Git commit,
    (d) another Kubernetes object write. No component ever calls
    another component's API — this IS what "GitOps reconciliation
    pattern" (8.3, 21.1) means when you see it end to end.

    [H] KEY TAKEAWAYS 🔑
    ─────────────────────
    - Two Git repos: team-easypj/easypj (source, webhook target) and
      team-easypj/easypj-k8s (GitOps, Argo CD's target) — never confuse
      which component watches which.
    - Events → Workflows → Git → CD → Rollouts is a CHAIN of 4
      independent watch/reconcile loops, not one program.
    - `docker build` inside a Workflow pod needs a daemon it usually
      doesn't have — kaniko/BuildKit is the production-correct answer.
    - Argo CD's "Synced" status and Argo Rollouts' canary status are
      TWO DIFFERENT state machines on the SAME underlying object.
    - Each Argo component's metrics live on a different port (8082-84,
      9090, 8090, 7777) and only Workflows requires you to manually
      create its metrics Service.
    🎯 EXAM RELEVANCE: expect a scenario question that gives you a
    symptom (stuck AnalysisRun, Sensor never firing, image never
    updating) and asks which of the 4 controllers is at fault —
    that's exactly what [G]'s trace teaches you to isolate.

    [I] EXAM GOTCHAS ⚠️
    ─────────────────────
    1. Assuming a Sensor can trigger `argocd app sync` directly — it
       can trigger a Workflow (via a Kubernetes trigger), never CD.
    2. Pointing the webhook EventSource at easypj-k8s (the GitOps
       repo) instead of easypj (the source repo) — nothing to test
       or build lives in easypj-k8s.
    3. Creating a Sensor before its EventBus exists — it silently sits
       "NotReady" (Appendix G #11) instead of erroring loudly.
    4. Reading Argo CD "Synced" as "the canary finished" — it only
       means the Rollout OBJECT's spec was applied; check
       `kubectl argo rollouts get rollout` for actual canary progress.
    5. Forgetting Argo Workflows' metrics Service isn't installed by
       default (unlike CD and Rollouts) — 20.4's dashboard will show
       nothing until you add it.

    [J] SPEED TIPS ⚡
    ───────────────────
    - `argo submit --from workflowtemplate/easypj-ci-pipeline -p
      commit-sha=$(git rev-parse --short HEAD)` — manually replay
      what the Sensor does, for local debugging without a real push.
    - `kubectl argo rollouts get rollout easypj-rollout --watch` in
      one terminal, `argocd app get easypj-prod --watch` in another —
      the fastest way to see BOTH state machines from [H] live.
    - `kubectl logs -n argo-events deploy/sensor-controller -f` catches
      a misfiring Sensor faster than digging through the UI.

    [K] COMMANDS TO REMEMBER 🔑
    ─────────────────────────
    ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
    │ # Trigger / inspect the pipeline manually                       │
    │ argo submit --from workflowtemplate/easypj-ci-pipeline \        │
    │   -p commit-sha=<sha>                                           │
    │ argo watch @latest          # live DAG progress                 │
    │ argo logs @latest -f                                            │
    │ # Events layer                                                  │
    │ kubectl get eventsource,sensor,eventbus -n argo-events          │
    │ kubectl logs -n argo-events deploy/sensor-controller            │
    │ # CD layer                                                      │
    │ argocd app get easypj-prod --show-operation                     │
    │ argocd app sync easypj-prod                                     │
    │ # Rollouts layer                                                │
    │ kubectl argo rollouts get rollout easypj-rollout --watch        │
    │ kubectl argo rollouts promote easypj-rollout                    │
    │ kubectl argo rollouts abort easypj-rollout                      │
    │ # Observability                                                 │
    │ kubectl port-forward svc/workflow-controller-metrics 9090:9090  │
    └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    [L] PRACTICE DRILL 🧪 (~25 min, do-it-now)
    ───────────────────────────────────────
    1. Manually `argo submit --from workflowtemplate/easypj-ci-pipeline`
       and watch it commit a fake tag bump to a scratch clone of
       easypj-k8s (5 min).
    2. `argocd app sync easypj-dev` and confirm the easypj-rollout
       object picked up the new tag (5 min).
    3. `kubectl argo rollouts get rollout easypj-rollout --watch` on
       easypj-staging (canary env) and manually
       `kubectl argo rollouts promote easypj-rollout` past one step
       (10 min).
    4. Kill the Prometheus port-forward mid-AnalysisRun and observe
       what status the AnalysisRun reports (5 min).
    Success check: you can state, for each of the 4 steps, WHICH
    controller was responsible and which was idle.

    [M] LAB QUESTIONS 🧪 (10, exam-style)
    ───────────────────────────────────────
    Q1. A PR merges to main on team-easypj/easypj. Which Argo
        component reacts FIRST, and what does it produce?
    A1. The github-webhook EventSource — it receives the POST /push
        and publishes the payload onto the EventBus.
    Explanation: EventSources are pure listeners; they never evaluate
    filters or decide to trigger anything — that's the Sensor's job.

    Q2. The Workflow finishes and pushes a commit to easypj-k8s. Which
        component detects this, and how, if no Git webhook is
        configured into argocd-server?
    A2. Argo CD's application-controller/repo-server — via its own
        periodic poll loop (default ~3 min).
    Explanation: tests that Argo Events triggering the Workflow and
    Argo CD detecting the Git change are two UNRELATED mechanisms.

    Q3. easypj-rollout is at 25% canary when its AnalysisRun (using
        error-rate-check) fails. Does Argo CD retry the sync?
    A3. No — the Application stays Synced (its spec WAS applied
        correctly); Argo Rollouts aborts/rolls back the canary on its
        own, independently of CD.
    Explanation: Sync status (CD) and canary health (Rollouts) are
    separate state machines on the same underlying object.

    Q4. A `docker build` step inside the Workflow pod fails with a
        permission error on /var/run/docker.sock. What's the fix?
    A4. Replace it with a kaniko executor step (no daemon/socket/
        privileged mode required).
    Explanation: mounting the Docker socket or running privileged
    docker:dind is a real cluster security risk (Ch 22.3); kaniko
    builds images in userspace.

    Q5. easypj-staging should auto-deploy after tests pass; easypj-prod
        requires manual promotion. Which component enforces "manual,"
        and is it the same one running the canary?
    A5. Argo CD's syncPolicy (manual/no automated block on the
        easypj-prod Application) enforces WHEN; Argo Rollouts (a
        different controller) then runs the canary once that sync
        happens.
    Explanation: CD gates arrival of a new version into prod; Rollouts
    gates how that version is introduced once it arrives.

    Q6. The error-rate-check AnalysisTemplate's Prometheus query reads
        data from where — the CI Workflow's test results, or something
        else?
    A6. Live metrics scraped from the running easypj Pods
        (http_errors_total/http_requests_total) in the cluster, NOT
        the Workflow's test output.
    Explanation: distinguishes pre-deploy testing (Workflows) from
    post-deploy production verification (Rollouts + Analysis).

    Q7. A teammate proposes having the github-webhook EventSource call
        `argocd app sync easypj-prod` directly, skipping the Git
        commit step. What's the objection?
    A7. It breaks GitOps: cluster state would depend on an imperative,
        out-of-band trigger instead of Git, losing drift-detection,
        audit history, and reliable self-heal.
    Explanation: this is "Identify Common Reconciliation Patterns"
    (8.3/21.1) made concrete — Git must remain the only source of truth.

    Q8. Which single object, if deleted, halts ALL future automatic CI
        triggering without touching Workflows or Argo CD resources?
    A8. The EventBus (or the Sensor) in argo-events.
    Explanation: EventBus is the required NATS transport between
    EventSource and Sensor (Ch 18) — remove it and delivery stops.

    Q9. The DAG's parallel test stage runs unit/lint/SAST/integration.
        SAST fails; unit and lint succeed. Does the build step run?
    A9. No — the build task's `depends:` includes SAST, and a failed
        dependency blocks it (unless SAST sets continueOn: failed,
        which this pipeline doesn't).
    Explanation: tests DAG `depends:` semantics (Workflows, 36%)
    inside a realistic integration scenario, not an isolated toy DAG.

    Q10. `argocd app get easypj-prod` shows Synced during a 50% canary.
         Why doesn't "Synced" mean "100% of traffic is on the new
         version"?
    A10. "Synced" only means the live Rollout OBJECT's spec matches
         Git — it says nothing about that Rollout's OWN internal
         progress, which is a separate status you check with
         `kubectl argo rollouts get rollout`.
    Explanation: the single most exam-relevant gotcha in this chapter.

    [N] VISUAL SUMMARY — one picture, the whole chapter
    ─────────────────────────────────────────────────
    ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
    │ git push (team-easypj/easypj)                                    │
    │   │                                                              │
    │   v  EventBus (NATS)                                             │
    │ github-webhook (EventSrc) → easypj-ci-trigger (Sensor)           │
    │   │ creates Workflow object                                      │
    │   v                                                              │
    │ easypj-ci-pipeline (WorkflowTemplate, 20.2/20.3-optimized):      │
    │   clone → parallel(unit,lint,SAST,integ) → kaniko build+push →  │
    │   commit tag → team-easypj/easypj-k8s  (SEPARATE repo!)          │
    │   │                                                              │
    │   v  Argo CD's OWN poll/webhook loop (independent!)              │
    │ easypj-dev/staging/prod Applications sync → apply easypj-rollout │
    │   │                                                              │
    │   v  Argo Rollouts' OWN reconcile loop (independent!)            │
    │ canary 10%(30s)→25%(2m)→50%(5m)→100%, gated by error-rate-check │
    │ (queries live easypj Pods via Prometheus :9090)                  │
    │   │                                                              │
    │   v  20.4                                                        │
    │ Prometheus scrapes all 4 controllers (8082-4/9090/8090/7777) →  │
    │ Grafana dashboards + Argo CD Notifications (Ch 6.3) alert humans │
    └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
    PROSE RECAP: one push triggers 5 independent reconcile loops
    chained by an EventBus message, two Kubernetes-object writes, and
    one Git commit — never a direct API call between Argo components.
    20.3's optimizations make the WorkflowTemplate leg faster; 20.4's
    metrics are how you'd prove any of this is actually healthy at
    3 AM without reading a single external doc.


Chapter 21: Argo CD + Workflows + Events + Rollouts — Integration Patterns
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
    🔴 ADVANCED · ⏱️ ~2.5 hours · 🎯 CAPA domain: synthesis across all 4 (Identify Common Reconciliation Patterns, reinforced) · 🏢 Daily-Work: very high

    [B] WHY THIS MATTERS
    ────────────────────
    Chapter 20 built ONE specific pipeline for easypj, start to finish.
    This chapter steps back and asks a different question: given a NEW
    scenario you've never seen before, which of these four tools do you
    reach for, and how do they combine? That's a genuinely different
    skill than following one worked example — it's pattern recognition,
    and it's exactly what a CAPA scenario question tests when it
    describes a situation instead of naming a tool. By the end, you'll
    be able to look at "we need X" and know immediately whether that's
    an Events job, a Workflows job, a Rollouts job, or some combination.

    ┌────────────────────────────────┬────────────────────────────────┐
    │  BEFORE CHAPTER 21              │  AFTER CHAPTER 21               │
    ├────────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────┤
    │ "I know how Chapter 20's ONE    │ "I can recognize which of 4     │
    │  pipeline works"                │  patterns a NEW scenario needs" │
    │ Each Argo tool's reconcile loop │ See the ONE shared reconciliation│
    │ feels like a separate mechanism │ model underneath all 4 tools    │
    │ Dev/staging/prod all treated    │ A deliberate strategy per        │
    │ the same                        │ environment, each justified      │
    └────────────────────────────────┴────────────────────────────────┘

    [C] LEARNING OBJECTIVES — 🎯 What you'll be able to DO
    ────────────────────────────────────────────────────
      - Match a new integration scenario to one of the 4 canonical
        patterns (push-to-deploy, manifest generation, CD+Rollouts,
        cluster watchdog) and justify the match.
      - Explain the shared watch-diff-reconcile loop underlying Argo
        CD, Workflows, Rollouts, and Events, and why it's the SAME
        pattern wearing 4 different CRDs.
      - Design a promotion strategy across dev/staging/prod that
        matches risk tolerance to trigger/approval/rollback mechanism.

    [D] CAPA RELEVANCE
    ───────────────────
    This chapter REINFORCES "Identify Common Reconciliation Patterns"
    (Argo CD domain, 34%) — Chapter 8 is the PRIMARY teaching chapter
    for that competency in an Argo-CD-specific context; this chapter
    extends the SAME pattern across all 4 tools, which is why it earns
    a place in "Beyond CAPA" rather than duplicating Ch 8's core
    content. Expect exam scenario questions ("given this requirement,
    which pattern fits") to draw on 21.1's 4 patterns directly.

    [E] SECTION-MAP DIAGRAM — you are here
    ────────────────────────────────────
    ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
    │  21.1 Four Patterns  ──>  21.2 Why They Work  ──>  21.3 Multi- │
    │  (the WHAT — 4 concrete    (the WHY — one shared    Environment│
    │   integration shapes)       reconcile loop under      Strategy  │
    │                              all 4 tools)              (applying │
    │                                                          it to    │
    │                                                          dev/     │
    │                                                          staging/ │
    │                                                          prod)    │
    └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
    PROSE RECAP: 21.1 gives you the pattern library, 21.2 explains why
    all 4 patterns are really variations on ONE idea, and 21.3 applies
    that idea to the concrete dev→staging→prod decision every real
    team has to make.

    21.1  Common Integration Patterns
          - Pattern 1: Event-triggered Workflow updates Git → CD syncs
          - Pattern 2: Workflow generates Kubernetes manifests → CD deploys
          - Pattern 3: CD deploys base → Rollouts handles progressive delivery
          - Pattern 4: Events watch cluster changes → Workflows react
          [PATTERN CARDS: 4 integration patterns with diagrams]
          ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
          │  PATTERN 1: PUSH-TO-DEPLOY                                 │
          │                                                            │
          │  👨‍💻 Push Code → 🔔 Events → 🔄 Workflows → 📦 Argo CD     │
          │                                                            │
          │  1. Dev pushes to GitHub                                   │
          │  2. Webhook EventSource receives push event                │
          │  3. Sensor triggers CI Workflow                            │
          │  4. Workflow: Clone → Test → Build → Push → Update GitOps  │
          │  5. Argo CD detects GitOps repo change → auto-syncs        │
          │  6. App deployed! 🎉                                       │
          │                                                            │
          │  easypj: Every push to main auto-deploys to dev            │
          ├────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
          │  PATTERN 2: MANIFEST GENERATION                            │
          │                                                            │
          │  🔄 Workflow → 📄 Generate K8s YAML → 📦 Argo CD deploys   │
          │                                                            │
          │  1. Workflow renders K8s manifests from templates          │
          │  2. Output YAML is committed to GitOps repo                │
          │  3. Argo CD syncs the generated manifests                  │
          │                                                            │
          │  easypj: CI Workflow generates env-specific YAML           │
          ├────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
          │  PATTERN 3: CD + ROLLOUTS (Progressive Delivery)           │
          │                                                            │
          │  📦 Argo CD → ☸️ Rollout CRD → 🚀 Canary/BlueGreen        │
          │                                                            │
          │  1. Argo CD deploys a Rollout (NOT a Deployment)           │
          │  2. Rollout controller manages progressive delivery        │
          │  3. AnalysisTemplate checks metrics before each step       │
          │  4. Auto-promote or auto-rollback based on metrics         │
          │                                                            │
          │  easypj: CD deploys Rollout, Rollout does canary 10→100%   │
          ├────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
          │  PATTERN 4: CLUSTER WATCHDOG                               │
          │                                                            │
          │  🔔 Resource EventSource → 🔄 Workflow (remediation)       │
          │                                                            │
          │  1. EventSource watches for specific K8s resource changes  │
          │  2. Sensor triggers Workflow when anomaly detected         │
          │  3. Workflow performs automated remediation                │
          │                                                            │
          │  easypj: Watch JobPost CRDs → auto-validate new posts      │
          └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    21.2  Why These Patterns Work: The Shared Reconciliation Model
          - Every Argo tool is the SAME loop: watch → diff → reconcile
          - What differs is only WHAT each tool watches and reconciles
          - This is why the 4 patterns in 21.1 compose so cleanly
          [MODEL DIAGRAM: One reconcile loop, four CRDs]
          ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
          │  THE SHARED RECONCILE LOOP — same shape, 4 CRDs             │
          │                                                            │
          │  ┌─────────────┬────────────────────┬─────────────────────┐│
          │  │ TOOL        │ WATCHES for a diff  │ RECONCILES by       ││
          │  ├─────────────┼────────────────────┼─────────────────────┤│
          │  │ Argo CD     │ Git vs live cluster │ kubectl apply the   ││
          │  │             │ state                │ diff (sync)         ││
          │  │ Argo        │ Workflow object vs   │ create/update pods  ││
          │  │ Workflows   │ actual pod status    │ per the DAG          ││
          │  │ Argo        │ Rollout spec vs      │ shift traffic/pods   ││
          │  │ Rollouts    │ actual ReplicaSet    │ per the strategy      ││
          │  │             │ state                 │ steps                 ││
          │  │ Argo Events │ external world vs    │ create a Workflow/    ││
          │  │             │ "have I seen this     │ trigger object         ││
          │  │             │ event before"          │                        ││
          │  └─────────────┴────────────────────┴─────────────────────┘│
          │                                                            │
          │           watch ──▶ diff ──▶ reconcile ──▶ (repeat)         │
          │                                                            │
          │  Every pattern in 21.1 is just this loop CHAINED: one       │
          │  tool's "reconcile" step (e.g. Workflow updates Git) IS     │
          │  the next tool's "diff" input (Argo CD notices Git changed).│
          └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

          🧠 MENTAL MODEL — four thermostats, not one furnace
          (Ch 20 [G] introduced this for one pipeline; here it's the
          general principle.) No Argo tool ever calls another tool's
          API directly. Each one watches its OWN input (Git, a Workflow
          object, a Rollout object, an external event stream) and reacts
          only when THAT input changes. Chaining them — as every pattern
          in 21.1 does — works because each tool's OUTPUT (a Git commit,
          a created object, a status field) happens to be another tool's
          WATCHED INPUT. There's no orchestrator coordinating all four;
          the chaining emerges from what each one happens to watch.

          🧭 DECISION GUIDE — matching a NEW scenario to a pattern
          "Code change should reach the cluster automatically" →
            Pattern 1 (Push-to-Deploy): Events → Workflows → Git → CD.
          "Deployment config should be generated, not hand-written" →
            Pattern 2 (Manifest Generation): Workflow renders YAML,
            commits it, CD deploys the result.
          "A deploy should ship gradually with a safety net, not
          all-or-nothing" →
            Pattern 3 (CD + Rollouts): CD deploys the Rollout OBJECT;
            Rollouts owns everything about HOW it rolls out.
          "The cluster itself should react to a resource changing,
          with no Git commit involved at all" →
            Pattern 4 (Cluster Watchdog): Resource EventSource → Sensor
            → remediation Workflow, entirely in-cluster.
          ⚠️ GOTCHA: these patterns are not mutually exclusive — Chapter
          20's real pipeline is Patterns 1 AND 3 chained together (push
          triggers Events/Workflows/CD, and CD's target is a Rollout,
          not a plain Deployment). Real systems usually combine 2+
          patterns, not pick exactly one.

    21.3  Multi-Environment Strategy
          - Dev: Auto-deploy on every push (fast feedback)
          - Staging: Auto-deploy after tests pass
          - Prod: Manual approval + canary + metrics
          [TABLE: Environment promotion strategy]
          ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
          │  MULTI-ENVIRONMENT PROMOTION STRATEGY                       │
          │                                                            │
          │  ┌──────────┬──────────┬──────────┬──────────┬──────────┐ │
          │  │ ENV      │ TRIGGER  │ STRATEGY │ APPROVAL │ ROLLBACK │ │
          │  ├──────────┼──────────┼──────────┼──────────┼──────────┤ │
          │  │ DEV      │ Auto     │ Recreate │ None     │ Instant  │ │
          │  │          │ (push)   │ (fast)   │          │ (re-push)│ │
          │  ├──────────┼──────────┼──────────┼──────────┼──────────┤ │
          │  │ STAGING  │ Auto     │ Rolling  │ Auto     │ Git      │ │
          │  │          │ (tests   │ Update   │ (tests   │ revert   │ │
          │  │          │  pass)   │          │  pass)   │          │ │
          │  ├──────────┼──────────┼──────────┼──────────┼──────────┤ │
          │  │ PROD     │ Manual   │ Canary   │ Manual   │ Argo CD  │ │
          │  │          │ (promote)│ 10→100%  │ (2+ eyes)│ rollback │ │
          │  └──────────┴──────────┴──────────┴──────────┴──────────┘ │
          │                                                            │
          │  PROMOTION FLOW:                                           │
          │  ┌──────┐    ┌──────────┐    ┌──────┐                     │
          │  │ DEV  │───▶│ STAGING  │───▶│ PROD │                     │
          │  │  🟢  │    │   🟡     │    │  🔴  │                     │
          │  └──────┘    └──────────┘    └──────┘                     │
          │  Auto on      Auto after     Manual approve               │
          │  every push   tests pass     + canary rollout              │
          │                                                            │
          │  easypj SETUP:                                             │
          │  • DEV: immediate feedback (< 2 min from push to live)    │
          │  • STAGING: integration testing (< 10 min from merge)     │
          │  • PROD: controlled rollout (< 1 hour, metric-verified)   │
          └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘


    [G] UNDER THE HOOD 🔧 — Tracing a Cross-Tool Failure to Its Owner
    ────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
    Because no tool calls another's API directly (21.2), a failure
    ANYWHERE in a chained pattern always has exactly ONE owning
    controller — the skill is knowing which watch/diff/reconcile loop
    to inspect first. A practical trace for Pattern 1 (the shape
    Chapter 20 builds in full):

        Symptom: "my push never deployed"
              │
              v
        Did the Sensor even fire? (Ch 18) ── kubectl logs sensor pod
              │ yes                              │ no
              v                                  v
        Did the Workflow succeed? (Ch 13)   Check EventBus health
              │ yes                          (Ch 17) — the #1 silent
              v                              blocker for a whole
        Did it commit to the GitOps repo?   namespace of Sensors
              │ yes
              v
        Did Argo CD detect + sync? (Ch 8)  ── argocd app diff
              │ yes
              v
        Did Rollouts progress the canary? (Ch 16) ── kubectl argo
                                                       rollouts get rollout

    PROSE RECAP: this is 21.2's model applied as a debugging tool — walk
    the chain in the SAME order data actually flows, and the first
    "no" you hit tells you exactly which chapter's commands to reach
    for. There is never a step where "the orchestrator" is the answer,
    because there isn't one.

    [H] KEY TAKEAWAYS 🔑
    ─────────────────────
    - All 4 Argo tools share ONE loop shape (watch → diff → reconcile);
      they differ only in WHAT they watch and HOW they reconcile.
    - The 4 integration patterns in 21.1 aren't 4 separate mechanisms —
      they're that ONE loop chained, where one tool's output becomes
      the next tool's watched input.
    - No tool calls another's API — chaining emerges from shared state
      (Git, Kubernetes objects), never a direct function call.
    - Real systems typically combine 2+ patterns at once (Ch 20 =
      Pattern 1 + Pattern 3), not pick exactly one.
    - Dev/staging/prod should each get a DELIBERATE trigger/approval/
      rollback combination matched to that environment's actual risk,
      not the same settings copy-pasted three times.
    🎯 EXAM RELEVANCE: scenario questions describing a NEW requirement
       and asking "which pattern/tool" draw directly on 21.1-21.2;
       questions about WHERE a chained pipeline broke draw on this [G].

    [I] EXAM GOTCHAS ⚠️
    ─────────────────────
    1. Assuming one Argo tool calls another's API when a pattern is
       described — none of them do; the connection is always Git or a
       Kubernetes object.
    2. Treating the 4 patterns as mutually exclusive choices — most
       real pipelines chain 2 or more.
    3. Applying the SAME sync/approval/rollback policy to dev, staging,
       AND prod — 21.3's table exists because that's the wrong default.
    4. Forgetting that "Identify Common Reconciliation Patterns" is
       primarily taught in Ch 8 (Argo-CD-specific) — this chapter
       extends, not replaces, that grounding.

    [J] SPEED TIPS ⚡
    ───────────────────
    - When a cross-tool pipeline breaks, trace it in DATA-FLOW order
      (21.2's decision guide / [G]'s trace), not by guessing which tool
      "feels" broken.
    - Before designing a NEW integration, ask "what does each tool
      WATCH here" first — the pattern usually falls out immediately
      once the watched inputs are named.

    [K] COMMANDS TO REMEMBER 🔑
    ──────────────────────────────
      # Same commands as Ch 8/13/16/18 — this chapter is about WHEN to
      # reach for which, not new syntax:
      kubectl logs -n argo-events -l sensor-name=<name> -f
      argo get <wf> && argo logs <wf>
      argocd app diff <name> && argocd app get <name>
      kubectl argo rollouts get rollout <name> --watch

    [L] PRACTICE DRILL 🧪 (~15 min, do-it-now)
    ─────────────────────────────────────────
      1. For each of these 3 NEW scenarios, name which of the 4 patterns
         (or combination) fits, in one sentence each:
         a) "Rotate a database credential and have every environment
            pick it up within a minute, no deploy involved."
         b) "A new JobPost CRD should get an auto-generated thumbnail
            image the moment it's created."
         c) "Ship a risky new search algorithm to 5% of users first,
            triggered by merging a specific PR label."
      2. For (c), sketch which Chapter 20 components you'd reuse
         unchanged vs. which you'd need to modify.

    [M] LAB QUESTIONS 🧪 (10, exam-style)
    ───────────────────────────────────────
    Q1. A team wants job-post CSV uploads to auto-import with zero Git
        commits involved anywhere in the flow. Which pattern?
    A1. Pattern 4 (Cluster Watchdog) — or more precisely, an Events-only
        flow (S3/minio EventSource → Sensor → Workflow) with no CD
        involvement at all, since nothing about cluster DEPLOYMENT
        state changes.
    Explanation: not every automation needs to touch Argo CD — only
    flows changing what's DEPLOYED do.

    Q2. Chapter 20's full pipeline (push → Events → Workflows → CD →
        Rollouts) — which 21.1 pattern(s) does it combine?
    A2. Pattern 1 (Push-to-Deploy) chained into Pattern 3 (CD +
        Rollouts) — Events/Workflows/CD get the new version INTO the
        cluster; CD's target being a Rollout (not a Deployment) is what
        adds Pattern 3 on top.
    Explanation: reinforces that real pipelines usually chain 2+
    patterns, not pick one.

    Q3. In the shared reconciliation model, what does Argo CD's
        controller WATCH, and what does it RECONCILE by doing?
    A3. Watches: Git repo state vs. live cluster state (a diff).
        Reconciles by: applying that diff to the cluster (sync).
    Explanation: direct application of 21.2's table row for Argo CD.

    Q4. True or False: when a Sensor's trigger creates a Workflow
        object, it does so by calling the Workflow Controller's API.
    A4. False.
    Explanation: it calls the Kubernetes API to CREATE a Workflow
    object; the Workflow Controller picks it up via its OWN independent
    watch, the same as any other Workflow submission (Ch 9, Ch 20 [G]).

    Q5. A pipeline fails and `argocd app diff` shows no drift, but the
        deployed image is clearly the old version. Per [G]'s trace,
        which step do you check NEXT?
    A5. Whether the CI Workflow actually committed the new tag to the
        GitOps repo in the first place — if CD shows no diff, it's
        correctly synced to whatever Git currently says, meaning the
        problem is upstream (Workflow/Events), not in CD itself.
    Explanation: "no diff" means CD is doing its job correctly against
    whatever input it was given — the bug is earlier in the chain.

    Q6. Why can't a single "orchestrator" component coordinate all 4
        Argo tools directly, per this chapter's model?
    A6. There isn't one by design — each tool's controller independently
        watches its own input and reconciles when it changes; chaining
        happens because outputs happen to be other tools' watched
        inputs (Git commits, Kubernetes objects), never a direct call.
    Explanation: the "four thermostats, not one furnace" mental model
    (21.2, Ch 20 [G]).

    Q7. A team applies prod's exact sync/approval/rollback settings to
        their dev environment. What's the likely complaint within a
        week?
    A7. Dev feedback becomes far too slow — requiring manual approval
        and a metric-gated canary for every trivial dev-only change
        defeats the "fast feedback" purpose of a dev environment
        (21.3's table).
    Explanation: environment-appropriate policy, not one-size-fits-all,
    is the point of 21.3.

    Q8. Which competency does this chapter's content REINFORCE rather
        than primarily teach, and where is it primarily taught instead?
    A8. "Identify Common Reconciliation Patterns" — primarily taught in
        Chapter 8, in an Argo-CD-specific context; this chapter extends
        the same underlying pattern across all 4 tools.
    Explanation: avoids double-counting the same competency as
    "PRIMARY" in two different chapters' CAPA-relevance framing.

    Q9. Design a promotion path for a security patch that must reach
        prod within the hour but still needs SOME safety net. Which
        row of 21.3's table is closest, and what would you tighten?
    A9. Closest to PROD's row (Canary, manual promote, Argo CD
        rollback) — but you'd likely shorten canary pause durations and
        reduce required approvers from "2+ eyes" to 1, trading some
        safety margin for speed given the patch's urgency.
    Explanation: 21.3's table is a STARTING template, not a fixed law —
    real incidents justify deliberately adjusting it.

    Q10. A new EventSource type is released upstream tomorrow. Does
         21.2's reconciliation model need to change to accommodate it?
    A10. No — it's just a new "watch" input feeding the same Sensor/
         Trigger reconcile loop; the model itself (watch → diff →
         reconcile) doesn't change per source type, only which CRD/
         protocol is being watched.
    Explanation: demonstrates why the shared model is a durable mental
    tool, not something that needs re-learning per new integration.

    ────────────────────
    ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐

    │                    Kubernetes objects) — NEVER a                   │

    └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
    PROSE RECAP: Chapter 21's real content isn't the 4 pattern cards —
    it's the realization that they're all the SAME reconcile loop
    wearing different CRDs, chained by shared state instead of direct
    calls. Once that clicks, designing a brand-new integration (or
    debugging a broken one) stops being memorization and becomes
    following one consistent trail. Chapter 22 takes this same wired-
    together system and hardens it for real production use.


Chapter 22: Production Hardening and Best Practices
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
    🔴 ADVANCED · ⏱️ ~3.5 hours · 🎯 Beyond CAPA — production reality, not exam-graded · 🏢 Daily-Work: very high

    [B] WHY THIS MATTERS
    ────────────────────
    Every other chapter in Parts 2-6 answers "how do I make this work."
    This chapter answers "how do I make this survive contact with a
    real team, a real budget, a real auditor, and a real 2 AM incident."
    None of it is on the CAPA exam — the exam tests whether you can
    configure Argo correctly; this chapter tests whether you can run it
    responsibly. If Parts 2-6 got easypj/jobpost running, this chapter
    is what separates a demo cluster from something a company would
    actually trust with production traffic.

    ┌────────────────────────────────┬────────────────────────────────┐
    │  BEFORE CHAPTER 22              │  AFTER CHAPTER 22               │
    ├────────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────┤
    │ "Argo CD needs a database        │ Know exactly what DOES and     │
    │  backup like any other app"      │ doesn't need backing up          │
    │ Security = "use RBAC" in the      │ A concrete top-10 list, incl.   │
    │ abstract                          │ the AppProject whitelist gap    │
    │ "It works on my cluster" is       │ Know the DR/cost/scale/audit/   │
    │ where the guide would end         │ migration/culture questions a   │
    │                                    │ real production rollout faces  │
    └────────────────────────────────┴────────────────────────────────┘

    [C] LEARNING OBJECTIVES — 🎯 What you'll be able to DO
    ────────────────────────────────────────────────────
      - Correctly explain what Argo CD does and does NOT need backed up
        (it has no database) and apply the right HA model per component.
      - Apply a concrete, corrected top-10 security checklist including
        AppProject resource whitelisting and real audit-log sources.
      - Diagnose common Argo CD/Workflows/Rollouts/Events failures using
        a corrected, exam-accurate troubleshooting table.
      - Plan disaster recovery, cost controls, multi-tenancy, and a
        realistic incremental migration path onto Argo.
      - Recognize that GitOps adoption is a people/process problem as
        much as a tooling one, and sequence a rollout accordingly.
