    answer questions that only come up once real users, real money, and
    real auditors are involved. 22.11-22.12 answer the question every
    reader eventually asks: "okay, how do I actually get MY team from
    here to there?"

    22.1  Argo CD Production Checklist
          - High availability setup (multiple replicas)
          - Backup strategy for Argo CD's ACTUAL state (see gotcha below)
          - TLS/SSL for Argo CD API
          - Rate limiting and resource quotas
          - Monitoring and alerting
          [CHECKLIST: Production readiness]
          ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
          │  ✅ ARGO CD PRODUCTION READINESS CHECKLIST                  │
          │                                                            │
          │  ☐ argocd-server: replicas ≥ 2 (plain Deployment scaling)   │
          │  ☐ argocd-repo-server: replicas ≥ 2 (manifest-render load)  │
          │  ☐ application-controller: sharded correctly for your       │
          │     cluster COUNT, not just "more replicas" (see 🔧 below)  │
          │  ☐ TLS terminated correctly (Ch 3.2's --insecure gotcha)    │
          │  ☐ admin user disabled once SSO is verified working         │
          │  ☐ AppProject resourceWhitelist set on every real project   │
          │  ☐ Git repos backed up (THIS is Argo CD's real backup)      │
          │  ☐ Kubernetes objects (Applications/AppProjects) exportable │
          │     via `kubectl get applications,appprojects -A -o yaml`   │
          │  ☐ Prometheus scraping ports 8082/8083/8084 (Ch 20.4)       │
          │  ☐ Resource requests/limits set on all argocd namespace pods│
          └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
          ⚠️ GOTCHA — Argo CD has NO database. Unlike some GitOps tools,
             Argo CD is intentionally stateless: the desired state lives
             entirely in GIT, the actual state lives in the CLUSTER, and
             Redis is only an ephemeral CACHE (manifest/comparison
             results) safe to lose and rebuild. "Backup Argo CD" really
             means: (1) your Git repos are already your backup of desired
             state — back THEM up like any other code; (2) back up
             Argo CD's own Kubernetes objects (Applications, AppProjects,
             Secrets) via `kubectl get applications,appprojects -A -o
             yaml`, since THOSE (not a database) are Argo CD's real
             config; Redis needs no backup at all.
          🔧 HA NUANCE — "multiple replicas" isn't uniform across
             components: argocd-server and argocd-repo-server are plain
             Deployments (scale replicas: N freely). argocd-
             application-controller is a StatefulSet that SHARDS by
             cluster (Ch 8 [G]) — each replica owns a hash subset of
             registered clusters, so "HA" here means adding shards, not
             identical redundant copies of the same work.

    22.2  Argo Workflows Production Checklist
          - Workflow archiving and retention
          - Resource limits for workflow pods
          - Artifact repository backup
          - Authentication and authorization
          [CHECKLIST: Production readiness]
          ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
          │  ✅ ARGO WORKFLOWS PRODUCTION READINESS CHECKLIST            │
          │                                                            │
          │  ☐ Workflow Archive DB configured (Postgres/MySQL/MariaDB — │
          │     Ch 9.3) — without it, `argo list --archived` is empty   │
          │  ☐ ttlStrategy + podGC set (Ch 11.6) — no silent pod/object │
          │     accumulation eating etcd/node capacity                  │
          │  ☐ Resource requests/limits on EVERY template, not just the │
          │     Workflow default (Ch 22.6's #1 real cost leak)          │
          │  ☐ Artifact repository (S3/MinIO) has a lifecycle/retention │
          │     policy — old build caches/test reports cost forever     │
          │  ☐ RBAC scoped per-namespace ServiceAccount for Sensors      │
          │     that submit Workflows (Ch 18.3), not cluster-admin       │
          │  ☐ CI build steps use kaniko/BuildKit, never a mounted       │
          │     Docker socket (Ch 20.2's security fix)                   │
          │  ☐ Prometheus scraping workflow-controller-metrics :9090     │
          │     (Service NOT created by default — Ch 20.4)               │
          │  ☐ parallelism: caps set on any large withItems/withParam    │
          │     fan-out (Ch 10.3)                                        │
          └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    22.3  Security Best Practices
          - RBAC for all Argo components
          - SSO/OIDC integration
          - Git repository access control
          - Secret management (NEVER in plain YAML!)
          - Network policies
          [SECURITY CARDS: Top 10 security practices]
          ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
          │  🔐 TOP 10 ARGO SECURITY BEST PRACTICES                    │
          │                                                            │
          │  1. 🔑 CHANGE DEFAULT PASSWORDS                            │
          │     argocd account update-password (immediately after install)│
          │                                                            │
          │  2. 🔗 USE SSO/OIDC (NOT local users in prod)              │
          │     Integrate with Okta, Azure AD, Google, GitHub, GitLab  │
          │     argocd-cm → oidc.config → configure provider           │
          │                                                            │
          │  3. 🚫 DISABLE ADMIN USER AFTER SSO                        │
          │     argocd-cm → admin.enabled: "false"                    │
          │                                                            │
          │  4. 🔒 RESTRICT AppProject DESTINATIONS + RESOURCE KINDS   │
          │     NEVER: destinations.namespace: "*"                     │
          │     ALWAYS: specific namespaces per environment            │
          │     ALSO set clusterResourceWhitelist/                     │
          │     namespaceResourceWhitelist — without them, an          │
          │     AppProject with unrestricted destinations can still     │
          │     deploy ANY resource kind (including ClusterRoleBinding) │
          │     even if namespaces are scoped correctly.                │
          │                                                            │
          │  5. 📦 NEVER STORE PLAINTEXT SECRETS IN GIT                │
          │     Use: Sealed Secrets, External Secrets Operator, Vault │
          │     Git repos with secrets = security incident waiting     │
          │                                                            │
          │  6. 🛡️ NETWORK POLICIES                                   │
          │     Restrict Argo CD namespace egress to only needed repos│
          │     Restrict ingress to Argo CD from trusted sources only │
          │                                                            │
          │  7. 📋 REGULAR AUDIT LOGS                                  │
          │     ⚠️ argocd admin notifications is NOT an audit tool —   │
          │     it manages notification subscriptions (Ch 6.3), not     │
          │     an audit trail. Real audit sources: Kubernetes audit    │
          │     logging scoped to the argocd namespace (who changed     │
          │     which Application/AppProject object, and when), plus    │
          │     argocd-server's own access/API logs for who ran what.  │
          │                                                            │
          │  8. 🔄 UPDATE ARGO COMPONENTS REGULARLY                    │
          │     Subscribe to security advisories at GitHub releases   │
          │     Run: argocd version (check for outdated versions)     │
          │                                                            │
          │  9. 🏷️ USE PRIVATE GIT REPOS WITH DEPLOY KEYS             │
          │     argocd repo add <url> --ssh-private-key-path <key>    │
          │     Rotate deploy keys regularly                          │
          │                                                            │
          │  10. ⚡ RESOURCE LIMITS FOR ALL ARGO PODS                  │
          │      Prevent DoS — set limits on argocd namespace pods    │
          │      Workflow pods especially need resource limits!       │
          └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    22.4  Troubleshooting Common Issues
          - Argo CD: Sync stuck, health unknown, repo unavailable
          - Argo Workflows: Pod pending, artifact missing, DAG deadlock
          - Argo Rollouts: Traffic not shifting, analysis failing
          - Argo Events: Webhook not receiving, sensor not triggering
          [TROUBLESHOOTING TABLE: Problem → Cause → Fix]
          ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
          │  TROUBLESHOOTING — Common Issues & Solutions               │
          │                                                            │
          │  ┌────────────────────┬────────────────────┬──────────────┐│
          │  │ PROBLEM           │ LIKELY CAUSE       │ FIX          ││
          │  ├────────────────────┼────────────────────┼──────────────┤│
          │  │ ARGO CD           │                    │              ││
          │  ├────────────────────┼────────────────────┼──────────────┤│
          │  │ Sync stuck at      │ Repo unreachable   │ Check repo   ││
          │  │ "Unknown" health   │ or auth failed     │ URL + creds  ││
          │  ├────────────────────┼────────────────────┼──────────────┤│
          │  │ OutOfSync but      │ Stale manifest     │ argocd app   ││
          │  │ nothing changed    │ cache (repo-server)│ get --hard-  ││
          │  │                    │                    │ refresh      ││
          │  ├────────────────────┼────────────────────┼──────────────┤│
          │  │ Healthy but        │ Wrong server URL   │ Verify dest. ││
          │  │ resources missing  │ or namespace       │ server+ns    ││
          │  ├────────────────────┼────────────────────┼──────────────┤│
          │  │ WORKFLOWS         │                    │              ││
          │  ├────────────────────┼────────────────────┼──────────────┤│
          │  │ Pod pending        │ Resource quota or  │ Check events ││
          │  │ indefinitely       │ node taint         │ + describe   ││
          │  ├────────────────────┼────────────────────┼──────────────┤│
          │  │ Artifact "not      │ Artifact repo not  │ Verify MinIO ││
          │  │ found" error       │ configured         │ / S3 config  ││
          │  ├────────────────────┼────────────────────┼──────────────┤│
          │  │ DAG tasks never    │ Dependency not     │ Verify deps  ││
          │  │ start (deadlock)   │ resolvable — OFTEN │ in DAG spec; ││
          │  │                    │ a depends: "X.     │ remember     ││
          │  │                    │ Succeeded" waiting │ Skipped !=   ││
          │  │                    │ on a task that was │ Succeeded    ││
          │  │                    │ Skipped, not run   │ (Ch 10.3)    ││
          │  ├────────────────────┼────────────────────┼──────────────┤│
          │  │ ROLLOUTS          │                    │              ││
          │  ├────────────────────┼────────────────────┼──────────────┤│
          │  │ Traffic not        │ Service selector   │ Verify       ││
          │  │ shifting to canary │ mismatch — NOTE:   │ canaryService/││
          │  │                    │ canaryService/     │ stableService ││
          │  │                    │ stableService, NOT │ (activeService││
          │  │                    │ activeService (that│ is blueGreen- ││
          │  │                    │ is Blue-Green only)│ only, Ch 14.3)││
          │  ├────────────────────┼────────────────────┼──────────────┤│
          │  │ AnalysisRun stuck  │ Prometheus         │ Check        ││
          │  │ at "Running"       │ unreachable        │ analysis spec ││
          │  ├────────────────────┼────────────────────┼──────────────┤│
          │  │ EVENTS            │                    │              ││
          │  ├────────────────────┼────────────────────┼──────────────┤│
          │  │ Webhook returns    │ NATS pod down or   │ kubectl logs ││
          │  │ 500 error          │ EventBus not ready │ -n argo-events│
          │  ├────────────────────┼────────────────────┼──────────────┤│
          │  │ Sensor not firing  │ EventSource not    │ Check        ││
          │  │ after event        │ publishing to bus  │ EventSource  ││
          │  └────────────────────┴────────────────────┴──────────────┘│
          │                                                            │
          │  UNIVERSAL DEBUG COMMANDS:                                 │
          │  kubectl logs -n <namespace> deploy/<component>           │
          │  kubectl describe <resource> -n <namespace>               │
          │  kubectl get events -n <namespace> --sort-by=.lastTimestamp│
          └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    22.5  Disaster Recovery and Backup Strategy
          - Git IS your primary backup — protect it like production
          - Back up Argo CD/Workflows/Rollouts/Events Kubernetes objects
          - Test restores, not just backups
          [CHECKLIST: DR readiness]
          ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
          │  DISASTER RECOVERY — WHAT TO ACTUALLY BACK UP                │
          │                                                            │
          │  ┌──────────────────┬──────────────────────────────────┐  │
          │  │ COMPONENT         │ REAL BACKUP TARGET                │  │
          │  ├──────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────┤  │
          │  │ Argo CD           │ Git repos (primary!) + `kubectl   │  │
          │  │                   │ get applications,appprojects,     │  │
          │  │                   │ applicationsets -A -o yaml`        │  │
          │  │ Argo Workflows    │ WorkflowTemplates/CronWorkflows    │  │
          │  │                   │ (Git-stored) + the Workflow        │  │
          │  │                   │ Archive DB (Postgres/MySQL, Ch 9)  │  │
          │  │ Argo Rollouts     │ Rollout/AnalysisTemplate manifests │  │
          │  │                   │ (Git-stored — no other state)      │  │
          │  │ Argo Events       │ EventSource/Sensor/EventBus        │  │
          │  │                   │ manifests (Git-stored)             │  │
          │  └──────────────────┴──────────────────────────────────┘  │
          │                                                            │
          │  🎯 KEY INSIGHT: because everything above Git-stores its    │
          │  desired state, restoring a wiped cluster is mostly         │
          │  "reinstall the 4 controllers, re-apply from Git" — the     │
          │  Workflow Archive DB is the ONE piece with state that       │
          │  ISN'T in Git (historical run records) and needs its own    │
          │  real backup.                                                │
          │                                                            │
          │  ⚠️ GOTCHA: a backup you've never restored isn't a backup — │
          │  schedule a quarterly DR drill: wipe a scratch cluster,      │
          │  time how long full restore actually takes end to end.       │
          └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    22.6  Cost Optimization for Argo Workloads
          - Workflow pods are usually the biggest spend — right-size them
          - podGC + ttlStrategy prevent silent resource leaks
          - Spot/preemptible nodes for CI Workflow pods
          [CHECKLIST: Cost controls]
          ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
          │  COST LEVERS, RANKED BY IMPACT                              │
          │                                                            │
          │  1. 💰 RIGHT-SIZE Workflow pod requests/limits — CI pods    │
          │     copy-pasted at "2 CPU / 4Gi" for a lint step that needs │
          │     100m/256Mi is the #1 real-world Argo cost leak.          │
          │  2. 🧹 podGC + ttlStrategy (Ch 11.6) — completed pods and    │
          │     old Workflow objects left forever silently hold PVCs,   │
          │     node capacity, and etcd size.                            │
          │  3. 🏷️ Spot/preemptible nodes for CI (retryable, short-     │
          │     lived) — NOT for long-running Argo CD/Rollouts/Events    │
          │     controllers, which need stability.                       │
          │  4. 📦 Artifact retention (Ch 10.5) — old test-report/       │
          │     build-cache artifacts in S3/MinIO cost storage forever   │
          │     unless a lifecycle policy expires them.                  │
          │  5. 🔀 parallelism: caps (Ch 10.3) — an uncapped 200-way     │
          │     test matrix can burst-scale a cluster expensively for    │
          │     a few minutes on every single push.                      │
          └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    22.7  Multi-Tenancy Patterns
          - One Argo CD control plane, many teams — AppProjects are the wall
          - Per-team namespaces for Workflows/Rollouts/Events objects
          - RBAC scoped so Team A cannot see/touch Team B's Applications
          [PATTERN: Shared control plane, isolated tenants]
          ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
          │  MULTI-TENANT easypj-style ORGANIZATION (hypothetical scale)│
          │                                                            │
          │  ONE Argo CD install serving MANY teams:                    │
          │    AppProject "team-easypj"    → sourceRepos: [easypj-k8s]  │
          │                                   destinations: easypj-*    │
          │    AppProject "team-billing"   → sourceRepos: [billing-k8s] │
          │                                   destinations: billing-*   │
          │  RBAC (argocd-rbac-cm): team-easypj's SSO group gets        │
          │    role bindings scoped to ONLY the team-easypj project —    │
          │    `argocd app list` for a team-billing user never shows     │
          │    team-easypj's Applications at all.                        │
          │                                                            │
          │  🧭 DECISION: one shared control plane (this pattern) vs.   │
          │  one Argo CD per team — shared wins on operational overhead │
          │  (one thing to upgrade/monitor) as long as AppProject +      │
          │  RBAC isolation is airtight; per-team installs win only      │
          │  when compliance requires hard infrastructure separation.    │
          └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    22.8  GitOps for Infrastructure, Not Just Applications
          - Argo CD can deploy Terraform/Crossplane/cluster-addons too
          - The same reconcile loop (Ch 21.2) applies beyond app manifests
          - easypj example: managing the RDS-equivalent Postgres via GitOps
          [PATTERN: App-of-apps for platform infrastructure]
          ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
          │  BEYOND jobpost: GitOps FOR THE PLATFORM ITSELF              │
          │                                                            │
          │  Everything in this guide deployed easypj's APPLICATION —   │
          │  but the SAME Argo CD watch-diff-reconcile loop (Ch 21.2)   │
          │  works on infrastructure manifests too:                      │
          │    - Crossplane Claims (provision a managed Postgres         │
          │      instance declaratively, same GitOps flow as jobpost)    │
          │    - Cluster add-ons (cert-manager, ingress-nginx, external- │
          │      dns) via an "app-of-apps" root Application               │
          │    - Namespace/RBAC/NetworkPolicy baseline for every new      │
          │      team's namespace, applied the instant it's created       │
          │                                                            │
          │  🏢 DAILY-WORK: a platform team commonly runs a SEPARATE     │
          │  "platform" AppProject + root Application pointing at an     │
          │  infra repo, kept deliberately separate from application     │
          │  teams' AppProjects (Ch 22.7) — infra changes get reviewed   │
          │  by platform engineers, not app developers, even though      │
          │  both flow through the identical Argo CD control plane.       │
          └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    22.9  Compliance and Audit Trails
          - Git history IS your change-approval audit trail
          - Kubernetes audit logs cover the argocd/argo/argo-rollouts/
            argo-events namespaces for who-did-what beyond Git
          - Required for SOC2/ISO27001-style change management evidence
          [CHECKLIST: Audit readiness]
          ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
          │  WHAT AN AUDITOR ACTUALLY ASKS FOR                          │
          │                                                            │
          │  "Show me who approved this production change" →            │
          │    git log --follow overlays/prod/ + the PR/MR review        │
          │    trail on team-easypj/easypj-k8s (Ch 20's GitOps repo)     │
          │  "Show me who ran a manual override outside the pipeline" → │
          │    Kubernetes audit log filtered to the argocd namespace,    │
          │    NOT `argocd admin notifications` (Ch 22.3's corrected      │
          │    gotcha) — that's for alert subscriptions, not audit         │
          │  "Prove nobody can deploy to prod without review" →          │
          │    AppProject RBAC (Ch 5) + branch-protection rules on        │
          │    easypj-k8s's prod overlay, shown together                  │
          │                                                            │
          │  🎯 KEY INSIGHT: GitOps is inherently AUDIT-FRIENDLY compared│
          │  to imperative `kubectl apply` — Git's own commit history IS │
          │  a substantial fraction of the evidence an auditor wants,     │
          │  which is a genuine, often-undersold GitOps selling point.    │
          └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    22.10  Performance Tuning at Scale
          - Argo CD: repo-server caching, manifest generation is the bottleneck
          - Argo Workflows: parallelism caps + pod churn at scale
          - When ONE Argo CD instance stops being enough
          [CHECKLIST: Scale tuning]
          ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
          │  WHERE ARGO ACTUALLY BOTTLENECKS AT SCALE                   │
          │                                                            │
          │  Argo CD: repo-server manifest generation (Helm template/    │
          │    Kustomize build) is CPU-bound and re-runs on every sync — │
          │    scale repo-server replicas + tune `reposerver.parallel    │
          │    ismlimit` before assuming application-controller is the   │
          │    bottleneck (it usually isn't, until cluster COUNT grows,  │
          │    Ch 22.1's sharding note).                                  │
          │  Argo Workflows: hundreds of simultaneous pods = etcd/API-   │
          │    server pressure, not controller CPU — parallelism caps    │
          │    (Ch 10.3) and podGC (Ch 11.6) are performance controls,   │
          │    not just cost controls (Ch 22.6).                          │
          │  Argo Rollouts/Events: rarely the bottleneck — they reconcile│
          │    far fewer objects than CD/Workflows at typical scale.      │
          │                                                            │
          │  🧭 DECISION: "one Argo CD instance per how many                 │
          │  Applications" has no universal number — watch repo-server    │
          │  CPU and application-controller reconcile latency directly    │
          │  rather than picking an arbitrary Application-count threshold.│
          └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    22.11  Migration Strategies to Argo
          - From plain kubectl/Helm CLI: start with Argo CD only, app-by-app
          - From Flux: both are GitOps: Application ≈ Flux Kustomization
          - From Jenkins/GitLab CI: migrate pipelines to Workflows gradually
          [PATTERN: Incremental migration, not a big-bang cutover]
          ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
          │  MIGRATING TO ARGO — INCREMENTALLY, NOT ALL AT ONCE          │
          │                                                            │
          │  FROM manual kubectl/Helm: pick ONE low-risk app (like       │
          │    easypj-dev) first, register it as an Argo CD Application  │
          │    with manual sync, verify `argocd app diff` shows zero      │
          │    drift against what's already live, THEN flip to           │
          │    automated sync — never start with prod.                    │
          │  FROM Flux: conceptually similar (both are GitOps             │
          │    reconcilers) — a Flux Kustomization maps roughly onto an   │
          │    Argo CD Application; migrate namespace-by-namespace,        │
          │    don't run both watching the SAME resources simultaneously  │
          │    (guaranteed fighting/flapping).                             │
          │  FROM Jenkins/GitLab CI pipelines: migrate stage-by-stage —   │
          │    e.g. keep Jenkins triggering, but have its LAST stage call  │
          │    `argo submit --from workflowtemplate/...` instead of a      │
          │    raw kubectl apply, THEN migrate the earlier stages once     │
          │    the team trusts Workflows for the deploy step.               │
          │                                                            │
          │  ⚠️ GOTCHA: never run two reconcilers (Flux AND Argo CD, or   │
          │  a human AND Argo CD auto-sync) against the SAME resources —  │
          │  they'll fight, each reverting the other's last change.        │
          └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    22.12  Building Team Adoption and a GitOps Culture
          - Tooling alone doesn't create GitOps discipline — process does
          - The hardest part is "no more kubectl apply to prod," not YAML
          - Start with visibility (dashboards) before enforcement (RBAC locks)
          [CHECKLIST: Rollout to a real team]
          ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
          │  ROLLING ARGO OUT TO A REAL TEAM — THE PEOPLE PART           │
          │                                                            │
          │  1. 👀 VISIBILITY FIRST — give the team the Argo CD/         │
          │     Workflows UI read access before restricting anything;    │
          │     let them SEE drift and pipeline runs before you enforce  │
          │     "you may no longer kubectl apply directly."               │
          │  2. 🚦 ONE LOW-STAKES APP FIRST — same principle as 22.11's   │
          │     migration pattern; a first success builds trust faster    │
          │     than a mandate does.                                      │
          │  3. 📏 CODIFY THE RULE, DON'T JUST ANNOUNCE IT — RBAC that    │
          │     actually blocks direct prod kubectl apply (Ch 5) beats    │
          │     a Slack message asking nicely.                            │
          │  4. 🎓 THE HARDEST HABIT CHANGE isn't learning Argo's YAML —  │
          │     it's the muscle-memory shift from "I fix production by    │
          │     SSHing/kubectl-ing in" to "I fix production by opening a  │
          │     PR" — budget real time for this, not just a training doc. │
          │                                                            │
          │  🎯 This entire guide's easypj/jobpost project exists for     │
          │  exactly this reason — the fastest way to build the habit is  │
          │  hands-on repetition against a real (if small) app, which is  │
          │  what Chapters 1-21 gave you.                                  │
          └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘


    [G] UNDER THE HOOD 🔧 — Why "GitOps Is Stateless" Simplifies Everything
    ────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
    Nearly every production-hardening question in this chapter traces
    back to one architectural fact established all the way back in Ch 2
    [G]: Argo CD (and, by the same design philosophy, Workflows/
    Rollouts/Events) don't OWN state — they reconcile TOWARD state that
    lives elsewhere (Git, or the live cluster). This is WHY:

        DR (22.5) is mostly "reinstall + re-apply from Git," not a
          database restore — there's no database holding desired state.
        Multi-tenancy (22.7) works with ONE control plane — the
          controller has no per-tenant STATE to keep separate, only
          RBAC-scoped VISIBILITY into shared Kubernetes objects.
        Migration (22.11) can be incremental — registering one more
          Application doesn't require migrating any shared database
          schema, because there isn't one.
        Audit (22.9) is mostly "read Git history" — the approval trail
          IS the state-change mechanism, not a separate logging bolt-on.

    PROSE RECAP: once you internalize "Argo tools are reconcilers with
    no owned state," most of this chapter's harder questions (backup
    WHAT, scale WHAT, migrate HOW) answer themselves from that one fact
    rather than needing to be memorized as separate rules.

    [H] KEY TAKEAWAYS 🔑
    ─────────────────────
    - Argo CD has NO database — Git is the real backup; Redis is a
      disposable cache; only the optional Workflow Archive DB (Ch 9)
      holds state that isn't in Git and needs real backup.
    - application-controller's "HA" is SHARDING (StatefulSet, per-
      cluster), not identical redundant replicas like argocd-server.
    - AppProject security needs BOTH destination restriction AND
      clusterResourceWhitelist/namespaceResourceWhitelist — namespace
      scoping alone doesn't restrict WHICH resource kinds can deploy.
    - `argocd admin notifications` is not an audit log — it manages
      alert subscriptions; real audit evidence comes from Kubernetes
      audit logging + Git history.
    - Canary traffic issues are diagnosed via canaryService/
      stableService, not activeService (that field is Blue-Green only).
    - DAG deadlocks are frequently a Skipped-vs-Succeeded mismatch in a
      depends: expression, not a genuinely unresolvable dependency.
    - GitOps' statelessness (this chapter's [G]) is WHY DR, multi-
      tenancy, migration, and audit are all simpler than they'd be for
      a stateful system.
    🎯 Exam relevance: this chapter is Beyond CAPA, but 22.3/22.4's
       corrected security and troubleshooting details reinforce real
       graded content from Ch 5, 10, and 14/16.

    [I] EXAM GOTCHAS ⚠️
    ─────────────────────
    1. Assuming Argo CD needs traditional database backup/restore
       procedures — it doesn't have one; Git is the backup.
    2. Restricting an AppProject's destinations.namespace without also
       setting a resourceWhitelist — resource-kind restriction is a
       separate, easily-forgotten control.
    3. Treating `argocd admin notifications` as an audit trail.
    4. Debugging a stuck canary by checking `activeService` — that
       field only exists under strategy.blueGreen, never
       strategy.canary.
    5. Assuming a DAG "deadlock" always means a genuinely broken
       dependency graph — check for a Skipped-vs-Succeeded mismatch
       first, it's far more common.
    6. Running two GitOps reconcilers (or a human + automated sync)
       against the same resources during a migration — guaranteed
       fighting/flapping.

    [J] SPEED TIPS ⚡
    ───────────────────
    - `kubectl get applications,appprojects,applicationsets -A -o yaml
      > backup.yaml` — the actual one-liner "backup Argo CD" command,
      since there's no database to dump.
    - Before declaring a canary traffic bug, `kubectl get svc <name>
      -o yaml | grep -A3 selector` on BOTH the canary and stable
      Service, not just one.
    - `argocd app get <name> --hard-refresh` bypasses the repo-server's
      manifest cache — faster than guessing why a sync looks stale.

    [K] COMMANDS TO REMEMBER 🔑
    ──────────────────────────────
      kubectl get applications,appprojects,applicationsets -A -o yaml
      argocd app get <name> --hard-refresh
      kubectl get svc <canary-svc> <stable-svc> -o yaml
      kubectl logs -n argo deploy/workflow-controller | grep -i parallelism
      # Kubernetes audit log query — cluster-specific, see your
      # platform's audit backend documentation

    [L] PRACTICE DRILL 🧪 (~15 min, do-it-now)
    ─────────────────────────────────────────
      1. Run the real "backup Argo CD" one-liner above against a
         scratch cluster; confirm it captures every easypj Application.
      2. Add clusterResourceWhitelist to an AppProject that previously
         had none; confirm a ClusterRoleBinding deploy attempt is now
         rejected even though the namespace was already correct.
      3. Deliberately misconfigure a canary Service's selector; use
         `kubectl get svc -o yaml` on both canaryService and
         stableService to find which one is wrong.
      4. Write a DAG where task B's `depends: "A.Succeeded"` but A gets
         Skipped by a when: condition; confirm B never runs, then fix
         it with `"A.Succeeded || A.Skipped"`.

    [M] LAB QUESTIONS 🧪 (10, exam-style)
    ───────────────────────────────────────
    Q1. A new hire asks where to find Argo CD's database backup
        schedule. What's the accurate answer?
    A1. There isn't one — Argo CD has no database. Desired state lives
        in Git (back that up like any code repo); Redis is a disposable
        cache; only the optional Workflow Archive DB (Argo Workflows,
        Ch 9) is real state needing its own backup.
    Explanation: the single most common "production hardening"
    misconception this chapter corrects.

    Q2. An AppProject restricts destinations.namespace to easypj-prod
        only. Does this alone prevent a malicious/mistaken Application
        from deploying a ClusterRoleBinding?
    A2. No — namespace restriction and resource-kind restriction are
        separate controls; without clusterResourceWhitelist set, any
        cluster-scoped resource kind can still be deployed.
    Explanation: reinforces the corrected security checklist item.

    Q3. Where should an auditor look to see who approved a specific
        production change to easypj?
    A3. Git commit/PR history on team-easypj/easypj-k8s's prod overlay
        — NOT `argocd admin notifications`, which manages alert
        subscriptions, not an audit trail.
    Explanation: corrects the common notifications-as-audit confusion.

    Q4. easypj-rollout's canary traffic isn't shifting. Which Service
        field should you check first — activeService or
        canaryService/stableService?
    A4. canaryService/stableService — activeService only exists under
        strategy.blueGreen and is irrelevant to a canary strategy.
    Explanation: a real, easy field-name mix-up between the two
    Rollout strategies (Ch 14.3 vs 14.4).

    Q5. A DAG task never starts despite its single dependency having
        run and been intentionally skipped by a when: condition. Most
        likely cause?
    A5. `depends: "X.Succeeded"` doesn't match Skipped — needs
        `"X.Succeeded || X.Skipped"` if the downstream task should run
        either way.
    Explanation: reinforces Ch 10.3's Succeeded-vs-Skipped distinction
    in a realistic "deadlock" framing.

    Q6. Why is application-controller's HA model described as
        "sharding" rather than plain redundant replicas?
    A6. Because it's a StatefulSet where each replica owns a hash
        subset of REGISTERED CLUSTERS (Ch 8 [G]) — adding a replica
        adds capacity for more clusters, it doesn't create identical
        redundant copies of the same reconciliation work.
    Explanation: distinguishes it from argocd-server/repo-server, which
    ARE plain interchangeable Deployment replicas.

    Q7. A platform team wants to migrate from Flux to Argo CD. What's
        the #1 risk of registering the same resources in BOTH tools
        during the transition?
    A7. The two reconcilers fight — each one reverts changes the other
        makes, since both are independently trying to force the SAME
        live resources to match their own idea of desired state.
    Explanation: migrate namespace-by-namespace/app-by-app, never
    double-register the same resources.

    Q8. Why does GitOps make compliance/audit evidence-gathering
        structurally easier than an imperative kubectl-based workflow?
    A8. Because the approval trail (Git commit history, PR reviews) IS
        the same mechanism that produces the change — there's no
        separate logging system to bolt on and keep in sync with what
        actually happened.
    Explanation: ties directly back to this chapter's [G] — state lives
    in Git, so Git's own history doubles as audit evidence.

    Q9. A CI Workflow step requests `resources: {limits: {cpu: "2",
        memory: "4Gi"}}` for a lint check that only ever uses ~100m/
        256Mi. What's the real-world cost impact, beyond just that one
        step?
    A9. Over-requested resources reserve capacity the scheduler can't
        give to other pods, inflating the CLUSTER's total required size
        (and bill) even though actual usage is tiny — the #1 real Argo
        cost leak per 22.6.
    Explanation: cost optimization is mostly about requests/limits
    accuracy, not exotic tooling.

    Q10. A team is about to enforce "no more direct kubectl apply to
         prod" via RBAC on day one of adopting Argo CD, with no prior
         visibility period. What does 22.12 suggest could go wrong?
    A10. Without a visibility-first period (dashboard/UI access showing
         drift and pipeline runs before restriction), the team hasn't
         built trust in the new system yet — enforcement without buy-in
         tends to produce workarounds or resistance rather than genuine
         adoption.
    Explanation: GitOps adoption is a process/culture change, not just
    an RBAC configuration task.

    [N] VISUAL SUMMARY
    ────────────────────
    ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
    │  CHAPTER 22 IN ONE PICTURE                                            │
    │                                                                        │
    │  ROOT FACT: Argo tools are STATELESS RECONCILERS (Ch 2 [G])           │
    │  desired state = Git · actual state = cluster · no owned database     │
    │        │                                                              │
    │        ├──> 22.1-22.4  HARDEN what exists (HA per-component nuance,   │
    │        │                security top-10 incl. resourceWhitelist,      │
    │        │                corrected troubleshooting table)              │
    │        │                                                              │
    │        ├──> 22.5-22.7  RESILIENCE (backup = Git + K8s objects, not    │
    │        │                a DB · cost = right-size pods · multi-tenant  │
    │        │                = shared control plane + AppProject walls)    │
    │        │                                                              │
    │        ├──> 22.8-22.10 SCALE the PRACTICE (infra-as-GitOps too ·      │
    │        │                Git history = audit trail · repo-server is    │
    │        │                usually the real CD bottleneck)               │
    │        │                                                              │
    │        └──> 22.11-22.12 GET THERE (incremental migration, never       │
    │                          two reconcilers on one resource · visibility │
    │                          before enforcement)                          │
    └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
    PROSE RECAP: every section in this chapter is a different
    consequence of the same architectural fact — Argo tools reconcile
    state they don't own. That's why backup is simple, multi-tenancy is
    cheap, migration can be incremental, and audit is mostly "read Git."
    Chapter 21 taught you how the four tools chain together; Chapter 22
    taught you how to run that chained system responsibly, at scale,
    with a real team, for real money. Part 7 turns all of Chapters 1-22
    into exam readiness.

       easypj/jobpost project. You'll install, deploy, workflow,
       canary, and event-trigger easypj in simulated exam scenarios.

Chapter 23: CAPA Exam Overview and Strategy
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
    23.1  CAPA Exam Format
          - 90 minutes, ~60 questions
          - Multiple choice ONLY — no live terminal, no hands-on CLI tasks
          - Pass score: ~75% (Linux Foundation does not publish an official cut)
          - Domains: Argo Workflows (36%), Argo CD (34%), Rollouts (18%), Events (12%)
          - CLOSED BOOK — no notes, no docs, no external references allowed
          [EXAM STATS CARDS: All key numbers]
          ┌──────────────┐ ┌──────────────┐ ┌──────────────┐ ┌──────────────┐
          │ ⏱️ DURATION    │ │ ❓ QUESTIONS   │ │ ✅ PASS SCORE  │ │ 📖 BOOK       │
          │              │ │              │ │              │ │              │
          │  90 minutes  │ │  ~60 (MCQ    │ │  ~75%        │ │  CLOSED —     │
          │              │ │  ONLY)       │ │  (unpublished)│ │  no notes/docs│
          └──────────────┘ └──────────────┘ └──────────────┘ └──────────────┘
          ┌──────────────┐ ┌──────────────┐ ┌──────────────┐ ┌──────────────┐
          │ 🔃 WORKFLOWS   │ │ 📦 ARGO CD     │ │ 🚀 ROLLOUTS    │ │ 🔔 EVENTS      │
          │              │ │              │ │              │ │              │
          │  36% (highest)│ │  34%         │ │  18%         │ │  12%          │
          └──────────────┘ └──────────────┘ └──────────────┘ └──────────────┘
          ⚠️ GOTCHA — this is a genuine, common misconception: CAPA is NOT
             hands-on-CLI and NOT open-book (unlike some other Linux
             Foundation certifications, e.g. CKA/CKAD/CKS, which ARE
             live-terminal, open-docs exams). Every lab and drill in this
             guide (Ch25's simulation included) exists to build the muscle
             memory that lets you answer a MULTIPLE-CHOICE question about
             that same command/YAML correctly and fast — not because the
             real exam hands you a terminal.

    23.2  Exam-Day Strategy
          - First 5 minutes: aliases, bookmarks, context check
          - Time management: 90 seconds per question
          - Flag difficult questions, return later
          - Verification checklist for every answer
          [STRATEGY GUIDE: Step-by-step exam approach]
          ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
          │  EXAM-DAY FLOW — 90 minutes, ~60 questions                  │
          │                                                            │
          │  0:00  Read the FIRST question. Don't overthink it — it's   │
          │        just as likely to be Events(12%) as Workflows(36%).  │
          │           │                                                 │
          │           ▼                                                 │
          │  Per question (~90s budget): read stem → identify domain    │
          │  from CRD/keyword → answer if certain → FLAG if unsure,      │
          │  move on. Never burn 5 minutes on question #3.               │
          │           │                                                 │
          │           ▼                                                 │
          │  0:75  All 60 answered or flagged. Return to FLAGGED only.  │
          │           │                                                 │
          │           ▼                                                 │
          │  0:88  Final 2 min: confirm every question has an answer    │
          │        selected (a blank scores the same as wrong).          │
          │           │                                                 │
          │           ▼                                                 │
          │  0:90  Submit.                                               │
          └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    23.3  Top 10 CAPA Exam Mistakes to Avoid
          [MISTAKES TABLE: 10 common errors + solutions]
          ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
          │  # │ MISTAKE                        │ FIX                  │

Chapter 24: CAPA Practice Questions — All Domains (Official Weights)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
    [B] WHY THIS MATTERS
    ────────────────────
    Every practice question so far has lived at the END of ONE chapter, about
    ONE tool, right after you just finished reading about that exact tool —
    the easiest possible test condition. The real CAPA exam does the
    opposite: 60 questions, all 4 domains shuffled together, with nothing
    telling you which is which. A question about a stuck sync wave can sit
    right next to one about a DAG retryStrategy, which sits next to one
    about an AnalysisRun's failure condition — you must first recognize
    WHICH domain a question belongs to before you can even start answering
    it, in roughly 90 seconds. That skill — fast domain identification
    under shuffle, then a correct answer under a clock — is what this
    chapter drills. Every question below is grounded in the same
    easypj/jobpost objects you've been operating since Chapter 2.

    ┌────────────────────────────────┬────────────────────────────────┐
    │ BEFORE CHAPTER 24                │ AFTER CHAPTER 24                 │
    ├────────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────┤
    │ Strong on each tool in           │ Strong on each tool WHEN         │
    │ isolation, right after its       │ SHUFFLED with the other 3 —      │
    │ own chapter                      │ the actual exam condition        │
    │ Reads a question slowly, unsure  │ Recognizes the domain from       │
    │ which domain it belongs to       │ trigger keywords in <10 seconds  │
    │ No sense of which domain is      │ A per-domain self-score showing  │
    │ your weak spot                   │ exactly where Ch25's labs should │
    │                                   │ focus first                      │
    └────────────────────────────────┴────────────────────────────────┘

    [C] LEARNING OBJECTIVES — 🎯 By the end of this chapter you can:
    ────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
      1. Identify a question's domain (CD / Workflows / Rollouts / Events)
         from its CRD names and trigger keywords in under 10 seconds.
      2. Answer scenario-style questions across all 4 domains inside a
         realistic ~90-seconds-per-question budget.
      3. Tell apart near-identical-sounding traps that live in DIFFERENT
         domains (e.g. Argo CD "sync wave" ordering vs Workflows "DAG"
         dependency ordering vs Rollouts "canary steps" ordering).
      4. Self-score each domain section separately and use the result to
         target Chapter 25's labs at your weakest domain first.

    [D] CAPA RELEVANCE
    ───────────────────
    This is the one chapter in the whole guide organized by the exact same
    boundary as the official exam blueprint, not by teaching sequence:
      24.1 Argo Workflows → 36% of the exam (the single highest-weighted
           domain — more than a third of every exam attempt)
      24.2 Argo CD        → 34% of the exam
      24.3 Argo Rollouts  → 18% of the exam
      24.4 Argo Events    → 12% of the exam
    These four percentages are the OFFICIAL CAPA blueprint weights, used
    consistently for every chapter's 🎯 CAPA domain tag throughout this
    guide — memorize this order; it is also roughly the order in which you
    should triage any remaining study time. Together, 24.1-24.4 deliver 35
    scenario-based practice questions (10 + 10 + 8 + 7). That split is NOT
    a literal 1:1 mirror of the exam's percentage weights — it is
    deliberately concentrated on the two highest-value domains (Workflows,
    Argo CD together carry 70% of the real exam), so drill those two
    sections hardest if you can only re-run part of this chapter before
    exam day.
    24.1  Argo Workflows Practice Questions (10 Questions — 36%)
          - DAG construction, Step sequencing, artifact passing
          - WorkflowTemplate vs Workflow, CronWorkflow scheduling
          - Parameters, retry strategies, resource templates
          [PRACTICE QUESTIONS: Scenario-based with answers]
          ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
          │  🔃 ARGO WORKFLOWS — 10 QUESTIONS (36% — highest weighted)   │
          └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
          Q1. easypj's CI DAG has `build-image` with
              `depends: "unit-tests && lint"`. unit-tests Succeeds,
              lint is Skipped (a when: condition). Does build-image run?
          A1. No.
          Explanation: bare `.Succeeded` in a depends expression does
          NOT match Skipped — you'd need
          `depends: "unit-tests && (lint.Succeeded || lint.Skipped)"`.

          Q2. Which template type auto-captures stdout into
              {{steps.X.outputs.result}} — Container or Script?
          A2. Script only.
          Explanation: Container templates need explicit
          outputs.parameters[].valueFrom.path wiring; Script auto-
          populates result.

          Q3. A WorkflowTemplate `easypj-ci-cd` is edited while a
              Workflow submitted from it is still running. Does the
              running Workflow pick up the change?
          A3. No — it executes against status.storedWorkflowTemplateSpec,
              a snapshot frozen at submit time.
          Explanation: templateRef/workflowTemplateRef resolve ONCE,
          not on every reconcile.

          Q4. Write the CronWorkflow field (and its type) that schedules
              easypj's nightly backup at 2 AM, current syntax.
          A4. spec.schedules: ["0 2 * * *"] — a LIST, not a string.
          Explanation: spec.schedules (v3.6+) replaced the older
          singular spec.schedule.

          Q5. Two CronWorkflow triggers would overlap for a job that
              must NEVER run twice simultaneously (e.g. pg_dump). Which
              concurrencyPolicy?
          A5. Forbid.
          Explanation: Allow lets both run (data risk); Replace kills
          the in-progress one (loses that backup); Forbid simply skips
          the new trigger.

          Q6. `argo retry <wf>` vs `argo resubmit <wf>` — which one
              re-resolves a just-edited shared WorkflowTemplate?
          A6. argo resubmit — it creates a NEW Workflow object and
              re-resolves templateRef fresh.
          Explanation: argo retry reuses the SAME object's frozen
          stored spec, re-running only failed nodes.

          Q7. A DAG fans out 50 test-matrix tasks via withParam with no
              other config. What real risk does this create?
          A7. No concurrency cap — all 50 can schedule pods at once,
              potentially flooding the cluster.
          Explanation: set `parallelism:` on the DAG/Workflow to throttle
          simultaneous task execution.

          Q8. Which Resource template action requires an additional
              mergeStrategy field, and what are its 3 valid values?
          A8. action: patch; mergeStrategy: strategic | merge | json.
          Explanation: strategic merge doesn't work on Custom Resources
          — use merge or json for CRDs.

          Q9. easypj's Workflow needs a Postgres backup step's artifact
              consumed by the very next DAG task. Which two fields link
              them?
          A9. outputs.artifacts (producer) and inputs.artifacts
              (consumer), matched by artifact NAME.
          Explanation: the controller moves the file through the
          configured artifact repository between the two pods.

          Q10. A Workflow has ttlStrategy.secondsAfterFailure: 604800
               but no podGC set. 40 completed pods from an old DAG run
               are still visible a week later. Why?
          A10. ttlStrategy controls when the WHOLE Workflow OBJECT is
               deleted (cascading to its pods); podGC independently
               controls cleanup of pods from EARLIER, already-finished
               steps while the Workflow is still running. Neither
               substitutes for the other.
          Explanation: reinforces Ch 11.6's ttlStrategy-vs-podGC
          distinction.

    24.2  Argo CD Practice Questions (10 Questions — 34%)
          - Application CRD, sync policies, AppProjects
          - Multi-cluster, ApplicationSets, health checks
          - Helm + Kustomize configuration, reconciliation patterns
          - CLI operations and debugging
          [PRACTICE QUESTIONS: Scenario-based with answers]
          ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
          │  📦 ARGO CD — 10 QUESTIONS (34%)                             │
          └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
          Q1. An Application has no `spec.project` field set. Which
              AppProject does it silently join?
          A1. "default" — auto-created, wildcard sourceRepos/
              destinations/resources, cannot be deleted.
          Explanation: omitting spec.project is NOT "no project" — it's
          the least-restrictive project possible.

          Q2. `argocd app sync easypj-prod` completes; the Application
              shows Synced but Health=Degraded. What does this mean?
          A2. The live manifests match Git exactly (sync succeeded), but
              the resulting workload isn't actually healthy (e.g. a
              CrashLoopBackOff Pod).
          Explanation: Sync Status and Health Status are two
          INDEPENDENT axes — Synced+Degraded is a real, non-
          contradictory state.

          Q3. Which sync option is required before you can safely
              `kubectl apply` a large CRD without hitting the 262144-
              byte annotation limit?
          A3. ServerSideApply=true (or install the CRD with
              kubectl apply --server-side originally).
          Explanation: client-side apply's last-applied-configuration
          annotation is what blows the size cap; server-side apply
          uses managedFields instead.

          Q4. easypj-prod's AppProject restricts destinations.namespace
              to easypj-prod only. Does this alone stop a rogue
              Application from deploying a ClusterRoleBinding?
          A4. No — destination restriction and resource-kind
              restriction are separate controls; clusterResourceWhitelist
              (or Blacklist) must also be set.
          Explanation: namespace scoping says WHERE; resource whitelisting
          says WHAT KIND — both are needed for real lockdown.

          Q5. Which ApplicationSet generator combines exactly TWO
              generators as a Cartesian product (e.g. env × cluster)?
          A5. Matrix.
          Explanation: Merge combines 2+ generators by matching keys
          (overlay pattern), not a Cartesian product — the two are not
          interchangeable.

          Q6. A Helm-sourced Application needs its replicaCount bumped
              via the CLI. Which flag — `-p` or `--repo`?
          A6. -p (e.g. `argocd app set easypj-prod -p replicaCount=3`).
          Explanation: -p/--parameter is a Helm-value override ONLY;
          --repo sets the source repo URL — different fields entirely.

          Q7. `kubectl logs -n argocd deploy/argocd-application-
              controller` returns "not found." Why, and the fix?
          A7. It's a StatefulSet, not a Deployment, since Argo CD 1.8
              (sharding). Fix: `kubectl logs -n argocd
              statefulset/argocd-application-controller`.
          Explanation: a classic, real, still-common operator mistake.

          Q8. Give the exact hook-delete-policy value that deletes a
              PreSync migration Job ONLY if it failed (leaving successes
              for inspection).
          A8. hook-delete-policy: HookFailed.
          Explanation: HookSucceeded is the opposite (delete on success,
          leave failures); BeforeHookCreation is the default (delete the
          OLD hook right before the next one is created).

          Q9. Which field enables staged, cross-Application promotion
              (dev → staging → prod), and what native Argo CD mechanism
              is commonly mistaken for it?
          A9. spec.strategy.type: RollingSync (ApplicationSet); commonly
              confused with sync-waves, which only order resources
              WITHIN one Application's sync.
          Explanation: two different scopes — one Application's internal
          ordering vs. many Applications' promotion ordering.

          Q10. An AppProject role `prod-readonly` has policy
               `p, proj:easypj-prod:prod-readonly, applications, get,
               easypj-prod/*, allow`. Can a user bound to this role run
               `argocd app sync easypj-prod`?
          A10. No — only `get` is allowed; `sync` requires broader
               action permission (e.g. `*` or explicit sync/override
               actions) that this policy doesn't grant.
          Explanation: RBAC policy lines are additive and specific —
          `get` never implicitly grants `sync`.

    24.3  Argo Rollouts Practice Questions (8 Questions — 18%)
          - Blue-Green vs Canary strategies
          - Rollout CRD, AnalysisTemplate, AnalysisRun
          - Traffic routing, promotion and rollback
          [PRACTICE QUESTIONS: Scenario-based with answers]
          ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
          │  🚀 ARGO ROLLOUTS — 8 QUESTIONS (18%)                        │
          └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
          Q1. A Blue-Green Rollout sets activeService/previewService but
              no autoPromotionEnabled field. What happens the instant
              Green's pods report Ready?
          A1. It promotes automatically — autoPromotionEnabled defaults
              to true.
          Explanation: a real gate requires explicitly setting it false.

          Q2. Which Service fields does a CANARY strategy use for
              traffic — activeService/previewService or
              canaryService/stableService?
          A2. canaryService/stableService.
          Explanation: activeService/previewService are Blue-Green-only
          fields — a frequent field-name mix-up on the exam.

          Q3. A canary's steps end at `setWeight: 50` with no further
              steps. The Rollout shows Healthy, but production traffic
              stays visibly ~50/50 between versions. Why?
          A3. Missing a final `setWeight: 100` step — traffic-routing
              providers only guarantee "best effort" at the LAST
              explicit weight.
          Explanation: "Healthy" reflects the Rollout controller's step
          list completing, not that traffic actually reached 100%.

          Q4. Write the correct Prometheus successCondition for "error
              rate under 1%" given a query returning an aggregated
              single series.
          A4. successCondition: result[0] < 0.01
          Explanation: Prometheus queries always return a vector, even
          fully aggregated — index [0], don't use bare `result`.

          Q5. An AnalysisRun shows Error 3 times in a row, then
              Successful on try 4. Did the analysis fail?
          A5. No — consecutiveErrorLimit (default 4) wasn't reached, and
              it RESETS on any success.
          Explanation: Error (couldn't measure) is tolerated differently
          than Failure (measured, value was bad).
          Q6. Without trafficRouting configured, a canary at
              `setWeight: 20` with 5 replicas — how many pods actually
              run the canary version, and is traffic exactly 20%?
          A6. ceil(5 × 0.20) = 1 pod; traffic is NOT exactly 20% — it's
              a best-effort POD-COUNT ratio (~1-in-6 ≈ 17%), not a
              guaranteed percentage.
          Explanation: exact traffic percentages require a real
          traffic-routing provider (Istio/NGINX/ALB/Gateway API).

          Q7. Why is SMI flagged as a legacy choice for a NEW 2026
              traffic-routing integration, even though Argo Rollouts
              still technically supports it?
          A7. CNCF has archived the SMI Spec, and (for Linkerd users
              specifically) Linkerd's own linkerd-smi extension is also
              archived — the Gateway API plugin is the current path.
          Explanation: "still supported" ≠ "current recommendation."

          Q8. `kubectl argo rollouts abort easypj-rollout` reverts
              active traffic to the previous ReplicaSet. Does it also
              revert spec.template back to the old image?
          A8. No — abort only reverts TRAFFIC; spec.template still
              points at the new version, so exiting the aborted state
              re-attempts it unless the spec is also reverted.
          Explanation: abort is a traffic-level emergency brake, not a
          full undo — `undo` (or a manual spec revert) is needed too.

    24.4  Argo Events Practice Questions (7 Questions — 12%)
          - EventSource types, Sensor configuration
          - Trigger parameterization, EventBus
          - Event components and architecture
          [PRACTICE QUESTIONS: Scenario-based with answers]
          ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
          │  🔔 ARGO EVENTS — 7 QUESTIONS (12%)                          │
          └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
          Q1. Which EventSource spec field configures S3-style bucket
              notifications — `s3` or `minio`?
          A1. minio — there is no `s3:` field; it's S3-protocol-
              compatible, configured under `spec.minio`.
          Explanation: a real AWS S3 bucket needs the AWS-SNS
          EventSource instead — minio is MinIO-exclusive.

          Q2. A Sensor's DataFilter has `comparator: "=="`. What happens
              on `kubectl apply`?
          A2. It's invalid — the real Comparator enum is
              >= > = != < <= — equality is a single `=`, never `==`.
          Explanation: a near-guaranteed novice typo, high exam value.

          Q3. Name the 5 filter categories available on an
              EventDependency (not just "data and context").
          A3. data, context, exprs, script, time.
          Explanation: each solves a different matching problem — e.g.
          time gates by time-of-day, script allows arbitrary Lua logic.

          Q4. Which trigger type is Workflow-lifecycle-aware (submit,
              resubmit, retry, resume, suspend, terminate, stop) —
              `k8s` or `argoWorkflow`?
          A4. argoWorkflow.
          Explanation: `k8s` is generic create/patch/update/delete on
          any resource; only argoWorkflow exposes Workflow-specific
          operations like resume.

          Q5. A Sensor's event arrives and its filter matches, but no
              Workflow is created — the Sensor log shows a "Forbidden"
              error. What's missing?
          A5. RBAC on the Sensor's ServiceAccount (spec.template.
              serviceAccountName) — needs create+list on
              workflows.argoproj.io to submit.
          Explanation: RBAC failures happen AFTER a successful filter
          match, which is why they look like "nothing happened" rather
          than a filter rejection.

          Q6. A Resource EventSource's ServiceAccount has only `watch`
              (not `list`) on a custom CR. What breaks?
          A6. The informer's initial LIST (full snapshot) fails before
              its WATCH stream can even start — the EventSource never
              fires, often with no obvious error.
          Explanation: `list` backs the required initial sync; `watch`
          alone isn't sufficient.

          Q7. A Calendar EventSource sets both `schedule` and `interval`
              on the same entry. Which one actually fires?
          A7. schedule — precedence is schedule > interval > recurrence,
              silently, with no validation warning about the ignored
              field.
          Explanation: a real, easy-to-miss precedence rule worth
          memorizing verbatim.


Chapter 25: Hands-On Labs — CAPA Exam Simulation
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
    [B] WHY THIS MATTERS
    ────────────────────
    Chapters 1-24 taught, then quizzed. This chapter is where you PROVE it —
    twice. First tool-by-tool (25.1-25.4): the same four labs from Chapter
    23.5's Week 1/2/3 study plan, run again now as a cumulative dress
    rehearsal, using only this guide's Appendices as reference instead of
    the full concept chapters. Then all at once, against a clock, with
    nothing open but this guide and the official docs (25.5) — as close to
    the real CAPA exam's pressure as a guide can simulate. This is the last
    NEW material in the book; everything past this chapter is reference
    (Appendices A-J). If you can complete 25.5 at or above CAPA's real pass
    bar, you are ready to book the exam.

    ┌────────────────────────────────┬────────────────────────────────┐
    │ BEFORE CHAPTER 25                │ AFTER CHAPTER 25                 │
    ├────────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────┤
    │ Know each tool from its own      │ Can stand up all 4 tools on a    │
    │ chapter's labs, done in isolation│ fresh cluster from memory, wired │
    │                                   │ together, in one sitting         │
    │ Never timed yourself across       │ Have a real 60-minute, 10-task   │
    │ domains                          │ score telling you exam-readiness │
    │ "I think I'm ready"              │ A rubric-backed answer to        │
    │                                   │ "am I ready", not a guess        │
    └────────────────────────────────┴────────────────────────────────┘

    [C] LEARNING OBJECTIVES — 🎯 By the end of this chapter you can:
    ────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
      1. Independently install and wire all FOUR Argo tools onto one fresh
         cluster, using only this guide's Appendices (not the concept
         chapters) as reference.
      2. Execute a full easypj GitOps flow end-to-end: Git push → Workflow
         CI → GitOps repo update → Argo CD sync → Rollouts canary →
         Events auto-trigger — with no manual step you can't explain.
      3. Complete a 60-minute, 10-task simulation at or above CAPA's real
         pass bar (~75%) under genuine time pressure.
      4. Diagnose and self-correct the single most common failure at each
         stage (the Ch1-22 "Under the Hood" / "Exam Gotchas" traps) without
         re-reading the chapter that taught it.

    [D] CAPA RELEVANCE
    ───────────────────
    This chapter is 100% CAPA-relevant by construction — 25.1-25.4 rehearse
    the exact competencies graded at their official weights (Workflows 36%,
    Argo CD 34%, Rollouts 18%, Events 12%), and 25.5 simulates the exam's
    real format directly.
    ⚠️ IMPORTANT CALIBRATION: the real CAPA exam itself is a 90-minute,
    60-question, remotely-proctored MULTIPLE-CHOICE exam — it does NOT hand
    you a live terminal. These labs exist because the fastest way to
    correctly answer a scenario-based multiple-choice question ("what
    happens if you `kubectl apply` this Rollout with no activeService set?")
    is to have actually broken and fixed that exact thing with your hands
    at least once. You are training muscle memory that the exam then
    tests as a multiple-choice recall, not because the proctor watches
    your terminal.
